<meta>This page provides a curriculum of preparatory reading recommended by Isaac Newton for students intending to study his masterwork, the *Philosophiae Naturalis Principia Mathematica*.</meta>

# Books recommended by Newton himself to be read before the *Principia*:

<margin>Geometry</margin> Euclid’s *Elements*.
Elements of Conic Sections <note>The study of shapes like ellipses, parabolas, and hyperbolas formed by intersecting a cone with a plane.</note>.
Elements of Curves, either by Jan de Witt, or Philippe de la Hire’s Latin treatise on Conic Sections, or Dr. Isaac Barrow’s *Epitome of Apollonius*.

<margin>Algebra</margin> Erasmus Bartholinus’s *Introduction* — then peruse such passages as are scattered throughout René Descartes’s *Geometry* and other algebraic writings of Frans van Schooten — including all the commentaries and solutions of the problems.

<margin>Astronomy</margin> A short account of the Copernican System <note>The heliocentric model where the Earth revolves around the Sun.</note>, and of Pierre Gassendi’s *Astronomy*.
As much of Nicholas Mercator’s work as relates to the same system and newer discoveries made by telescopes.
These are sufficient for understanding Newton’s book; however, Christiaan Huygens’s *The Pendulum Clock* <note>original: "Horologium Oscillatorium"</note> will make one much better prepared.

On the first reading — understand the propositions along with some of the <gloss>Definitions</gloss> and demonstrations which are easier than the rest.
Proceed from the easier to the harder — after the first 60 pages, pass on to the Third Book. When the design of that book is understood, then turn back to other propositions at your leisure.
See: Newton’s Correspondence in Cotes <note>Refers to the correspondence between Isaac Newton and Roger Cotes, who edited the second edition of the *Principia*.</note>.

<summary>This page outlines Isaac Newton's personal recommendations for the mathematical and astronomical texts one should master before attempting to read his *Principia*. It suggests a specific pedagogical path through geometry, algebra, and planetary theory, including a strategic tip to skip to the third book after the first sixty pages.</summary>

# DEDICATION

...commands and receives the universal acknowledgments of all who profess or value its several branches.

They justly admire that, amidst a close attendance to the cares of your profession—in which you now fill the most honorable seat—you are indefatigably promoting the improvement of natural knowledge <note>original: "natural knowledge"; this refers to the study of the physical world, what we now call science.</note>. You do this by carrying out some laudable <note>praiseworthy</note> designs of your own, by assisting and encouraging others, and by adding new stores to that immense treasure already brought into your extensive collection of whatever is rare and valuable in nature or art <note>original: "art"; here meaning human skill, craftsmanship, or technology rather than just fine art.</note>.

# THE
## Author's Preface.

SINCE *the ancients (as we are told by* Pappus) *highly valued the science of* Mechanics *in the investigation of natural things; and the moderns, laying aside <term>substantial forms</term> and <term>occult qualities</term>, have endeavored to subject the phenomena of nature to the laws of* mathematics; *I have in this treatise developed* Mathematics, *as far as it relates to* Philosophy. *The ancients considered* Mechanics *in two ways: as rational, which proceeds accurately by demonstration, and* practical. *To* practical Mechanics *all the manual arts belong, from which* Mechanics *took its name. But as craftsmen do not work with perfect accuracy, it happens that* Mechanics *is so distinguished from* Geometry, *that what is perfectly*

<summary>Isaac Newton introduces his preface by explaining his goal to apply mathematical laws to the phenomena of nature, moving away from older philosophical concepts. He distinguishes between "rational mechanics," which relies on precise proofs, and "practical mechanics," which relates to the manual arts.</summary>
<keywords>Isaac Newton, Mechanics, Mathematics, Natural Philosophy, Pappus, Geometry, Rational Mechanics, Practical Mechanics, substantial forms, occult qualities</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the Author's Preface, specifically elaborating on the relationship between Geometry and Mechanics by defining Geometry as a subset of Mechanics focused on precise measurement.</meta>

accurate is called *Geometrical*, what is less so is called *Mechanical*. But the errors are not in the art, but in the craftsmen. He who works with less accuracy is an imperfect *Mechanic*, and if any could work with perfect accuracy, he would be the most perfect *Mechanic* of all. For the drawing of straight lines and circles, upon which *Geometry* is founded, belongs to *Mechanics*. *Geometry* does not teach us to draw these lines, but requires them to be drawn. For it requires that the learner should first be taught to draw these accurately before he enters upon *Geometry*; then it shows how, by these operations, problems may be solved. To draw straight lines and circles are problems, but not geometrical problems. The solution of these problems is required from *Mechanics*; and by *Geometry*, the use of them, when so solved, is shown. And it is the glory of *Geometry* that from those few principles, brought in from elsewhere, it is able to produce so many things. Therefore, *Geometry* is founded in mechanical practice and is nothing but that part of universal *Mechanics* which accurately proposes and demonstrates the art of measuring. But since the manual arts are

<meta>catchword: chiefly</meta>

<summary>Newton argues that Geometry is a branch of universal Mechanics that focuses on precise measurement, asserting that the act of drawing lines and circles is a mechanical rather than a geometrical problem. He maintains that any inaccuracy in geometry arises from the practitioner (the mechanic) rather than the principles of the art itself.</summary>
<keywords>Geometry, Mechanics, accuracy, craftsmen, straight lines, circles, measurement, universal mechanics, mathematical principles</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the Author's Preface, transitioning from the definition of Geometry as a branch of Mechanics to the definition of Rational Mechanics and the specific focus of this work on natural forces rather than manual arts.</meta>

# The Author's P R E F A C E.

<sig>A 2</sig>

*chiefly concerned with the moving of bodies, it happens that* Geometry *is commonly referred to their magnitudes, and* Mechanics *to their motion. In this sense,* Rational Mechanics *will be the science of motions resulting from any forces whatsoever and of the forces required to produce any motions, accurately proposed and demonstrated. This part of* Mechanics *was cultivated by the ancients in the* Five Powers *which relate to manual arts, who considered gravity (it not being a manual power) in no other way than as it moved weights by those powers. Since our design concerns not manual arts but philosophy, and our subject is not manual but natural powers, we consider chiefly those things which relate to gravity, levity, elastic force, the resistance of fluids, and similar forces, whether attractive or impulsive. And therefore we offer this work as mathematical principles of philosophy. For all the difficulty of philosophy seems to consist in this: from the phenomena of motions to investigate the forces of* Nature, *and then from these forces to demonstrate the other phenomena. And to this end, the general propositions in the first and second book are directed. In the third book we give an example of this in the explanation of*

<vocab>Geometry, Mechanics, Rational Mechanics, Five Powers, gravity, levity, elastic force, resistance of fluids, mathematical principles of philosophy, Nature</vocab>

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>Newton defines Rational Mechanics as the precise science of motions and forces, distinguishing his work from manual arts by focusing on natural forces like gravity and elasticity. He explains that the goal of his mathematical principles is to derive the forces of nature from observed motions and then use those forces to explain other natural phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>Rational Mechanics, natural forces, gravity, mathematical principles of philosophy, phenomena of motion, Five Powers, Nature</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the Author's Preface, detailing how mathematical propositions are used to derive the forces of gravity from celestial phenomena and expressing the hope that all natural phenomena can be explained through similar mechanical principles.</meta>

# The Author's P R E F A C E.

<margin>
<unclear>to infinite</unclear>
<unclear>q. 231.</unclear>
<unclear>nature of Light</unclear>
</margin>

*the System of the World. For by the propositions mathematically demonstrated in the first books, we there derive from the celestial phenomena the forces of gravity with which bodies tend to the Sun and the several planets. Then from these forces, by other propositions which are also mathematical, we deduce the motions of the planets, the comets, the moon, and the sea. I wish we could derive the rest of the phenomena of Nature by the same kind of reasoning from mechanical principles. For I am induced by many reasons to suspect that they may all depend upon certain forces by which the particles of bodies, by some causes hitherto unknown, are either mutually impelled towards each other and cohere in regular shapes, or are repelled and recede from each other; since these forces are unknown, philosophers have hitherto searched Nature in vain. But I hope the principles here laid down will afford some light either to that, or some truer, method of philosophy.*

*In the publication of this work, the most acute and universally learned Mr.* Edmund Halley *not only assisted me with his efforts in correcting the press and taking care of the diagrams, but it was to*

<summary>Newton explains how he derives the laws of gravity and planetary motion from mathematical principles, suggesting that all natural phenomena might result from attractive or repulsive forces between particles. He also acknowledges Edmund Halley's significant contributions to the book's publication and technical preparation.</summary>

### THE
# PREFACE
### O F
## *Mr. Roger Cotes*,
### To the Second Edition of this Work, so far as it relates to the Inventions and Discoveries herein contained.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'T' decorated with foliage.</image-desc>

THOSE who have treated natural philosophy may be nearly categorized into three classes. Of these, some have attributed specific and occult qualities to the various species of things, upon which they claim the operations of different bodies depend in some unknown way. This is the essence of the doctrine of the Schools derived from *Aristotle* and the Peripatetics. They assert that the various effects of bodies arise

<meta>catchword: arise</meta>

<summary>Roger Cotes begins his preface by categorizing natural philosophers into three groups, starting with the Scholastic followers of Aristotle who rely on hidden qualities to explain physical phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Cotes, natural philosophy, Aristotle, Peripatetics, occult qualities, Schools, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's critique of Scholastic philosophy (the first class) and transitions into a critique of speculative mechanical philosophy (the second class) before introducing experimental philosophy (the third class).</meta>

<header>Mr. Cotes’s Preface.</header>

arise from the particular natures of those bodies. But from where it is that bodies derive those natures they do not tell us; and therefore they tell us nothing. And being entirely employed in giving names to things, and not in searching into things themselves, we may say that they have invented a philosophical way of speaking, but not that they have made known to us true philosophy.

<margin><unclear>Treat / Heat / Est.m</unclear></margin>
Others, therefore, by laying aside that useless heap of words, thought to employ their efforts to better purpose. These supposed all matter to be homogeneous, and that the variety of forms which is seen in bodies arises from some very plain and simple attributes of the component particles. And by proceeding from simple things to those which are more complex, they certainly proceed correctly, if they attribute no other properties to those primary attributes of the particles than Nature has done. But when they take the liberty of imagining at will unknown figures and magnitudes, and uncertain arrangements and motions of the parts; and moreover of
<margin>+</margin>supposing hidden fluids, freely pervading the pores of bodies, endowed with an all-performing subtlety, and agitated with hidden motions; they now wander into dreams and chimeras, and neglect the true constitution of things; which certainly is not to be expected from fallacious conjectures, when we can scarcely reach it by the most certain observations. Those who derive the foundation on which they build their speculations from hypotheses may indeed form an ingenious romance, but it will still be a romance.

There is left then the third class, who profess experimental philosophy. These indeed derive the causes of all things from the most simple principles possible;

<meta>catchword: possible;</meta>

<summary>Roger Cotes concludes his critique of Scholastic philosophers, who focus only on naming things, and critiques a second group who rely on imaginative hypotheses and hidden fluids. He introduces the third class of philosophers who follow the experimental method.</summary>

<keywords>Roger Cotes, Scholasticism, mechanical philosophy, hypotheses, experimental philosophy, matter, particles, hidden fluids, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the third class of philosophers—those who practice experimental philosophy—contrasting their rigorous method with the speculative approach of the second class discussed on the previous page.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

<margin>255</margin>
possible; but then they assume nothing as a principle that is not proved by phenomena. They frame no hypotheses, nor do they receive them into philosophy otherwise than as questions whose truth may be disputed. They proceed, therefore, in a twofold method: synthetic and analytical. From some select phenomena they deduce by analysis the forces of nature and the more simple laws of forces; and from there, by synthesis, they show the constitution of the rest. This is that incomparably best way of philosophizing which our renowned author most justly embraced above the rest, and thought alone worthy to be cultivated and adorned by his excellent labors. Of this he has given us a most illustrious example by the explanation of the System of the World, most successfully deduced from the Theory of Gravity. That the *virtue* of gravity was found in all bodies, others suspected or imagined before him; but he was the only and the first philosopher who could demonstrate it from appearances and make it a solid foundation for the most noble speculations.

<margin>A. B.</margin>
I know, indeed, that some persons, and those of great renown, being too much prepossessed with certain prejudices, are unwilling to assent to this new principle, and are ready to prefer uncertain notions to certain ones. It is not my intention to detract from the reputation of these eminent men; I shall only lay before the reader such considerations as will enable him to pass an equitable judgment in this dispute.

Therefore, so that we may begin our reasoning from what is most simple and nearest to us, let us consider for a moment what the nature of gravity is here with us on Earth, that we may proceed the more safely;

<summary>Roger Cotes describes the "experimental philosophy" used by Isaac Newton, which relies on analysis and synthesis rather than speculative hypotheses. He notes that while others suspected gravity existed, Newton was the first to demonstrate it as a fundamental principle of the world system.</summary>
<keywords>experimental philosophy, Newton, phenomena, analysis, synthesis, gravity, scientific method, System of the World</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of gravity, focusing on its properties as a mutual force and its relationship to mass, moving from its nature on Earth to its universal application.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE,

safely when we come to consider it in the heavenly bodies, that lie at so vast a distance from us. It is now agreed by all philosophers that all circumterrestrial bodies gravitate towards the Earth. That no bodies really light are to be found, is now confirmed by manifold experience. That which is relative levity, is not true levity, but apparent only; and arises from the preponderating gravity of the contiguous bodies.

Moreover, as all bodies gravitate towards the Earth, so does the Earth again towards bodies. That the action of gravity is mutual, and equal on both sides, is thus proved. Let the mass of the Earth be distinguished into any two parts whatever, either equal, or in any way unequal; <margin>N B</margin> now if the weights of the parts towards each other were not mutually equal, the lesser weight would give way to the greater, and the two parts joined together would move on ad infinitum in a straight line towards that part to which the greater weight tends; altogether against experience. Therefore we must say that the <margin>!</margin> weights of the parts are established in equilibrium; that is, that the action of gravity is mutual and equal on both sides.

The weights of bodies, at equal distances from the centre of the Earth, are proportional to the quantities of matter in the bodies. This is gathered from the equal acceleration of all bodies that fall from a state of rest by the force of their weights; for the forces by which unequal bodies are equally accelerated must be proportional to the quantities of the matter to be moved. Now that all bodies are equally accelerated in falling appears from this, that when the resistance of the air is taken away, as it is under an exhausted receiver <note>a vacuum chamber</note>, bodies falling de-

<meta>catchword: scribe</meta>

<summary>Roger Cotes explains that gravity is a mutual and equal force between the Earth and all objects, and that an object's weight is directly proportional to its mass. He supports these claims by noting that all bodies fall at the same rate of acceleration when air resistance is removed.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that gravity is proportional to mass, establishing that the total attractive force of a body is the sum of the forces of its parts, and begins to apply these terrestrial laws to celestial motion.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

describe equal spaces in equal times; and this is proved even more accurately by the experiments of pendulums.

The attractive forces of bodies at equal distances are proportional to the quantities of matter in the bodies. For since bodies gravitate toward the Earth, and the Earth in turn toward those bodies with equal momentum, the weight of the Earth toward every body—or the force with which the body attracts the Earth—will be equal to the weight of that same body toward the Earth. But this weight was shown to be proportional to the quantity of matter in the body; and therefore the force with which every body attracts the Earth, or the absolute force of the body, will be proportional to that same quantity of matter.

Therefore, the attractive force of entire bodies arises from and is composed of the attractive forces of their parts; because, as was just shown, if the bulk of the matter is increased or decreased, its power is proportionally increased or decreased. We must therefore conclude that the action of the Earth is composed of the united actions of its parts; and therefore that all terrestrial bodies must attract each other mutually, with absolute forces that are proportional to the matter attracting. This is the nature of gravity on Earth; let us now see what it is in the heavens.

That every body continues in its state of either rest or uniform motion in a straight line, unless it is compelled to change that state by forces impressed upon it, is a law of nature universally accepted by all philosophers. But from this it follows that bodies which move in curved lines, and are therefore continually moving away from the straight lines that are tangents to their orbits, are by some

<summary>Roger Cotes explains that gravitational force is proportional to the amount of matter in a body and is the sum of the forces of its individual parts. He transition from describing gravity on Earth to celestial mechanics by invoking the law of inertia.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, moving from the properties of gravity on Earth to the forces governing the motion of celestial bodies in their orbits.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

continued force retained in those curvilinear paths. Since the planets move in curvilinear orbits, there must be some force operating, by whose repeated actions they are perpetually made to deflect from the tangents.

Now it is gathered by mathematical reasoning, and clearly demonstrated, that all bodies that move in any curved line described in a plane, and which—by a radius drawn to any point, whether stationary or moving in any way—describe areas about that point proportional to the times, are urged by forces directed toward that point. This must therefore be granted. Since all astronomers agree that the primary planets describe areas proportional to the times about the Sun, and the secondary planets about the primary, it follows that the forces by which they are perpetually turned aside from the straight-line tangents and made to revolve in curvilinear orbits are directed toward the bodies that are situated in the centers of the orbits. This force may therefore not improperly be called centripetal with respect to the <margin>C. B.</margin> revolving body, and attractive with respect to the central body, whatever cause it may be imagined to arise from.

But besides, these things must also be granted, as being mathematically demonstrated: If several bodies revolve with a uniform motion in concentric circles, and the squares of the periodic times are as the cubes of the distances from the common center, the centripetal forces will be inversely as the squares of the distances. Or, if bodies revolve in orbits that are very near to circles, and the <term>apsides</term> <note>the points in an orbit closest to or farthest from the center</note> of the orbits remain stationary, the centripetal forces of the revolving bodies will be inversely as the squares of the distances. That both these cases hold in all the
<meta>catchword: Planets</meta>

He mathematically demonstrates that this force follows an inverse-square law based on the planets' orbital periods and distances.</summary>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

Therefore, the centripetal forces of all the planets are inversely proportional to the squares of the distances from the centers of their orbits. If anyone should object that the <term>apsides</term> of the planets, and especially of the Moon, are not perfectly stationary, but are carried with a slow kind of motion <term>in consequentia</term> <note>in the direction of the signs of the zodiac; forward motion</note>; one may provide this answer: that even if we were to grant that this very slow motion arises because the proportion of the centripetal force is slightly different from the square, we are still able to compute mathematically the extent of that aberration and find it to be perfectly imperceptible. For the ratio of the lunar centripetal force itself, which must be the most irregular of them all, will indeed be slightly greater than the square, but it will be nearly sixty times closer to that than it is to the cube. But we may give a more accurate answer by saying that this progression of the <term>apsides</term> arises not from a deviation from the inverse-square proportion, but from a completely different cause, as is most admirably shown in this philosophy. It is certain, then, that the centripetal forces by which the primary planets tend toward the Sun, and the secondary planets toward their primary ones, are exactly inversely proportional to the squares of the distances.

From what has been said so far, it is plain that the planets are kept in their orbits by some force perpetually acting upon them; it is plain that this force is always directed toward the centers of their orbits; it is plain that its effectiveness increases as it nears the center and diminishes as it moves away; and that it increases in the same proportion as the square of the distance decreases, and decreases in the same

<summary>Roger Cotes explains that planetary orbits are governed by centripetal forces that follow the inverse-square law. He addresses the motion of the Moon's apsides, arguing that such deviations do not disprove the law but are explained by other factors within Newton's philosophy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the inverse-square law of centripetal force, transitioning from the general planetary law to a specific comparison between the Moon's orbital force and the Earth's gravity.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

Let us now see whether, by making a comparison between the centripetal forces of the planets and the force of gravity, we might perhaps find them to be of the same kind. They will be of the same kind if we find the same laws and characteristics on both sides. Let us, then, first consider the centripetal force of the Moon, which is nearest to us.

The straight-line distances that bodies falling from rest travel in a given time at the very beginning of their motion, when the bodies are urged by any forces whatsoever, are proportional to those forces. This is evident from mathematical reasoning. Therefore, the centripetal force of the Moon revolving in its orbit is to the force of gravity at the surface of the Earth as the distance that the Moon would travel toward the Earth in a very small interval of time—if deprived of all its circular motion and descending by its centripetal force—is to the distance that a heavy body would travel when falling by the force of its gravity near the Earth in that same interval of time. The first of these distances is equal to the <term>versed sine</term> <note>a trigonometric function representing the distance between the center of an arc and the center of its chord</note> of the arc traveled by the Moon in the same amount of time, because that versed sine measures the displacement of the Moon from the tangent line produced by the centripetal force; therefore, it can be calculated if the orbital period of the Moon and its distance from the center of the Earth are known. The latter distance is found by experiments with pendulums, as *Mr. Huygens* has shown. Therefore, by performing a calculation, we shall find that the first distance is to the latter—or that the centripetal force of the Moon revolving in its orbit will be to the force of gravity at the <term>superficies</term> <note>surface</note>

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>Roger Cotes explains the mathematical method for comparing the Moon's centripetal force with terrestrial gravity, using the distance of fall and the versed sine of the orbital arc. By comparing these values, he aims to demonstrate that orbital forces and gravity are the same phenomenon.</summary>

<meta>This page completes the mathematical proof that the force holding the Moon in its orbit is identical to the force of gravity on Earth, confirming the inverse square law.</meta>

of the Earth, as the square of the semi-diameter of the Earth to the square of the semi-diameter of the orbit. But by what was shown before, the very same ratio holds between the centripetal force of the Moon revolving in its orbit and the centripetal force of the Moon near the surface of the Earth. Therefore, the centripetal force near the surface of the Earth is equal to the force of gravity. Therefore, these are not two different forces, but one and the same; for if they were different, these forces united would cause bodies to descend to the Earth with twice the velocity they would fall with by the force of gravity alone. Therefore, it is plain that the centripetal force, by which the Moon is perpetually either impelled or attracted out of the tangent and retained in its orbit, is the very force of terrestrial gravity reaching up to the Moon. 

And it is very reasonable to believe that this power should extend itself to vast distances, since upon the tops of the highest mountains we find no sensible diminution of it. Therefore the Moon gravitates toward the Earth; but on the other hand, the Earth by a mutual action equally gravitates toward the Moon; which is also abundantly confirmed in this philosophy, where the Tides in the Sea and the Precession of the Equinoxes are treated; which arise from the action both of the Moon and of the Sun upon the Earth. Hence lastly, we discover by what law the force of gravity decreases at great distances from the Earth. For since gravity is in no way different from the Moon’s centripetal force, and this is reciprocally proportional to the square of the distance; it follows that it is in that very ratio that the force of gravity decreases.

<summary>Roger Cotes demonstrates that the centripetal force governing the Moon's orbit is identical to terrestrial gravity, proving that gravity extends into space. This identity establishes that gravity follows the inverse square law and accounts for celestial phenomena like tides and the precession of the equinoxes through mutual attraction.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's preface, expanding the principle of universal gravitation from the Earth-Moon system to the entire solar system, including the primary planets and their moons.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE:

<sig>a</sig>
<meta>catchword: That</meta>

Let us now go on to the rest of the Planets. Because the revolutions of the primary planets about the Sun, and of the secondary planets about Jupiter and Saturn, are phenomena of the same kind as the revolution of the Moon about the Earth; and because it has been demonstrated, moreover, that the centripetal forces of the primary planets are directed toward the center of the Sun, and those of the secondary planets toward the centers of Jupiter and Saturn, in the same manner as the centripetal force of the Moon is directed toward the center of the Earth; and since, besides, all these forces are inversely as the squares of the distances from the centers, in the same manner as the centripetal force of the Moon is as the square of the distance from the Earth; we must, of course, conclude that the nature of all is the same. Therefore, as the Moon gravitates toward the Earth, and the Earth in turn toward the Moon; so also all the secondary planets will gravitate toward their primary planets, and the primary planets in turn toward their secondary; and so all the primary planets toward the Sun; and the Sun in turn toward the primary planets.

Therefore the Sun gravitates toward all the planets, and all the planets toward the Sun. For the secondary planets, while they accompany the primary, revolve meanwhile with the primary planets about the Sun. Therefore, by the same argument, the planets of both kinds gravitate toward the Sun, and the Sun toward them. That the secondary planets gravitate toward the Sun is, moreover, abundantly clear from the inequalities <note>The "inequalities" refers to the periodic perturbations or irregularities in the Moon's orbital motion.</note> of the Moon; a most accurate theory of which, revealed with a most admirable sagacity, we find explained in the third book of this work.

<summary>Roger Cotes argues that the same gravitational force governing the Earth and Moon applies to all primary and secondary planets, establishing a system of mutual attraction between the Sun and all planetary bodies. He cites the complex motions of the Moon as evidence for this universal gravitational influence.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Cotes, gravitation, primary planets, secondary planets, Sun, Moon, Jupiter, Saturn, centripetal force, inverse square law, Principia Book III</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's preface, moving from the gravity of planets to the motion and gravity of comets, illustrating the universal reach of the Sun's attractive force.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

That the attractive power of the Sun is propagated on all sides to prodigious distances, and is diffused to every part of the wide space that surrounds it, is most evidently shown by the motion of the Comets; which, coming from places immensely distant from the Sun, approach very near to it, and sometimes so near that in their perihelia <note>The perihelion is the point in the orbit of a planet, asteroid, or comet that is nearest to the sun.</note> they almost touch its body. The theory of these bodies was altogether unknown to astronomers until, in our own times, our excellent author most happily discovered it and demonstrated the truth of it by most certain observations. So it is now apparent that the Comets move in conic sections having their foci in the Sun’s center, and by radii drawn to the Sun describe areas proportional to the times. But from these phenomena it is manifest, and mathematically demonstrated, that those forces by which the Comets are retained in their orbits are directed toward the Sun, and are inversely proportional to the squares of the distances from its center. Therefore, the Comets gravitate toward the Sun; and therefore the attractive force of the Sun not only acts on the bodies of the Planets, placed at given distances and very nearly in the same plane, but reaches also to the Comets in the most different parts of the heavens and at the most different distances. This, therefore, is the nature of gravitating bodies: to propagate their force at all distances to all other gravitating bodies. But from there it follows that all the Planets and Comets attract each other mutually, and gravitate mutually toward each other; which is also confirmed by the perturbation of Jupiter and Saturn, observed by astronomers, which is caused by the mutual actions of these two Planets upon each other;

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<summary>Roger Cotes explains that comets follow the same gravitational laws as planets, proving that the Sun’s gravity extends vastly and that all celestial bodies attract one another.</summary>

We have now proceeded far enough to show that it must be acknowledged that the Sun, the Earth, and all the heavenly bodies attending the Sun, attract each other mutually. Therefore, all the smallest particles of matter in every one of them must have their individual attractive forces, whose effect is proportional to their quantity of matter, as was shown above regarding the terrestrial particles. At different distances, these forces will also be in the inverse square ratio of the distances; for it is mathematically demonstrated,
<margin>NB</margin>
that particles attracting according to this law will compose globes attracting according to the same law.

The previous conclusions are grounded on this axiom which is accepted by all philosophers: namely, that effects of the same kind—that is, those whose known properties are the same—originate from
<margin>3.</margin>
the same causes and have the same unknown properties as well. For who doubts that if gravity is the cause of the descent of a stone in *Europe*, it is also the cause of the same descent in *America*? If there is a mutual gravitation between a stone and the Earth in *Europe*, who will deny the same to be mutual in *America*? If in *Europe* the attractive force of a stone and the Earth is composed of the attractive forces of their parts, who will deny the same composition in *America*? If in *Europe* the attraction of the Earth is propagated to all kinds of bodies and to all distances, why may it not as well be propagated in a similar manner in *America*? All philosophy is founded on this rule; for if that is taken away, we can affirm nothing of

<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's argument regarding the universal properties of matter, specifically addressing the logical necessity of treating gravity as a primary quality similar to extension and mobility. It builds upon the third rule of reasoning in philosophy mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

# Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

of universals. The constitution of particular things is known by observations and experiments; and when that is done, it is by this rule that we judge universally of the nature of such things in general.

Since then all bodies, whether upon Earth or in the Heavens, are heavy, so far as we can make any experiments or observations concerning them; we must certainly allow that gravity is found in all bodies universally. And in the same way as we ought not to suppose that any bodies can be anything other than extended, movable, or impenetrable; so we ought not to conceive that any bodies can be anything other than heavy. The extension, mobility, and impenetrability of bodies become known to us only by experiments; and in the very same manner, their gravity becomes known to us. All bodies we can make any observations upon are extended, movable, and impenetrable; and from that, we conclude all bodies—even those we have no observations concerning—to be extended and movable and impenetrable. So all bodies we can make observations on, we find to be heavy; and from that, we conclude all bodies—including those we have no observations of—to be heavy also. If anyone should say that the bodies of the fixed Stars are not heavy because their gravity is not yet observed; they may say for the same reason that they are neither extended, nor movable, nor impenetrable, because these qualities of the fixed Stars are not yet observed. In short, either gravity must have a place among the primary qualities of all bodies, or extension, mobility, and impenetrability must not. And if the nature of things is not rightly explained by the gravity of bodies, it will not be

<summary>Cotes argues that gravity must be considered a universal property of all matter, just like extension and impenetrability, because it is confirmed through the same process of experimental observation and inductive reasoning.</summary>

Some, I know, disapprove of this conclusion and mutter something about occult qualities. They are continually cavilling with us, claiming that gravity is an occult property, and that occult causes are to be quite ba-
<margin>YB</margin>
nished from philosophy. But the answer to this is easy: those are indeed occult causes whose existence is hidden—imagined but not proven—but not those whose real existence is clearly demonstrated by observations. Therefore, gravity can by no means be called an occult cause of the celestial motions, because it is plain from the phenomena that such a power does really exist. Rather, those have recourse to occult causes who set up imaginary vortices, made of a matter entirely fictitious and imperceptible to our senses, to direct those motions.

But shall gravity therefore be called an occult cause and thrown out of philosophy because the 
<note>The following phrase is underlined in the original manuscript</note> cause of gravity is hidden and not yet discovered? 
Those who affirm this should be careful not to fall into an absurdity that may overturn the foundations of all philosophy. For causes usually proceed in a continuous chain from those that are more compound to those that are more simple; when we have arrived at the most simple cause, we can go no further. Therefore, no mechanical account or explanation of the most simple cause is to be expected or given; for if it could be given, the cause would not be the most simple. Will you then call these most simple causes "occult" and reject them? Then you must reject those that immediately depend upon them, and those which depend upon those last,

<summary>Roger Cotes argues that gravity is a proven phenomenon rather than an "occult quality," contrasting it with the purely speculative "vortices" of other philosophers. He explains that fundamental causes cannot always have a mechanical explanation, as they are the simplest links in the chain of causality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's defense of Newtonian physics against critics who label gravity as "supernatural" because its mechanical cause is unknown. He argues that primary properties of matter, by definition, do not have mechanical explanations and defends the Newtonian method of deriving laws from observed phenomena over the Cartesian method of using imaginary causes.</meta>

# Mr. COTES'S PREFACE.

until philosophy is quite cleared and freed from all causes.

There are some who say that gravity is supernatural and call it a perpetual miracle. Therefore, they want it rejected because supernatural causes have no place in physics. It is hardly worth spending time answering this ridiculous objection, which overturns all philosophy. For they will either deny that gravity exists in bodies—which cannot be said—or they will call it supernatural because it is not produced by the other properties of bodies, and therefore not by mechanical causes. But there are certainly primary properties of bodies; and these, because they are primary, have no dependence on others. Let them consider whether all these are not likewise supernatural and should be rejected in the same way; and then consider what kind of philosophy we would be left with.

There are some who dislike this celestial physics because it contradicts the opinions of *Descartes* and seems hard to reconcile with them. Let them enjoy their own opinion, but let them act fairly and not deny us the same liberty they demand for themselves. Since the *Newtonian* philosophy appears true to us, let us have the liberty to embrace and keep it, and to follow causes proved by phenomena rather than causes that are only imagined and not yet proven. The business of true philosophy is to derive the nature of things from causes that truly exist, and to inquire into those laws upon which the Great Creator actually chose to found this most beautiful frame of the world, rather than those by which He might have done the same had He so pleased. It is reasonable enough to suppose that from several

causes, somewhat differing from each other, the same effect may arise; but the true cause will be that from which it truly and actually arises. The others have no place in true philosophy. The same motion of the hour hand in a clock may be caused either by a hanging weight or a spring enclosed within. But if a certain clock were actually moved by a weight, we would laugh at a man who supposed it was moved by a spring and, based on that principle—adopted suddenly without further examination—tried to explain the motion of the pointer. For certainly, the path he should have taken was to look into the internal parts of the machine, so that he <margin>3.</margin> might find the true principle of the proposed motion. A similar judgment should be made regarding those philosophers who insist that the heavens are filled with a most subtle matter, which is perpetually carried around in vortices. For even if they could explain the phenomena ever so accurately by their hypotheses, we could not yet say that they have discovered true philosophy and the true causes of the celestial motions, unless they could either demonstrate that those causes actually exist, or at least that no others exist. Therefore, if it is made clear that the attraction of all bodies is a property actually existing *in rerum naturâ* <note>in the nature of things</note>; and if it is also shown how the motions of the celestial bodies may be explained by that property, it would be very irrelevant for anyone to object that these motions ought to be accounted for by vortices—even if we were to allow that such an explanation of those motions was possible. But we allow no such thing; for the phenomena can by no means be accounted for by vor-

vortices; as our author has abundantly proved from the clearest reasons. So men must be strangely fond of chimeras who can spend their time so idly as to patch up a ridiculous fiction and present it with new comments of their own.

If the bodies of the planets and comets are carried around the sun in vortices, the bodies so carried, and the parts of the vortices immediately surrounding them, must be carried with the same velocity and in the same direction, and have the same density and the same *vis inertiae* <note>force of inertia</note> corresponding to the bulk of the matter. But it is certain that the planets and comets, when in the very same parts of the heavens, are carried with various velocities and in various directions. Therefore, it necessarily follows that those parts of the celestial fluid which are at the same distances from the sun must revolve at the same time with different velocities in different directions; for one kind of velocity and direction is required for the motion of the planets, and another for that of the comets. But since this cannot be accounted for, we must either say that all the celestial bodies are not carried around by vortices, or else that their motions are derived, not from one and the same vortex, but from several distinct ones which fill and pervade the spaces around the sun.

But if several vortices are contained in the same space, and are supposed to penetrate each other and to revolve with different motions—then because these motions must agree with those of the bodies carried about by them, which are perfectly regular and performed in conic sections which are sometimes very eccentric and sometimes nearly circles—one may very reasonably ask how it happens that these vortices remain intact and have suffered

<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's critique of the vortex theory, arguing that such fictitious systems are unnecessarily complex and no better than arbitrary fantasies.</meta>

no kind of disturbance over so many ages from the actions of conflicting matter. Certainly, if these fictitious motions are more complex and harder to explain than the true motions of the planets and comets, there seems to be no point in admitting them into philosophy; since every cause ought to be simpler than its effect. Allowing men to indulge their own fancies, suppose someone were to claim that the planets and comets are surrounded by atmospheres like our *Earth*; a hypothesis that seems more reasonable than that of vortices. Let him then claim that these atmospheres, by their own nature, move around the *Sun* and describe conic sections—a motion much more easily imagined than that of vortices penetrating each other. Lastly, let him claim that the planets and comets are carried around the *Sun* by these atmospheres of theirs, and then applaud his own wisdom in discovering the causes of celestial motions. He who rejects this fable must also reject the other; for two drops of water are no more alike than this hypothesis of atmospheres and that of *vortices*.

<margin><unclear>scribble</unclear></margin>
*Galileo* has shown that when a thrown stone moves in a parabola, its deflection into that curve from its straight path is caused by the gravity of the stone toward the *Earth*—that is, by an occult quality. But now someone more clever than he might come along to explain the cause in this way. He will suppose a certain subtle matter—not detectable by our sight, our touch, or any other of our senses—which fills the spaces near and adjacent to the surface of the *Earth*; and he will claim that this matter is moved in different directions and with various, often contrary, motions, describing

<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's critique of the vortex theory, specifically addressing how arbitrary "subtle fluids" could be used to explain any motion—like a stone’s parabolic path—without actually providing a scientific cause. # Mr. COTES’S PREFACE.

parabolic curves. Then see how easily he may account for the deflection of the stone mentioned above. The stone, he says, floats in this subtle fluid, and following its motion, cannot help but describe the same figure. But the fluid moves in parabolic curves; and therefore the stone must move in a parabola as a matter of course. Would not the cleverness of this philosopher be thought very extraordinary, who could deduce the appearances of nature from mechanical causes—matter and motion—so clearly that even the simplest man may understand it? Or indeed, should we not smile to see this new *Galileo* taking so much mathematical effort to introduce occult qualities into philosophy, from which they have been so happily excluded? But I am ashamed to dwell so long upon trifles.

The sum of the matter is this: the number of Comets is certainly very great; their motions are perfectly regular, and they observe the same laws as those of the Planets. The orbits in which they move are conic sections, and very eccentric ones at that. They move in every direction toward all parts of the Heavens and pass through the planetary regions with all possible freedom, and their motion is often contrary to the order of the signs. These phenomena are most evidently confirmed by astronomical observations and cannot be accounted for by vortices. In fact, they are utterly irreconcilable with the vortices of the Planets. There can be no room for the motions of Comets unless the celestial spaces are entirely cleared of that fictitious matter.

For if the Planets are carried around the Sun in vortices, the parts of the vortices that immediately surround every Planet must be of the same density as the Planet, as was shown above. There-

He concludes that the celestial spaces must be void of such fictitious matter for these observed planetary and cometary motions to exist.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's critique of the Cartesian vortex theory. # Mr. COTES'S PREFACE.

From this will arise a massive resistance to the passage of the Comets, which inevitably would be very perceptible; if not enough to stop and entirely absorb their motions. But now it appears from the perfectly regular motion of the Comets that they experience no resistance that is in the least bit perceptible. Therefore, they meet with no matter of any kind that has any resisting force, or, consequently, any density or *vis inertiae*. For the resistance of mediums arises either from the *inertia* of the fluid matter or from its lack of lubricity. The resistance that arises from a lack of lubricity is very small and is scarcely observable in commonly known fluids, unless they are very viscous like oil and honey. The resistance we find in air, water, quicksilver, and similar fluids that are not viscous is almost entirely of the first kind. It cannot be reduced by a greater degree of subtlety if the density and *vis inertiae* (to which this resistance is proportional) remain; as is most clearly demonstrated by our Author in his noble theory of resistances in the second book.

Bodies moving through a fluid gradually communicate their motion to the surrounding fluid; through this communication, they lose their own motion and are consequently slowed down. Therefore, the retardation is proportional to the motion communicated. When the velocity of the moving body is constant, the communicated motion is proportional to the density of the fluid; and therefore the retardation or resistance will be proportional to that same density of the fluid. Nor can this resistance be removed unless the fluid, flowing around to the rear parts of the body, restores the lost motion. Now, this cannot be achieved unless the pressure of the fluid on the rear

Therefore, the resistance of fluids arising from their <term>vis inertiae</term> can by no means be removed. So we must conclude that the celestial fluid has no <term>vis inertiae</term> because it has no resisting force; that it has no force with which to communicate motion because it has no <term>vis inertiae</term>; that it has no force to produce any change in one or more bodies because it has no force with which to communicate motion; and that it has no kind of efficacy because it has no faculty with which to produce any change of any kind. Therefore, this hypothesis may certainly and justly be called ridiculous and unworthy of a philosopher, since it is altogether without foundation and does not in the least serve to explain the nature of things. Those who would have the heavens filled with a fluid matter, but suppose it to be void of any <term>vis inertiae</term>, do indeed deny a vacuum in words, but allow it in fact. For since a fluid matter of that kind can in no way be distinguished from empty space, the dispute is now about the names and not the natures of things. If any are so fond of matter that they will by no means admit of a space void of body, let us consider what conclusion they must eventually reach.

<meta>This page continues Roger Cotes's preface to the second edition of Newton's *Principia*. # Mr. Cotes's Preface.

Nature might be assisted everywhere by a subtle ether pervading and filling all things; however, this cannot be said, since we have shown from the phenomena of the comets that this ether has no efficacy at all. Or they will say that it became so by the same will of God for some unknown end; which ought not to be said, because for the same reason a different constitution might just as well be supposed. Or lastly, they will not say that it was caused by the will of God, but by some necessity of its nature. Therefore, they will at <margin><unclear>Art 3</unclear></margin> last sink into the mire of that infamous herd who dream that all things are governed by Fate, and not by Providence, and that matter exists by the necessity of its nature always and everywhere, being infinite and eternal. But assuming these things, it must also be everywhere uniform; for a variety of forms is entirely inconsistent with necessity. It must also be unmoved; for if it were necessarily moved in any determinate direction with any determinate velocity, it would by a similar necessity be moved in a different direction with a different velocity; but it can never move in different directions with different velocities; therefore, it must be unmoved. Without any doubt, this world, so diversified with that variety of forms and motions we find in it, could arise from nothing but the perfectly free will of God directing and presiding over all.

From this fountain, those laws which we call the laws of Nature have flowed; in these there appear many traces indeed of the wisest contrivance, but not the least shadow of necessity. Therefore, we must not seek these from uncertain conjectures, but learn them from observations and ex-

# Mr. COTES'S PREFACE.

...periments. He who thinks he can find the true principles of physics and the laws of natural things by the force of his own mind alone, and the internal light of his reason, must either suppose that the world exists by necessity, and by that same necessity follows the laws proposed; or, if the order of Nature was established by the will of GOD, that he himself—a miserable reptile—can tell what was best to be done. All sound and true philosophy is founded on the appearance of things; even if these appearances draw us, however much against our wills, to such principles as most clearly manifest to us the most excellent counsel and supreme dominion of the All-wise and Almighty Being, those principles are not therefore to be set aside because some men may perhaps dislike them. They may call them, if they please, miracles or <term>occult qualities</term>; but names maliciously given ought not to be a disadvantage to the things themselves, unless they will say at last that all philosophy ought to be founded on atheism. Philosophy must not be corrupted to please these men, for the order of things will not be changed.

Fair and impartial judges will therefore rule in favor of this most excellent method of philosophy, which is founded on experiments and observations. It can hardly be said or imagined what light and what splendor has been added to this method by this admirable work of our illustrious author; whose fortunate and sublime genius, resolving the most difficult problems and reaching discoveries of which the mind of man was previously thought incapable, is deservedly admired by all those who are more than superficially skilled in these matters. The gates are now set open; and by

<meta>This page concludes Roger Cotes's preface, moving from the defense of Newton's experimental method to a concluding praise of the universe's divine order and the theological implications of these scientific discoveries.</meta>

# Mr. COTES'S PREFACE.

By these means, we may freely enter into the knowledge of the hidden secrets and wonders of natural things. He has so clearly explained and set before our eyes the most beautiful structure of the System of the World; that if King *Alphonsus* were now alive, he would not complain about a lack of either simplicity or harmony within it. Therefore, we may now more closely behold the beauties of Nature and occupy ourselves with delightful contemplation; and—what is the best and most valuable fruit of philosophy—be encouraged from there to more profoundly reverence and adore the great Maker and Lord of all. He must be blind who, from the most wise and excellent arrangements of things, cannot see the infinite Wisdom and Goodness of their Almighty Creator; and he must be mad and senseless who refuses to acknowledge them.

## DEFINITIONS.

### DEF. I.

*The quantity of matter is a measure of the same, arising from its density and bulk jointly.*

Thus, air of double density, in double the space, is quadruple in quantity; in triple the space, it is sextuple in quantity. The same is to be understood of snow, and fine dust or powders, that are condensed by compression or liquefaction; and of all bodies that are by any causes

<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

however differently condensed. In this context, I have no regard for a medium—if any such exists—that freely flows through the gaps between the parts of bodies. It is this quantity that I will refer to everywhere hereafter by the name of body or mass. And this same quantity is known by the weight of each body; for it is proportional to the weight, as I have found by very accurately performed experiments with pendulums, which shall be shown hereafter.

## DEFINITION II.

*The quantity of motion is its measure, arising from the velocity and quantity of matter combined.*

The motion of the whole is the sum of the motions of all its parts; and therefore, in a body double in quantity with equal velocity, the motion is double; with twice the velocity, it is quadruple.

## DEFINITION III.

*The <term>Vis Insita</term>, or innate force of matter, is a power of resisting by which every body, as much as it is able, strives to persevere in its present state, whether it be of rest or of moving uniformly forward in a straight line.*

This force is always proportional to the body whose force it is, and differs in no way from the inactivity of the mass except in the way we conceive of it. Because of the inactivity of matter, a body is not without difficulty

<summary>Newton establishes that mass is proportional to weight based on pendulum experiments, defines momentum as the product of mass and velocity, and introduces "vis insita" (inertia) as a body's natural resistance to changes in motion.</summary>

<keywords>mass, weight, pendulums, quantity of motion, momentum, velocity, Vis Insita, innate force, inertia, resistance, straight line</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of inertia (Vis Inertiae) and the distinction between resistance and impulse, then moves on to define impressed force.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Book I. of Natural Philosophy.</header>

difficulty put out of its state of rest or motion. For this reason, this *Vis insita* may, by a most significant name, be called *Vis Inertiae*, or the Force of Inactivity. But a body exerts this force only when another force impressed upon it endeavors to change its condition; and the exercise of this force may be considered as both resistance and impulse. It is resistance insofar as the body, to maintain its present state, withstands the force impressed; it is impulse insofar as the body, by not easily giving way to the impressed force of another, endeavors to change the state of that other. Resistance is usually ascribed to bodies at rest, and impulse to those in motion; but motion and rest, as commonly conceived, are only relatively distinguished, nor are those bodies always truly at rest which are commonly assumed to be so.

## DEFINITION IV.

*An impressed force is an action exerted upon a body in order to change its state, either of rest or of moving uniformly forward in a straight line.*

This force consists in the action only and remains no longer in the body when the action is over. For a body maintains every new state it acquires by its *Vis Inertiae* alone. Impressed forces have different origins, such as from percussion, from pressure, or from centripetal force.

# D E F I N I T I O N V.

<margin>Note</margin>
->*A centripetal force is that by which bodies are drawn or impelled, or in any way tend, toward a point as to a center.*<-

Gravity is of this sort, by which bodies tend toward the center of the Earth; magnetism, by which iron tends toward the lodestone; and that force, whatever it may be, by which the planets are perpetually drawn aside from the <term>rectilinear</term> motions which they would otherwise pursue, and made to revolve in <term>curvilinear</term> orbits. A stone, whirled around in a sling, endeavors to pull away from the hand that turns it; and by that endeavor, it stretches the sling, and does so with even greater force as it is revolved with greater velocity, and as soon as it is let go, it flies away. That force which opposes this endeavor, and by which the sling perpetually draws the stone back toward the hand and retains it in its orbit—because it is directed toward the hand as the center of the orbit—I call the centripetal force. And the same thing is to be understood for all bodies revolving in any orbits. They all endeavor to recede from the centers of their orbits; and were it not for the opposition of a contrary force which restrains them to, and keeps them in, their orbits—which I therefore call centripetal—they would fly off in straight lines with a uniform motion. A projectile, if it were not for the force of gravity, would not deviate toward the Earth, but would move away from it in a straight line and with a uniform motion, if air resistance were removed. It is by its gravity that it is perpetually drawn aside from its rectilinear course and made to deviate toward the Earth, more or less, according

The quantity of any centripetal force may be considered as of three kinds: absolute, accelerative, and motive.

### D E F I N I T I O N VI.

*The absolute quantity of a centripetal force is the measure of the same, proportional to the efficacy of the cause that propagates it from the center, through the surrounding spaces.*

Thus the magnetic force is greater in one lodestone and less in another, according to their sizes and strength.

### D E F I N I T I O N VII.

*The accelerative quantity of a centripetal force is the measure of the same, proportional to the velocity which it generates in a given time.*

Thus the force of the same lodestone is greater at a shorter distance, and less at a greater distance; likewise, the force of gravity is greater in valleys, less on the tops of exceedingly high mountains, and even less (as shall be shown hereafter) at greater distances from the body of the Earth;

### D E F I N I T I O N VIII.

*The motive quantity of a centripetal force is the measure of the same, proportional to the motion which it generates in a given time.*

<margin><unclear>scribble</unclear></margin>
Thus the weight is greater in a larger body, and less in a smaller body; it is greater near the Earth, and less at more remote distances. This kind of quantity is the centripetency, or propensity of the whole body toward the center, or as I might say, its weight; and it is always known by the quantity of a force equal and opposite to it, which is just sufficient to prevent the descent of the body.

These quantities of forces we may, for the sake of brevity, call by the names of motive, accelerative, and absolute forces; and for the sake of distinction, consider them with respect to the bodies that tend toward the center, to the places of those bodies, and to the center of force toward which they tend: That is to say, I refer the motive force to the body, as an endeavor and propensity of the whole toward a center, arising from the propensities of the several parts taken together; the accelerative force to the place of the body, as a certain power or energy diffused from the center to all surrounding places to move the bodies that are in them; and the absolute force to the center, as endowed with some cause, without which those motive forces would not be propagated through the surrounding spaces; whether that cause is some central body (such as a lodestone in

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of centripetal forces, specifically distinguishing between the mathematical description of forces and their physical causes, while further defining the relationship between accelerative and motive forces.</meta>

# Mathematical Principles Book I.

<margin>?</margin>
<margin>NB</margin>
the center of the force of *magnetism*, or the Earth in the center of the gravitating force) or anything else that does not yet appear. For I here intend only to give a *mathematical* notion of those forces, without considering their *physical* causes and seats.

Therefore, the *accelerative* force will stand in the same relation to the *motive* force as *velocity* does to *motion*. For the *quantity of motion* arises from the *velocity* multiplied by the *quantity of matter*; and the *motive force* arises from the *accelerative force* multiplied by the same quantity of matter. For the sum of the actions of the *accelerative* force upon the several particles of the body is the *motive* force of the whole. Hence it is that near the surface of the Earth, where the accelerative gravity (or the force productive of gravity in all bodies) is the same, the motive gravity or the *weight* is proportional to the *body*; but if we should ascend to higher regions, where the accelerative gravity is less, the *weight* would be likewise diminished and would always be proportional to the product of the *body* multiplied by the *accelerative* gravity. So in those regions where the accelerative gravity is reduced by half, the *weight* of a body two or three times smaller will be four or six times less.

<margin>? NB</margin>
I likewise call *attractions* and *impulses*, in the same sense, *accelerative* and *motive*; and I use the words *attraction*, *impulse*, or *propensity* of any sort toward a center interchangeably and indifferently, one for another, considering those forces not *physically* but *mathematically*. Therefore, the reader is not to imagine that by those words I anywhere take it upon myself to define the kind or the manner of any *action*, the causes, or the physical reason for it, or that I attribute *forces* in a true and *physical* sense to certain centers (which are only *mathematical* points) when

He emphasizes that his terms describe mathematical relationships rather than defining the actual physical causes of attraction.</summary>

### S C H O L I U M.

So far, I have laid down the definitions of such words as are less common, and explained the sense in which I want them to be understood in the following discourse. I do not define Time, Space, Place, and Motion, as they are well known to everyone. I must only observe that the general public conceives of those quantities only in relation to objects of the senses. From this, certain prejudices arise; to remove these, it is helpful to distinguish between Absolute and Relative, True and Apparent, and Mathematical and Common.

I. Absolute, True, and Mathematical Time, of itself and from its own nature, flows at a constant rate without regard to anything external, and is also called Duration. Relative, Apparent, and Common Time is some sensible and external measure of Duration (whether accurate or uneven) by means of motion, which is commonly used instead of True Time—such as an hour, a day, a month, or a year.

II. Absolute Space, in its own nature and without regard to anything external, always remains similar and immovable. Relative Space is some movable dimension or measure of absolute space, which our senses determine by its position relative to bodies, and which is commonly mistaken for immovable space. Such is the dimension of an underground, an aerial, or a celestial space, determined by its position in relation to the Earth. Absolute and Relative Space are the same in shape and size, but they do not always remain numerically the same. For instance, if the Earth moves,

<meta>catchword: a space</meta>

<summary>Isaac Newton introduces the Scholium to distinguish between absolute and relative concepts of time and space, arguing that while the general public perceives these through relations to physical objects, they possess absolute, mathematical properties independent of external factors.</summary>
<keywords>Isaac Newton, Scholium, Absolute Time, Relative Time, Duration, Absolute Space, Relative Space, Mathematical Principles, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Absolute and Relative Space, specifically addressing how a relative space (like the air around the Earth) moves through absolute space. It then defines the concepts of Place and Motion.</meta>

a space of our air, which relatively and in respect to the Earth remains always the same, will at one time be one part of the absolute space into which the air passes; at another time it will be another part of it, and so, understood absolutely, it will be perpetually changeable.

III. Place is a part of space which a body occupies and is, according to the space, either absolute or relative. I say a *part of space*, not the position nor the external surface of the body. For the places of equal solids are always equal; but their surfaces, because of their different shapes, are often unequal. Positions properly have no quantity, nor are they so much the places themselves as the properties of places. The motion of the whole is the same thing as the sum of the motions of the parts; that is, the movement of the whole out of its place is the same thing as the sum of the movements of the parts out of their places. Therefore, the place of the whole is the same thing as the sum of the places of the parts, and for that reason, it is internal and within the whole body.

IV. Absolute motion is the movement of a body from one absolute place to another, and relative motion is the movement from one relative place to another. Thus, in a ship under sail, the relative place of a body is that part of the ship which the body occupies—or that part of its interior space which the body fills, and which therefore moves along with the ship. Relative rest is the remaining of the body in the same part of the ship or of its interior space. But real, absolute rest is the remaining of the body in the same part of that immovable space in which the ship itself, its interior, and all that it contains are moved. Therefore, if the Earth is really at rest, the body,

# Book I. of Natural Philosophy.

which is relatively at rest in the ship, will actually and absolutely move with the same velocity that the ship has on the Earth. But if the Earth also moves, the true and absolute motion of the body will arise partly from the true motion of the Earth in immovable space and partly from the relative motion of the ship on the Earth. And if the body also moves relatively within the ship, its true motion will arise partly from the true motion of the Earth in immovable space, and partly from the relative motions of both the ship on the Earth and the body in the ship; and from these relative motions, the relative motion of the body on the Earth will arise. For example, if that part of the Earth where the ship is located were truly moved toward the East with a velocity of 10,010 parts, while the ship itself—with a fresh gale and full sails—is carried toward the West with a velocity expressed as 10 of those parts, but a sailor walks within the ship toward the East with 1 part of that velocity, then the sailor will be moved truly and absolutely in immovable space toward the East with a velocity of 10,001 parts, and relatively on the Earth toward the West with a velocity of 9 of those parts.

In astronomy, absolute time is distinguished from relative time by the equation, or correction, of common time. For natural days are actually unequal, although they are commonly considered equal and used as a measure of time. Astronomers correct this inequality for their more accurate calculation of celestial motions. It may be that there is no such thing as a perfectly uniform motion by which time may be accurately measured. All motions may be accelerated or slowed down, but the true or uniform progress of absolute time is not subject to any change. The duration or continued existence of things remains the same, whether the motions

The necessity of this equation for determining the times of a phenomenon is evidenced by the experiments of the pendulum clock as well as by the eclipses of the satellites of *Jupiter*.

<margin>2) NB</margin>
As the order of the parts of time is immutable, so too is the order of the parts of space. Suppose those parts were moved out of their places, and they would be moved (if the expression may be allowed) out of themselves. For times and spaces are, as it were, the places both of themselves and of all other things. All things are placed in time regarding the order of succession;
<margin>*</margin>
and in space regarding the order of situation. It is from their essence or nature that they are places, and it is absurd for the primary places of things to be movable. These are, therefore, the absolute places, and movements out of those places are the only absolute motions.

<margin>†</margin>
But because the parts of space cannot be seen or distinguished from one another by our senses, we use sensible measures of them in their place. For we define all places based on the positions and distances of things from any body considered to be immovable; and then, with respect to such places, we estimate all motions, considering bodies as being transferred from some of those places into others. And so, instead of absolute places and motions, we use relative ones, and we do so without any inconvenience in common affairs; but in philosophical inquiries, we ought to abstract from our senses and consider the things themselves, distinct from what are merely sensible measures of them. For it may be that there is no body truly at rest to which the places and motions of others may be referred.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>Newton explains that absolute time and space are distinct from their sensible measures, noting that while relative measures are sufficient for daily life, philosophical inquiry requires distinguishing absolute motion from relative positions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinctions between absolute and relative categories, moving from the nature of space and time to the specific properties that differentiate absolute and relative rest and motion.</meta>

# Book I. of Natural Philosophy.

<meta>catchword: A property</meta>
<meta>marginal tick marks in right margin at lines 5, 12, and 18</meta>

But we may distinguish *Rest* and *Motion*, *absolute* and *relative*, from one another by their *Properties*, *Causes*, and *Effects*. It is a property of *Rest* that bodies really at rest do rest with respect to one another. And therefore, as it is possible that in the remote regions of the fixed stars, or perhaps far beyond them, there may be some body absolutely at rest; but it is impossible to know from the *position* of bodies to one another in our regions whether any of these keep the same position relative to that remote body; it follows that absolute rest cannot be determined from the position of bodies in our regions.

It is a property of *motion* that the parts which retain given positions relative to their wholes partake in the motions of those wholes. For all the parts of *revolving* bodies endeavor to recede from the *axis* of *motion*; and the *impetus* of bodies moving forward arises from the joint *impetus* of all the parts. Therefore, if surrounding bodies are moved, those that are *relatively* at rest within them will partake in their *motion*. For this reason, the true and *absolute motion* of a body cannot be determined by its *translation* from those which only seem to *rest*: for the external bodies ought not only to appear at rest, but to be really at rest. For otherwise, all included bodies, besides their *translation* from the vicinity of the surrounding ones, partake likewise in their true motions; and though that translation was not made, they would not be really at rest but only seem to be so. For the surrounding bodies stand in the same relation to the surrounded as the *exterior* part of a whole does to the *interior*, or as the *shell* does to the *kernel*; but if the *shell* moves, the *kernel* will also move as part of the whole, without any removal from the vicinity of the *shell*.

<summary>This page defines the properties of absolute rest and motion, explaining that absolute rest cannot be determined by local relative positions and that internal parts of a system inherit the motion of the surrounding whole.</summary>
<keywords>absolute rest, relative motion, fixed stars, revolving bodies, axis of motion, impetus, translation, physical mechanics</keywords>
