<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinctions between absolute and relative categories, specifically focusing on how the motion of a place affects the bodies within it and how forces distinguish true motion from relative motion.</meta>

### Mathematical Principles Book I.

A property very closely related to the preceding one is this: if a place moves, whatever is placed within it moves along with it. Therefore, a body that is moved from a place already in motion also partakes in the motion of its place. For this reason, all motions starting from places in motion are nothing other than parts of entire and absolute motions; and every entire motion is composed of the motion of the body out of its first place, the motion of this place out of its own place, and so on, until we reach some immovable place, as in the previously mentioned example of the sailor. Therefore, entire and absolute motions can be determined in no other way than by immovable places; for that reason, I previously referred those absolute motions to immovable places, and relative ones to movable places. Now, no other places are immovable except those that, from infinity to infinity, all retain the same given positions relative to one another; and on this account, they must always remain unmoved, and thereby constitute what I call immovable space.

The causes by which true and relative motions are distinguished from one another are the forces impressed upon bodies to generate motion. True motion is neither generated nor altered except by some force impressed upon the body moved; but relative motion may be generated or altered without any force impressed upon the body. For it is sufficient only to impress some force on other bodies with which the first is compared; by their moving away, that relationship may be changed which constituted the relative rest or motion of the first body. Again, true motion always undergoes some change from any force impressed upon the moving body; but relative motion does not necessarily undergo any change from such forces. For if the same forces are likewise impressed on those other
->bodies.<-

# Book I of Natural Philosophy.

bodies with which the comparison is made, so that the relative position may be preserved, then that condition will be preserved in which the relative motion consists. And therefore, any relative motion may be changed when the true motion remains unaltered, and the relative may be preserved when the true motion undergoes some change. For these reasons, true motion definitely does not consist in such relations.

The effects which distinguish absolute from relative motion are the forces of receding from the axis of circular motion. For there are no such forces in a circular motion that is purely relative; but in a true and absolute circular motion, they are greater or lesser according to the quantity of the motion. If a vessel, suspended by a long cord, is turned around so many times that the cord is strongly twisted, then filled with water and held at rest along with the water; and afterward, by the sudden action of another force, it is whirled around the opposite way, and while the cord is untwisting itself, the vessel continues for some time in this motion; the surface of the water will at first be flat, just as it was before the vessel began to move. But the vessel, by gradually communicating its motion to the water, will make it begin perceptibly to revolve, and recede little by little from the middle and ascend the sides of the vessel, forming itself into a concave shape (as I have experienced). The swifter the motion becomes, the higher the water will rise, until at last, performing its revolutions in the same time as the vessel, it becomes relatively at rest within it. This ascent of the water shows its endeavor to recede from the axis of its motion; and the true and absolute circular motion of the water, which is here directly contrary to the relative motion, reveals itself and may be measured by this endeavor. At first, when the relative motion of the water in the vessel was

# Mathematical Principles Book I.

<page-num>16</page-num>

...greatest, it produced no endeavor to recede from the axis: the water showed no tendency toward the circumference, nor any ascent toward the sides of the vessel, but remained with a flat surface, and therefore its true circular motion had not yet begun. But afterward, when the relative motion of the water had decreased, its ascent toward the sides of the vessel proved its endeavor to recede from the axis; and this endeavor showed the real circular motion of the water perpetually increasing, until it had acquired its greatest quantity, when the water rested relatively in the vessel. And therefore this endeavor does not depend upon any translation of the water in respect to the surrounding bodies, nor can true circular motion be defined by such translations. There is only one real circular motion of any one revolving body, corresponding to only one power of endeavoring to recede from its axis of motion, as its proper and adequate effect; but relative motions in one and the same body are innumerable, according to the various relations it bears to external bodies, and like other relations, are altogether devoid of any real effect, other than how they may perhaps participate in that one and only true motion. And therefore, in the system of those who suppose that our heavens, revolving below the sphere of the fixed stars, carry the planets along with them, the several parts of those heavens and the planets—which are indeed relatively at rest in their heavens—do yet really move. For they change their position relative to one another (which never happens to bodies truly at rest) and, being carried together with their heavens, participate in their motions, and as parts of revolving wholes, endeavor to recede from the axis of their motions.

Wherefore relative quantities are not the quantities themselves whose names they bear, but those sensible measures...

He applies this principle to planetary motion, asserting that planets have true motion even if they appear at rest within their celestial spheres.</summary>

measures of them (either accurate or inaccurate) which are commonly used instead of the measured quantities themselves. And if the meaning of words is to be determined by their use, then by the names Time, Space, Place, and Motion, their measures are properly to be understood; and the expression will be unusual and purely mathematical if the measured quantities themselves are meant. On which account, they <margin>HB</margin> strain the Sacred Writings who interpret those words as the measured quantities. Nor do those people less defile the purity of mathematical and philosophical truths who confuse real quantities themselves with their relations and common measures.

It is indeed a matter of great difficulty to discover, and effectively to distinguish, the true motions of particular bodies from the apparent ones: because the parts of that immovable space in which those motions are performed do by no means come under the observation of our senses. Yet the situation is not altogether hopeless; for we have some arguments to guide us, partly from the apparent motions, which are the differences of the true motions; partly from the forces, which are the causes and effects of the true motions. For instance, if two globes, kept at a given distance from one another by means of a cord that connects them, were revolved about their common center of gravity, we might, from the tension of the cord, discover the endeavor of the globes to recede from the axis of their motion, and from there we might compute the quantity of their circular motions. And then if any equal forces should be impressed at once on the opposite faces of the globes to augment or diminish their circular motions, from the increase or decrease of the tension of the cord, we might infer the increment or decrement of their motions; and from there it would be found on what faces

those forces should be applied, so that the motions of the globes might be increased the most; that is, we might discover their rear faces, or those which, in the circular motion, follow behind. But once the following faces are known, and consequently the opposite ones that precede them, we would likewise know the direction of their motions. In this way, we could find both the quantity and the direction of this circular motion, even in an immense vacuum where there was nothing external or perceptible with which the globes could be compared. Now, if some remote bodies were placed in that space that always maintained a fixed position relative to one another, as the Fixed Stars do in our regions, we could not actually determine from the relative movement of the globes among those bodies whether the motion belonged to the globes or to the bodies. However, if we observed the cord and found that its tension was exactly the tension which the motions of the globes required, we could conclude that the motion was in the globes and that the bodies were at rest; then, finally, from the movement of the globes among the bodies, we would find the direction of their motions. But how we are to determine true motions from their causes, effects, and apparent differences—and *vice versa*, how from motions, whether true or apparent, we may gain knowledge of their causes and effects—shall be explained more extensively in the following treatise. For it was for this purpose that I composed it.

<header>LAWS of Natural Philosophy.</header>

# Axioms or Laws of Motion.

---

## LAW I.

*Every body continues in its state of rest, or of uniform motion in a straight line, unless it is compelled to change that state by forces impressed upon it.*

Projectiles continue in their motions, so far as they are not slowed by the resistance of the air or driven downwards by the force of gravity. A top, whose parts are constantly pulled aside from straight-line motions by their cohesion, does not stop rotating, except as it is slowed by the air. The larger bodies of the planets and comets, meeting with less resistance in freer spaces, preserve their motions—both progressive and circular—for a much longer time.

## LAW II.

*The change of motion is always proportional to the motive force impressed; and is made in the direction of the straight line in which that force is impressed.*

If any force generates a motion, a double force will generate double the motion, and a triple force will generate triple the motion, whether that force is impressed all at once and

<sig>C 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: at</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Newton's first two laws of motion, defining inertia and the proportional relationship between force and the resulting change in motion. It provides examples of these laws in action, such as projectiles, rotating tops, and the movement of celestial bodies.</summary>

<keywords>Axioms, Laws of Motion, Law of Inertia, Law of Acceleration, motive force, gravity, projectiles, planets, comets, Newton</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Second Law of Motion regarding the composition of forces and introduces the Third Law of Motion, which defines the equality of action and reaction.</meta>

at once, or gradually and successively. And this motion (being always directed the same way as the generating force) if the body moved before, is added to or subtracted from the former motion, according to whether they directly cooperate with or are directly contrary to each other; or joined obliquely, when they are oblique, so as to produce a new motion compounded from the direction of both.

### LAW III.

*To every action there is always opposed an equal reaction: or the mutual actions of two bodies upon each other are always equal, and directed to contrary parts.*

Whatever draws or presses another is as much drawn or pressed by that other. If you press a stone with your finger, the finger is also pressed by the stone. If a horse pulls a stone tied to a rope, the horse (if I may say so) will be equally pulled back towards the stone: for the stretched rope, by the same effort to relax or unbend itself, will pull the horse as much towards the stone as it pulls the stone towards the horse, and will obstruct the progress of the one as much as it advances that of the other. If a body strikes another, and by its force changes the motion of the other; that body also (because of the equality of the mutual pressure) will undergo an equal change in its own motion, towards the contrary part. The changes made by these actions are equal, not in the velocities, but in the motions of bodies; that is to say, if the bodies are not hindered by any other obstacles. For because the motions

## COROLLARY I.

*A body acted upon by two forces joined together will describe the diagonal of a parallelogram in the same time that it would describe the sides by those forces acting separately.* (Plate 1, Figure 1.)

If a body in a given time, by force *M* applied separately at point *A*, should be carried with a uniform motion from *A* to *B*; and by force *N* applied separately at the same place, should be carried from *A* to *C*: complete the parallelogram *ABCD*, and by both forces acting together, it will in the same time be carried along the diagonal from *A* to *D*. For since force *N* acts in the direction of the line *AC*, which is parallel to *BD*, this force (by the second law) will not at all alter the velocity generated by the other force *M*, by which the body is carried towards the line *BD*. The body, therefore, will arrive at the line *BD* in the same time, whether force *N* is applied or not; and therefore, at the end of that time, it will be found somewhere on the line *BD*. By the same argument, at the end of the same time, it will be found somewhere on the line *CD*. Therefore, it will be found at point *D*, where both lines meet. And it will move in a straight line from *A* to *D* according to Law 1.

<meta>catchword: COROL-</meta>

## COROLLARY II.

*And from this is explained the composition of any one direct force AD out of any two oblique forces AB and BD; and, conversely, the resolution of any one direct force AD into two oblique forces AB and BD: which composition and resolution are abundantly confirmed by the principles of mechanics. (Fig. 2.)*

Suppose the unequal radii OM and ON, drawn from the center O of any wheel, support the weights A and P by the cords MA and NP, and the forces of those weights needed to move the wheel were required. Through the center O, draw the straight line KOL, meeting the cords perpendicularly at K and L; and from the center O, using OL (the greater of the distances OK and OL) as the radius, describe a circle meeting the cord MA at D. After drawing OD, make AC parallel to it and DC perpendicular to it. Now, since it makes no difference whether the points K, L, and D of the cords are fixed to the plane of the wheel or not, the weights will have the same effect whether they are suspended from the points K and L, or from D and L. Let the total force of the weight A be represented by the line AD, and let it be resolved into the forces AC and CD; of which the force AC, pulling the radius OD directly from the center, will have no effect on moving the wheel. However, the other force DC, pulling the radius DO perpendicularly, will have the same effect as if it pulled perpendicularly on the radius OL (which is equal to OD); that is, it will have the same

same effect as the weight *P*, if that weight is to the

But the weight *p* pressing upon those two oblique planes may be considered as a <term>wedge</term> between the two internal surfaces of a body split by it; and from this, the forces of the <term>wedge</term> and the <term>mallet</term> may be determined. For the force with which the weight *p* presses the plane *pQ* is to the force with which the same—whether by its own gravity or by the blow of a <term>mallet</term>—is impelled in the direction of the line *pH* toward both the planes, as *pN* is to *pH*; and to the force with which it presses the other plane *pG*, as *pN* is to *NH*. And thus the force of the <term>screw</term> may be deduced from a similar <term>resolution of forces</term>; it being nothing other than a <term>wedge</term> impelled with the force of a <term>lever</term>. Therefore, the use of this Corollary spreads far and wide, and by that broad application, its truth is further confirmed.

For on what has been said depends the whole doctrine of <term>mechanics</term>, which has been variously demonstrated by different authors.

From this, the forces of <term>machines</term> are easily deduced, which are composed of wheels, <term>pulleys</term>, <term>levers</term>, cords, and weights, ascending directly or obliquely, and other mechanical powers; as also the force of the <term>tendons</term> to move the <term>bones</term> of animals.

## C O R O L L A R Y III.

*The quantity of motion, which is collected by taking the sum of the motions directed toward the same directions, and the difference of those that are directed to contrary directions, suffers no change from the action of bodies among themselves.*

For Action and its opposite Reaction are equal, by Law 3, and therefore, by Law 2, they produce equal changes in the motions toward opposite directions. Therefore, if the motions are directed toward the same directions, whatever is added to the motion of the preceding body will be subtracted from the motion of that which follows; so that the sum will be the same as before. If the bodies meet with contrary motions, there will be an equal deduction from the motions of both; and therefore the difference of the motions directed toward opposite directions will remain the same.

Thus if a spherical body *A* with two units of velocity is triple the size of a spherical body *B* which follows in the same straight line with ten units of velocity; the motion of *A* will be to that of *B* as 6 is to 10. Suppose then their motions to be 6 units and 10 units, and the sum will be 16 units. Therefore upon the meeting of the bodies, if *A* acquires 3, 4, or 5 units of motion, *B* will lose as many; and therefore after <term>reflection</term> *A* will proceed with 9, 10, or 11 units, and *B* with 7, 6, or 5 units; the sum always remaining 16 units as before. If the body *A* acquires 9, 10, 11, or 12 units of motion, and therefore after meeting proceeds with 15, 16, 17, or 18 units; the body *B*, losing

<meta>catchword: B, losing</meta>

<summary>Corollary III establishes the principle of the conservation of momentum, explaining that the total quantity of motion in a system remains constant regardless of internal actions and reactions. This is demonstrated by showing that any momentum gained by one body in a collision is equally lost by the other.</summary>
<keywords>Quantity of motion, momentum, Law 3, Law 2, Action and Reaction, reflection, collision, spherical bodies</keywords>

B, losing as many parts as A has gained, will either proceed with one part, having lost 9; or stop and remain at rest, having lost its whole <term>progressive motion</term> of 10 parts; or it will go back with one part, having not only lost its whole motion, but (if I may say so) one part more; or it will go back with 2 parts, because a <term>progressive motion</term> of 12 parts is taken away. And so the sums of the <term>conspiring motions</term> (15 + 1, or 16 + 0) and the differences of the <term>contrary motions</term> (17 - 1 and 18 - 2) will always be equal to 16 parts, as they were before the meeting and <term>reflection</term> of the bodies. But, the motions being known with which the bodies proceed after <term>reflection</term>, the <term>velocity</term> of either will also be known, by taking the <term>velocity</term> after to the <term>velocity</term> before <term>reflection</term> in the same proportion as the motion after is to the motion before. As in the last case, where the motion of body A was 6 parts before <term>reflection</term> and 18 parts after, and the <term>velocity</term> was 2 parts before <term>reflection</term>; its <term>velocity</term> after <term>reflection</term> will be found to be 6 parts, by saying: as the 6 parts of motion before is to 18 parts after, so are 2 parts of <term>velocity</term> before <term>reflection</term> to 6 parts after.

But if the bodies are either not spherical, or moving in different straight lines <term>impinge</term> obliquely one upon the other, and their motions after <term>reflection</term> are required: in those cases we are first to determine the position of the <term>plane</term> that touches the concurring bodies at the point of <term>concourse</term>; then the motion of each body (by Corollary 2) is to be resolved into two, one <term>perpendicular</term> to that <term>plane</term>, and the other <term>parallel</term> to it. This done, because the bodies act upon each other in the direction of a line <term>perpendicular</term> to this <term>plane</term>, the <term>parallel</term> motions are to be retained the same after <term>reflection</term> as before; and to the <term>perpendicular</term> motions we are to assign equal changes towards the contrary parts; in

in such a manner that the sum of the <term>conspiring</term> motions, and the difference of the <term>contrary motions</term>, may remain the same as before. From such kinds of reflections, the circular motions of bodies about their own centers also sometimes arise. But these are cases which I do not consider in what follows; and it would be too tedious to demonstrate every particular detail that relates to this subject.

## C O R O L L A R Y IV.

*The common <term>center of gravity</term> of two or more bodies does not alter its state of motion or rest by the actions of the bodies among themselves; and therefore the common <term>center of gravity</term> of all bodies acting upon each other (excluding outward actions and impediments) is either at rest or moves uniformly in a <term>straight line</term>.*

For if two points proceed with a uniform motion in <term>straight lines</term>, and their distance is divided in a given ratio, the dividing point will be either at rest or proceed uniformly in a <term>straight line</term>. This is demonstrated hereafter in Lemma 23 and its Corollary, when the points are moved in the same plane; and by a similar way of arguing, it may be demonstrated when the points are not moved in the same plane. Therefore, if any number of bodies move uniformly in <term>straight lines</term>, the common <term>center of gravity</term> of any two of them is either at rest or proceeds uniformly in a <term>straight line</term>; because the line which connects the centers of those two bodies so moving is divided at that common center in a given ratio. In like manner the common center of those

In the same way, the common center of these three and of a fourth body is either at rest or moves uniformly in a <term>straight line</term>; because the distance between the common center of the three bodies and the center of the fourth is there also divided in a given ratio, and so on in *infinitum*. Therefore, in a <term>system of bodies</term> where there is neither any <term>mutual action</term> among themselves, nor any foreign force impressed upon them from without, and which consequently move uniformly in <term>straight lines</term>, the common <term>center of gravity</term> of them all is either at rest or moves uniformly forward in a <term>straight line</term>.

Moreover, in a <term>system of bodies</term> acting mutually upon each other, since the distances between their centers and the common <term>center of gravity</term> of both are reciprocally as the bodies, the <term>relative motions</term> of those bodies—whether approaching or receding from that center—will be equal among themselves. Therefore, since the changes that happen to motions are equal and directed toward opposite parts, the common center of those bodies, by their <term>mutual action</term> between themselves, is neither advanced nor retarded, nor suffers any change as to its state of motion or rest. But in a system of several bodies, because the common <term>center of gravity</term> of any two acting mutually upon each other suffers no change in its state by that action; and much less the common <term>center of gravity</term> of the others with which that action does not intervene; but the distance between those two centers is divided by the common <term>center of gravity</term> of all the bodies into parts reciprocally proportional to the total sums of those bodies

<meta>This page continues the proof regarding the common center of gravity of a system of bodies, concluding that its state of motion or rest is unaffected by internal mutual actions between the bodies within the system.</meta>

whose centers they are; and therefore while those two centers retain their state of motion or rest, the common center of all also retains its state. It is manifest that the common center of all never suffers any change in the state of its motion or rest from the actions of any two bodies between themselves. But in such a system, all the actions of the bodies among themselves either happen between two bodies, or are composed of actions interchanged between some two bodies; and therefore they never produce any alteration in the common center of all as to its state of motion or rest. Therefore, since that center—when the bodies do not act mutually upon one another—either is at rest or moves uniformly forward in some <term>straight line</term>, it will, notwithstanding the mutual actions of the bodies among themselves, always persevere in its state, either of rest or of proceeding uniformly in a <term>straight line</term>, unless it is forced out of this state by the action of some power impressed from without upon the whole system. And therefore the same law takes place in a system consisting of many bodies as in one single body, with regard to their persevering in their state of motion or rest. For the progressive motion, whether of one single body or of a whole system of bodies, is always to be estimated from the motion of the <term>center of gravity</term>.

### C O R O L L A R Y V.

->*The motions of bodies included in a given*<-
->*space are the same among themselves,*<-
->*whether that space is at rest or moves*<-
->*uniformly forward in a <term>straight line</term>*<-
->*without any circular motion.*<-

For the differences of the motions tending in the same direction, and the sums of those tending in opposite directions, are initially (by assumption) the same in both cases; and it is from those sums and differences that the collisions and impulses arise with which the bodies mutually strike one another. Therefore (by Law 2), the effects of those collisions will be equal in both cases; and therefore the mutual motions of the bodies among themselves in one case will remain equal to the mutual motions of the bodies among themselves in the other. We have clear proof of this from the experiment of a ship: where all motions occur in the same manner, whether the ship is at rest or is carried uniformly forward in a <term>straight line</term>.

<meta>catchword: COROL-</meta>

<summary>This corollary establishes the principle of Galilean relativity, stating that the relative motions of bodies within a system are identical whether the system is at rest or moving uniformly in a straight line. Newton illustrates this by noting that internal collisions and movements behave the same way on a ship at rest as they do on one moving steadily forward.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression from Corollary V, which discussed uniform motion of a system, to Corollary VI, which addresses the effects of equal parallel accelerative forces on a system of bodies. It then begins the Scholium, providing historical and experimental context for the Laws of Motion.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>LAWS of <term>Natural Philosophy</term>.</header>

## COROLLARY VI.

*If bodies, moving in any way among themselves, are urged in the direction of parallel lines by equal accelerative forces, they will all continue to move among themselves in the same manner as if they had been urged by no such forces.*

For these forces, acting equally (with respect to the quantities of the bodies to be moved) and in the direction of parallel lines, will (by Law 2) move all the bodies equally (as to velocity) and therefore will never produce any change in the positions or motions of the bodies among themselves.

## SCHOLIUM.

So far I have laid down such principles as have been received by mathematicians and are confirmed by an abundance of experiments. By the first two Laws and the first two Corollaries, *Galileo* discovered that the descent of bodies observed the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>The "duplicate ratio of the time" refers to the square of the time ($t^2$); in modern terms, distance is proportional to the square of the time ($d = \frac{1}{2}at^2$).</note> of the time, and that the motion of projectiles followed the curve of a parabola; experience agrees with both, except in so far as these motions are slightly retarded by the resistance of the air. When a body is falling, the uniform force of its gravity, acting equally, impresses equal forces upon that body in equal intervals of time, and therefore generates equal velocities; and in the total time, it impresses a total force and generates a total velocity proportional to the time. And the spaces described in

proportional times are as the velocities and the times combined; that is, in a <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>The "duplicate ratio of the times" refers to the square of the times ($t^2$).</note> of the times. And when a body is thrown upwards, its uniform gravity impresses forces and takes away velocities proportional to the times; and the times of ascending to the greatest heights are proportional to the velocities to be taken away, and those heights are as the velocities and the times combined, or in the duplicate ratio of the velocities. And if a body is projected in any direction, the motion arising from its projection is compounded with the motion arising from its gravity. For example, if the Body *A* by its motion of projection alone (*Fig. 3.*) could describe the straight line *AB* in a given time, and by its motion of falling alone could describe the altitude *AC* in the same time; complete the parallelogram *ABDC*, and the body, by that compounded motion, will at the end of the time be found in the place *D*; and the curved line *AED*, which that body describes, will be a parabola, to which the straight line *AB* will be a tangent at *A*; and whose ordinate *BD* will be proportional to the square of the line *AB*. On these same laws and corollaries depend those things which have been demonstrated concerning the times of the vibration of pendulums, and are confirmed by the daily experiments of pendulum clocks. By these same laws, together with the third Law, *Sir Christopher Wren*, *Dr. Wallis*, and *Mr. Huygens*, the greatest geometers of our times, each determined the rules of the collision and reflection of hard bodies; and at nearly the same time, they communicated their discoveries to the *Royal Society*, exactly agreeing among themselves regarding those rules. *Dr. Wallis* was indeed somewhat earlier in his publication; then followed *Sir Christopher Wren*, and lastly, *Mr. Huygens*. <meta>This page continues the experimental demonstration of the Third Law of Motion, using pendulums to account for air resistance and calculate the precise momentum of bodies before and after collision.</meta>

Let the chord of the arc *t A* represent the velocity which the body *A* had in the place *A* immediately after reflection. For *t* will be the true and correct place to which the body *A* would have ascended if the resistance of the air had been removed. In the same way, we are to correct the place *k* to which the body *B* ascends by finding the place *l* to which it would have ascended *in a vacuum*. And thus everything may be subjected to experiment in the same manner as if we were really placed *in a vacuum*. 

Once these things are done, we are to take the product (if I may say so) of the body *A* and the chord of the arc *T A* (which represents its velocity), so that we may have its motion in the place *A* immediately before reflection; and then by the chord of the arc *t A*, so that we may have its motion in the place *A* immediately after reflection. And likewise, we are to take the product of the body *B* and the chord of the arc *B l*, so that we may have its motion immediately after reflection. In like manner, when two bodies are released together from different places, we are to find the motion of each both before and after reflection; then we may compare the motions with each other and determine the effects of the reflection. 

Testing this with ten-foot pendulums, using unequal as well as equal bodies and making the bodies collide after a descent through large distances, such as 8, 12, or 16 feet, I always found—without an error of 3 inches—that when the bodies collided directly, equal changes in opposite directions were produced in their motions; and consequently, that action and reaction were always equal. For example, if the body *A* impinged upon the body *B* (at rest) with 9 units of motion, and losing 7, proceeded after reflection with 2, the body *B* was carried forward with those 7 units. If the bodies collided

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Third Law of Motion, providing specific numerical examples of momentum conservation during collisions and addressing potential experimental errors in the pendulum tests.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>LAWS of Natural Philosophy.</header>

collided with opposite motions, *A* with twelve parts of motion and *B* with six, then if *A* receded with 2, *B* receded with 8, that is, with a deduction of 14 parts of motion on each side. For by subtracting 12 parts from the motion of *A*, nothing will remain; but by subtracting 2 parts more, a motion will be generated of 2 parts in the opposite direction. Similarly, by subtracting 14 parts from the motion of the body *B* (which had 6 parts), a motion of 8 parts is generated in the opposite direction. But if the bodies were both made to move in the same direction—*A*, the swifter, with 14 parts of motion, and *B*, the slower, with 5—and after reflection *A* continued with 5 while *B* likewise continued with 14 parts, then 9 parts were transferred from *A* to *B*. And so it was in other cases. By the encounter and collision of bodies, the quantity of motion—calculated from the sum of the motions directed in the same way, or from the difference of those directed in opposite ways—was never changed. For an error of an inch or two in measurements may be easily attributed to the difficulty of executing everything with accuracy. It was not easy to release the two pendulums so exactly together that the bodies would strike one another at the lowest point *AB*, nor to mark the places *s* and *k* to which the bodies ascended after the collision. Moreover, some errors might also have occurred from the unequal density of the parts of the pendulous bodies themselves, and from irregularities of texture resulting from other causes.

nature; I must add that the experiments we have been describing, by no means depending upon that quality of hardness, succeed as well in soft as in hard bodies. For if the rule is to be tried in bodies not perfectly hard, we only need to diminish the reflection in such a certain proportion as the quantity of the elastic force requires. By the theory of *Wren* and *Huygens*, bodies absolutely hard return from one another with the same velocity with which they meet. But this may be affirmed with more certainty of bodies perfectly elastic. In bodies imperfectly elastic, the velocity of the return is to be diminished along with the elastic force; because that force (except when the parts of bodies are bruised by their collision, or suffer some such extension as happens under the strokes of a hammer) is (as far as I can perceive) certain and determined, and makes the bodies return from one another with a relative velocity which is in a given ratio to that relative velocity with which they met. This I tried in balls of wool, made up tightly and strongly compressed. For first, by letting go the pendulous bodies and measuring their reflection, I determined the quantity of their elastic force; and then, according to this force, I estimated the reflections that ought to happen in other cases of collision. And other experiments made afterward agreed with this computation; the balls always receded from one another with a relative velocity which was to the relative velocity with which they met in a ratio of about 5 to 9. Balls of steel returned with almost the same velocity; those of cork with a velocity something less; but in balls of glass the proportion was about 15 to 16. And thus the third law, so far as it regards percussions and reflections, is proved by a theory exactly agreeing with experience.

<summary>Newton demonstrates that the Third Law of Motion applies to both hard and elastic bodies, using experiments with various materials to show that relative velocity after impact is proportional to the velocity before impact.</summary>
<keywords>elasticity, hardness, relative velocity, Christopher Wren, Christiaan Huygens, Third Law of Motion, percussion, reflection, experimental physics, wool, steel, cork, glass</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Third Law of Motion, moving from the collision of bodies to the principles of mutual attraction and gravitation.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>LAWS of Natural Philosophy.</header>
<sig>D 3</sig>

In the case of attractions, I briefly demonstrate the matter in this way. Suppose an obstacle is interposed to hinder the meeting of any two bodies, *A* and *B*, which mutually attract one another. Then, if either body, such as *A*, is more attracted toward the other body *B* than that other body *B* is toward the first body *A*, the obstacle will be more strongly pushed by the pressure of body *A* than by the pressure of body *B*. Therefore, it will not remain in equilibrium; instead, the stronger pressure will prevail and will cause the system of the two bodies, together with the obstacle, to move directly toward the direction where *B* lies. In free spaces, it would move forward infinitely with a motion that is perpetually accelerated. This is absurd and contrary to the first law. For by the first law, the system ought to persevere in its state of rest, or of moving uniformly forward in a straight line; therefore, the bodies must press the obstacle equally and be equally attracted to one another. I performed this experiment with a <term>loadstone</term> and iron. If these are placed apart in suitable vessels and made to float near one another in still water, neither of them will propel the other; rather, by being equally attracted, they will sustain each other’s pressure and rest at last in an equilibrium.

Similarly, the gravitation between the Earth and its parts is mutual. Let the Earth *FI* (*Fig. 5.*) be cut by any plane *EG* into two parts, *EGF* and *EGI*; their weights toward each other will be mutually equal. For if by another plane *HK*, parallel to the former *EG*, the greater part *EGI* is cut into two parts, *EGKH* and *HKI*, of which *HKI* is equal to the part *EFG* first cut off: it is evident that the middle part *EGKH* will have no tendency by its own weight toward either side, but will hang, as it were, and rest in an equilibrium between both. But the one extreme part *HKI*

<meta>This page continues the demonstration that the gravitational attraction between the parts of the Earth is mutual and equal, then transitions to applying the Third Law of Motion to mechanical instruments like balances and pulleys.</meta>

will with its whole weight bear upon and press the middle part toward the other extreme part E G F; and therefore the force with which E G I (the sum of the parts H K I and E G K H) tends toward the third part E G F, is equal to the weight of the part H K I—that is, to the weight of the third part E G F. And therefore the weights of the two parts E G I and E G F toward each other are equal, as I intended to prove. And indeed, if those weights were not equal, the whole Earth, floating in the non-resisting ether, would give way to the greater weight and, retreating from it, would be carried off *to infinity*.

And as those bodies are equivalent in power during collision and reflection whose velocities are inversely proportional to their innate forces, so in the use of mechanical instruments, those agents are equivalent in power and mutually sustain each other’s contrary pressure whose velocities—estimated according to the direction of the forces—are inversely proportional to the forces.

So those weights have equal force to move the arms of a balance which, during the movement of the balance, are inversely proportional to their upward and downward velocities. That is, if the ascent or descent is direct, those weights have equal force which are inversely proportional to the distances of the points at which they are suspended from the axis of the balance; but if they are turned aside by the intervention of oblique planes or other obstacles and made to ascend or descend obliquely, those bodies will be equivalent in power which are inversely proportional to the heights of their ascent and descent measured perpendicularly; and that is because of the direction of gravity downward.

And in like manner in the pulley, or in a combination of pulleys, the force of a hand drawing the rope directly—that is, toward the weight—whether ascending directly or

# LAWS of Natural Philosophy.

In clocks and similar instruments, made up of a combination of wheels, the contrary forces that promote and impede the motion of the wheels will mutually sustain each other if they are inversely proportional to the velocities of the parts of the wheel on which they are impressed.

The force of the screw used to press a body is to the force of the hand that turns the handles by which it is moved, as the circular velocity of the handle at the part where it is pushed by the hand is to the progressive velocity of the screw toward the pressed body.

The forces by which the wedge presses or drives the two parts of the wood it cleaves are to the force of the mallet upon the wedge, as the progress of the wedge in the direction of the force impressed upon it by the mallet is to the velocity with which the parts of the wood yield to the wedge, in the direction of lines perpendicular to the sides of the wedge. And the same explanation applies to all machines.

The power and use of machines consists only in this: that by diminishing the velocity, we may increase the force, and vice versa. From this, in all sorts of proper machines, we have the solution to this problem: *To move a given weight with a given power*, or with a given force to overcome any other given resistance. For if machines are so designed that the velocities of the agent and the resistant are inversely proportional to their forces, the agent will just sustain the resistant; but with a greater disparity of velocity, it will overcome it. So that if the disparity of velocities is great enough to overcome all that resistance which com—

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the third law of motion as applied to mechanical systems, specifically addressing the balance of forces and resistances like attrition and cohesion. This page follows the previous discussion on pulleys, wheels, screws, and wedges.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

...commonly arises either from the attrition of contiguous bodies as they slide by one another, or from the cohesion of continuous bodies that are to be separated, or from the weights of bodies to be raised; the excess of the force remaining, after all those resistances are overcome, will produce an acceleration of motion proportional to it, both in the parts of the machine and in the resisting body. But to treat of mechanics is not my present business. I only intended to show by these examples the great extent and certainty of the third law of motion. For if we estimate the action of the agent from its force and velocity combined; and likewise the reaction of the impediment from the combined velocities of its several parts, and from the forces of resistance arising from the attrition, cohesion, weight, and acceleration of those parts; the action and reaction in the use of all sorts of machines will be found always equal to one another. And so far as the action is transmitted by the intervening instruments and at last impressed upon the resisting body, the ultimate determination of the action will always be contrary to the determination of the reaction.

### LEMMA I.

*Quantities, and the ratios of quantities, which in any finite time converge continually toward equality, and before the end of that time approach nearer to each other than by any given difference, become ultimately equal.*

## LEMMA II.

*If in any figure AacE (Plate 1, Figure 6) bounded by the straight lines Aa and AE, and the curve acE, there are inscribed any number of parallelograms Ab, Bc, Cd, etc., contained under equal bases AB, BC, CD, etc., and the sides Bb, Cc, Dd, etc., parallel to one side Aa of the figure; and the parallelograms aKbl, bLcm, cMdn, etc., are completed. Then if the width of those parallelograms is supposed to be diminished, and their number to be increased to infinity: I say that the ultimate ratios which the inscribed figure AKbLcMdD, the circumscribed figure AalbmcndoE, and the curvilinear figure AabcdE will have to one another are ratios of equality.*

For the difference between the inscribed and circumscribed figures is the sum of the parallelograms Kl, Lm, Mn, and Do; that is (because all their bases are equal), the rectangle formed by one of their bases Kb and the sum of their altitudes Aa; that is, the rectangle ABla.

SECTION I. *of Natural Philosophy.*

But this rectangle, because its width *AB* is supposed to be diminished *infinitely*, becomes smaller than any given space. And therefore (by Lemma 1) the figures inscribed and circumscribed become ultimately equal to one another; and even more so will the intermediate curved figure be ultimately equal to either.
*Q. E. D.*

## L E M M A III.

*The same ultimate ratios are also ratios of equality when the widths AB, BC, CD, etc., of the parallelograms are unequal, and are all diminished infinitely.*

For suppose *AF* is equal to the greatest width, and complete the parallelogram *FAaf*. This parallelogram will be greater than the difference between the inscribed and circumscribed figures; but, because its width *AF* is diminished *infinitely*, it will become smaller than any given rectangle. *Q. E. D.*

COROLLARY 1. Hence the ultimate sum of those vanishing parallelograms will in all parts coincide with the curved figure.

COROLLARY 2. Even more so will the straight-lined figure, contained under the chords of the vanishing arcs *ab, bc, cd*, etc., ultimately coincide with the curved figure.

COROLLARY 3. And also the circumscribed straight-lined figure contained under the tangents of the same arcs.

COROLLARY 4. And therefore these ultimate figures (as to their perimeters *acE*) are not straight-lined, but curved limits of straight-lined figures.

# L E M M A IV.

*If in two figures AacE and PprT (Plate 1, Fig. 7), you inscribe (as before) two ranks of parallelograms, with an equal number in each rank, and when their widths are diminished to infinity, the ultimate ratios of the parallelograms in one figure to those in the other—each compared respectively—are the same; I say that those two figures AacE and PprT are to one another in that same ratio.*

For as the parallelograms in the one are individually compared to the parallelograms in the other, so (by composition) is the sum of all in the one to the sum of all in the other; and so is the one figure to the other, because (by Lemma 3) the ratio of the former figure to the former sum, and the latter figure to the latter sum, are both ratios of equality. *Q. E. D.*

## LEMMA V.

*In similar figures, all types of homologous sides, whether curvilinear or rectilinear, are proportional; and the areas are in the duplicate ratio of the homologous sides.*

## LEMMA VI.

*If any arc ACB (Plate 2, Fig. 1) given in position is subtended by its chord AB, and at any point A in the middle of the continuous curvature, is touched by a straight line AD, extended in both directions; then if the points A and B approach one another and meet, I say the angle BAD, contained between the chord and the tangent, will be diminished to infinity, and ultimately will vanish.*

For if that angle does not vanish, the arc *ACB* would form with the tangent *AD* an angle equal to a rectilinear angle; and therefore the curvature at point *A* would not be continuous, which is contrary to the supposition.

# L E M M A VII.

> *With the same things being supposed, I say that the ultimate ratio of the arc, chord, and tangent, any one to any other, is the ratio of equality. Plate 2, Figure 1.*

For while the point *B* approaches toward the point *A*, consider always *AB* and *AD* as produced to the remote points *b* and *d*, and draw *bd* parallel to the secant *BD*; and let the arc *Acb* be always similar to the arc *ACB*. Then, supposing the points *A* and *B* to coincide, the angle *dAb* will vanish, by the preceding lemma; and therefore the straight lines *Ab* and *Ad* (which are always finite) and the intermediate arc *Acb* will coincide, and become equal among themselves. Therefore the straight lines *AB* and *AD*, and the intermediate arc *ACB* (which are always proportional to the former ones) will vanish; and ultimately acquire the ratio of equality. *Q. E. D.*

*COR.* 1. Hence if through *B* (*Plate 2, Figure 2*) we draw *BF* parallel to the tangent, always cutting any straight line *AF* passing through *A* at *F*; this line *BF* will be ultimately in the ratio of equality with the evanescent arc *ACB*; because, by completing the parallelogram *AFBD*, it is always in a ratio of equality with *AD*.

*COR.* 2. And if through *B* and *A* more straight lines are drawn, such as *BE*, *BD*, *AF*, and *AG*, cutting the tangent *AD* and its parallel *BF*; the ultimate ratio of all the abscissas *AD*, *AE*, *BF*, and *BG*, and of the chord and arc *AB*, any one to any other, will be the ratio of equality.

## L E M M A VIII.

*If the straight lines AR, BR, (Plate 2, Figure 1) with the arc ACB, the chord AB, and the tangent AD, form three triangles R A B, R A C B, and R A D, and the points A and B approach and meet: I say that the ultimate form of these vanishing triangles is one of similarity, and their ultimate ratio is one of equality.*

For while the point B approaches toward the point A, always consider AB, AD, and AR as extended to the remote points b, d, and r, and r b d as drawn parallel to R D, and let the arc A c b be always similar to the arc ACB. Then, assuming the points A and B coincide, the angle b Ad will vanish; and therefore the three triangles r A b, r A c b, and r A d (which are always finite) will coincide, and on that account become both similar and equal. And therefore the triangles R A B, R A C B, and R A D, which are always similar and proportional to these, will ultimately become both similar and equal to each other. Q. E. D.
COROLLARY. And hence in all our reasonings about ultimate ratios, we may interchangeably use any one of those triangles for any other.

## L E M M A IX.

*If a straight line AE (Plate 2, Figure 3) and a curve line ABC, both given by position, intersect each other at a given angle A; and to that straight line, at another given angle, BD and CE are <term>ordinately applied</term>, meeting the curve at B and C; and as the points B and C together approach toward and meet at point A: I say that the areas of the triangles ABD and ACE will ultimately be to each other in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the sides.*

For while the points *B* and *C* approach point *A*, suppose *AD* is always extended to the remote points *d* and *e*, so that *Ad* and *Ae* are proportional to *AD* and *AE*; and let the ordinates *db* and *ec* be drawn parallel to the ordinates *DB* and *EC*, meeting the lines *AB* and *AC* (extended) at *b* and *c*. Let the curve *Abc* be similar to the curve *ABC*, and draw the straight line *Ag* so as to touch both curves at *A*, and intersect the ordinates *DB*, *EC*, *db*, and *ec* at *F*, *G*, *f*, and *g*. Then, assuming the length *Ae* remains the same, let the points *B* and *C* meet at point *A*; as the angle *cAg* vanishes, the curvilinear areas *Abd* and *Ace* will coincide with the rectilinear areas *Afd* and *Age*; and therefore (by Lemma 5) they will be to one another in the duplicate ratio of the sides *Ad* and *Ae*. But the areas *ABD* and *ACE* are always proportional to these areas, just as the sides *AD* and *AE* are proportional to those sides. And therefore the areas *ABD* and *ACE* are ultimately one

## L E M M A X.

*The spaces which a body describes by any finite force acting upon it—whether that force is fixed and unchangeable, or is continually increased or continually diminished—are, at the very beginning of the motion, to one another in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the times.*

Let the times be represented by the lines *AD* and *AE*, and the velocities produced in those times by the <term>ordinates</term> *DB* and *EC*. The spaces described with these velocities will be as the areas *ABD* and *ACE* formed by those ordinates; that is, at the very beginning of the motion (according to Lemma 9), they are in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the times *AD* and *AE.* Q.E.D.

**COROLLARY 1.** And from this, one may easily infer that the errors of bodies describing similar parts of similar figures in proportional times are nearly in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the times in which they are generated; provided that these errors are generated by any equal forces similarly applied to the bodies, and measured by the distances of the bodies from those places of the similar figures at which the bodies would have arrived in those proportional times without the action of those forces.

<meta>catchword: COR.</meta>

<summary>Lemma X establishes that for any finite force, the distance an object travels is proportional to the square of the time at the start of its motion. Corollary 1 applies this principle to explain how small deviations or "errors" in a body's path relate to the time elapsed.</summary>
<keywords>Lemma X, duplicate ratio, finite force, velocity, kinematics, proportional times, distance, Newton, errors of motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the corollaries of Lemma X, which relates force, space, and time at the beginning of motion. It concludes with a Scholium explaining the mathematical language of proportions (direct, inverse, and compound ratios) used throughout the work.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

**COR. 2.** But the errors that are generated by proportional forces similarly applied to bodies at similar parts of similar figures are proportional to the forces and the squares of the times jointly.

**COR. 3.** The same thing is to be understood of any spaces whatsoever described by bodies driven by different forces. All of these, at the very beginning of the motion, are proportional to the forces and the squares of the times jointly.

**COR. 4.** And therefore the forces are proportional to the spaces described at the very beginning of the motion directly, and to the squares of the times inversely.

**COR. 5.** And the squares of the times are proportional to the spaces described directly, and to the forces inversely.

## S C H O L I U M.

If in comparing indefinite quantities of different kinds with one another, any one is said to be proportional to any other directly or inversely, the meaning is that the former increases or decreases in the same ratio as the latter, or as its reciprocal. And if any one is said to be proportional to any other two or more directly or inversely, the meaning is that the first increases or decreases in the ratio compounded of the <term>ratios</term> in which the others, or the reciprocals of the others, increase or decrease. As if *A* is said to be proportional to *B* directly and *C* directly and *D* inversely, the meaning is that *A* increases or decreases in the same ratio as *B* $\times$ *C* $\times$ $\frac{1}{D}$; that is to say, that *A* and $\frac{BC}{D}$ are to each other in a <term>given ratio</term>.

<meta>catchword: Lem.</meta>

### L E M M A XI.

*The vanishing subtense of the angle of contact, in all curves which at the point of contact have a finite curvature, is ultimately in the duplicate ratio (the squared ratio) of the subtense of the corresponding arc. Plate 2. Figure 4.*

CASE 1. Let *AB* be that arc, *AD* its tangent, *BD* the subtense of the angle of contact perpendicular to the tangent, and *AB* the subtense of the arc. Draw *BG* perpendicular to the subtense *AB*, and *AG* perpendicular to the tangent *AD*, meeting at *G*; then let the points *D, B*, and *G* approach the points *d, b*, and *g*, and suppose *J* to be the ultimate intersection of the lines *BG* and *AG* when the points *D* and *B* have reached *A*. It is evident that the distance *GJ* may be less than any assignable length. But (from the nature of the circles passing through the points *A, B, G* and *A, b, g*) $AB^2 = AG \times BD$, and $Ab^2 = Ag \times bd$; and therefore the ratio of $AB^2$ to $Ab^2$ is compounded of the ratios of *AG* to *Ag* and of *BD* to *bd*. But because *GJ* may be assumed to be of a length less than any assignable, the ratio of *AG* to *Ag* may be such as to differ from the ratio of equality by less than any assignable difference; and therefore the ratio of $AB^2$ to $Ab^2$ may be such as to differ from the ratio of *BD* to *bd* by less than any assignable difference. Therefore, by *Lemma 1*, the ultimate ratio of $AB^2$ to $Ab^2$ is the same as the ultimate ratio of *BD* to *bd*. *Which was to be demonstrated.*

CASE 2. Now let *BD* be inclined to *AD* at any given angle, and the ultimate ratio of *BD* to *bd* will

**CASE 3.** And if we suppose the angle *D* is not given, but that the straight line *BD* converges to a given point, or is determined by any other condition whatever; nevertheless, the angles *D* and *d*, being determined by the same law, will always draw nearer to equality, and approach nearer to each other than by any assigned difference, and therefore, by *Lemma 1*, will ultimately be equal, and therefore the lines *BD* and *bd* are in the same ratio to each other as before. *Q.E.D.*

What I call the <term>sesquiplicate ratio</term> is the <term>sub-duplicate</term> <note>square root</note> of the triplicate, as being compounded of the simple and <term>subduplicate ratio</term>.

### SCHOLIUM.

But we have all along supposed the angle of contact to be neither infinitely greater nor infinitely less than the angles of contact made by circles and their tangents; that is, that the curvature at the point *A* is neither infinitely small nor infinitely great, or that the interval *AJ* is of a finite magnitude. For *DB* may be taken as *AD*²: in which case no circle can be drawn through the point *A* between the tangent *AD* and the curve *AB*, and therefore the angle of contact will be infinitely less than those of circles. And by a similar reasoning, if *DB* be made successively as *AD*³, *AD*⁴, *AD*⁵, *AD*⁶, etc., we shall have a series of angles of contact, proceeding to infinity, wherein every succeeding term is infinitely less than the preceding.

And if *DB* be made successively as *AD*², *AD*³/², *AD*⁴/³, *AD*⁵/⁴, *AD*⁶/⁵, *AD*⁷/⁶, etc., we shall have another infinite series of angles of contact, the first of which is of the same sort as those of circles, the second infinitely greater, and every succeeding one infinitely greater than the preceding. But between any two of these angles, another series of intermediate angles of contact may be interposed, proceeding both ways to infinity, wherein every succeeding angle shall be infinitely greater or infinitely less than the preceding. As if between the

Nor is nature confined to any bounds.

Those things which have been demonstrated of curve lines and the <term>superficies</term> <note>surfaces</note> which they enclose, may be easily applied to the curve surfaces and contents of solids. These lemmas are premised to avoid the tediousness of deducing complex demonstrations *ad absurdum*, according to the method of the ancient geometers. For demonstrations are more concise by the method of indivisibles; but because the hypothesis of indivisibles seems somewhat harsh, and therefore that method is considered less geometrical, I chose rather to reduce the demonstrations of the following <margin>*</margin>propositions to the first and last sums and ratios of <term>nascent and evanescent quantities</term> <note>quantities in the state of beginning to exist or vanishing</note>, that is, to the limits of those sums and ratios; and so to premise, as briefly as I could, the demonstrations of those limits. For in this way the same thing is performed as by the method of indivisibles; and now that those principles have been demonstrated, we may use them with more safety. Therefore if hereafter I should happen to consider quantities as made up of particles, or should use little curve lines instead of straight ones; I would not be understood to mean indivisibles, but evanescent divisible quantities; not the sums and ratios of determinate parts, but always the limits of sums and ratios: and the force of such demonstrations always depends on the method laid down in the foregoing lemmas.

Perhaps it may be objected, that there is no ultimate proportion of evanescent quantities; because the

He explains that his demonstrations for curves and surfaces also apply to the volumes of solids.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical defense of limits, specifically addressing the concept of the ultimate ratio by comparing it to the physical concept of instantaneous velocity at the moment a body stops.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>SECT. I. *of Natural Philosophy.*</header>
<sig>E 4</sig>

proportion, before the quantities have vanished, is not the ultimate, and once they have vanished, there is none. But by the same argument it may be alleged that a body arriving at a certain place and stopping there has no <term>ultimate velocity</term>; because the velocity, before the body reaches the place, is not its ultimate velocity, and once it has arrived, there is none. But the answer is easy; for by the <term>ultimate velocity</term> is meant that with which the body is moved—neither before it arrives at its last place and the motion ceases, nor after, but at the very instant it arrives; that is, that velocity with which the body arrives at its last place and with which the motion ceases. And in like manner, by the <term>ultimate ratio</term> of <term>evanescent quantities</term> <note>quantities that are vanishing</note> is to be understood the ratio of the quantities, not before they vanish nor afterward, but that with which they vanish. In like manner, the first ratio of <term>nascent quantities</term> <note>quantities just beginning to exist</note> is that with which they begin to be. And the first or last sum is that with which they begin and cease to be (or to be augmented or diminished). There is a limit which the velocity at the end of the motion may attain but not exceed. This is the <term>ultimate velocity</term>. And there is a similar limit in all quantities and proportions that begin and cease to be. And since such limits are certain and definite, determining them is a problem strictly <term>geometrical</term>. Moreover, we may be allowed to use whatever is <term>geometrical</term> in determining and demonstrating anything else that is likewise <term>geometrical</term>.

It may also be objected that if the <term>ultimate ratios</term> of <term>evanescent quantities</term> are given, their ultimate magnitudes will also be given; and so all quantities would consist of <term>indivisibles</term>, which is contrary to what <term>Euclid</term> demonstrated concerning <term>incommensurables</term> <note>magnitudes with no common measure</note> in the 10th book of his *Elements*. But this objection is

<meta>catchword: founded</meta>

<summary>The author defines "ultimate ratio" and "ultimate velocity" as limits reached at the precise moment a quantity vanishes or a motion ceases, rather than the values before or after that instant. This defines these limits as certain, definite, and strictly geometrical concepts, while introducing a common objection regarding indivisibles.</summary>
<keywords>ultimate velocity, ultimate ratio, evanescent quantities, nascent quantities, limits, geometry, Euclid, indivisibles, incommensurables, Elements</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical defense of limits, specifically addressing the concept of the ultimate ratio by comparing it to the physical concept of instantaneous velocity at the moment a body stops.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I</header>

...founded on a false supposition. For those <term>ultimate ratios</term> with which quantities vanish are not truly the ratios of ultimate quantities, but limits toward which the ratios of quantities decreasing without limit always converge; and which they approach more closely than by any given difference, but never go beyond, nor in effect reach, until the quantities are diminished to infinity. This will appear more evident in quantities that are infinitely great. If two quantities, whose difference is given, are increased to infinity, the <term>ultimate ratio</term> of these quantities will be given—namely, the ratio of equality; but it does not follow from this that the ultimate or greatest quantities themselves, whose ratio that is, will be given. Therefore, if in what follows, for the sake of being more easily understood, I should happen to mention quantities as "least," "<term>evanescent</term>," or "ultimate," you are not to suppose that quantities of any <term>determinate magnitude</term> are meant, but rather such as are conceived to be always diminished without end.

# SECTION II.
## Of the discovery of centripetal forces.

### PROPOSITION I. THEOREM I.

*The areas, which revolving bodies describe by radii drawn to an immovable center of force, lie in the same immovable planes, and are proportional to the times in which they are described.* Pl. 2. Fig. 5.

Suppose the time to be divided into equal parts, and in the first part of that time, let the body by its <term>innate force</term> describe the straight line AB. In the second part of that time, if not hindered, the same would (by Law 1) proceed directly to *c*, along the line *Bc* equal to AB; so that by the radii AS, BS, and *cS* drawn to the center, the equal areas ASB and *BSc* would be described. But when the body has arrived at B, suppose that a centripetal force acts at once with a great impulse, and turning aside the body from the straight line *Bc*, compels it afterwards to continue its motion along the straight line BC. Draw *cC* parallel to BS, meeting BC at C; and at the end of the second part of the time, the body (by Corollary 1 of the Laws) will be found at C, in the same plane as the triangle ASB. Join SC, and, because SB and *cC* are parallel,

<summary>Newton begins proving Kepler's Second Law, demonstrating that a body moving under a central force describes equal areas in equal times. He uses the concept of discrete time intervals and impulses to show that the resulting motion remains in a single plane and maintains proportional area over time.</summary>
<keywords>centripetal force, innate force, Law 1, area law, orbital mechanics, planetary motion, geometry, Isaac Newton</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition I, Theorem I, from page 57, completing the geometric demonstration that a body under a centripetal force sweeps out equal areas in equal times (Kepler's Second Law).</meta>

<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

parallel, the triangle *SBC* will be equal to the triangle *SBc*, and therefore also to the triangle *SAB*. By the same argument, if the centripetal force acts successively at points *C, D, E,* etc., and causes the body to describe the straight lines *CD, DE, EF,* etc., during each single interval of time, they will all lie in the same plane; and triangle *SCD* will be equal to triangle *SBC*, triangle *SDE* to *SCD*, and triangle *SEF* to *SDE*. Therefore, in equal times, equal areas are described in one immovable plane; and, by addition, any sums of those areas, such as *SADS* and *SAFS*, are proportional to the times in which they are described. Now let the number of those triangles be increased and their width decreased infinitely; and (by Corollary 4, Lemma 3) their ultimate perimeter *ADF* will be a curved line. Therefore, the centripetal force, by which the body is perpetually drawn back from the tangent of this curve, will act continuously; and any described areas *SADS* and *SAFS*, which are always proportional to the times of description, will in this case also be proportional to those times. *Q.E.D.*

COR. 1. The velocity of a body attracted toward an immovable center, in spaces without resistance, is inversely proportional to the perpendicular dropped from that center to the straight line that touches the orbit. For the velocities at those points *A, B, C, D, E* are proportional to the bases *AB, BC, CD, DE, EF* of equal triangles; and these bases are inversely proportional to the perpendiculars dropped upon them.

COR. 2. **Cor. 3.** If the chords *AB*, *BC*, and *DE*, *EF*, of arcs described in equal times in spaces void of resistance, are completed into the parallelograms *ABCV* and *DEIZ*; the forces at *B* and *E* are to each other in the ultimate ratio of the diagonals *BV* and *EZ*, when those arcs are diminished *in infinitum* <note>to infinity</note>. For the motions *Bc* and *Ef* of the body (by Corollary 1 of the Laws) are composed of the motions *Bc* and *BV*, and *Ef* and *EZ*; but *BV* and *EZ*, which are equal to *Cc* and *Ff* in the demonstration of this proposition, were generated by the impulses of the centripetal force at *B* and *E*, and are therefore proportional to those impulses.

**Cor. 4.** The forces by which bodies, in spaces void of resistance, are drawn back from rectilinear <note>straight-line</note> motions and turned into curvilinear orbits, are to one another as the versed sines of arcs described in equal times; these versed sines tend toward the center of force and bisect the chords when those arcs are diminished to infinity. For such versed sines are the halves of the diagonals mentioned in Corollary 3.

**Cor. 5.** And therefore those forces are to the force of gravity as the said versed sines are to the versed sines (perpendicular to the horizon) of those parabolic arcs which projectiles describe in the same time.

**Cor. 6.** And the same things all hold true (by Corollary 5 of the Laws) when the planes in which the bodies move, together with the centers of force which are placed in those planes, are not at rest but move uniformly forward in straight lines.

<meta>This page transitions from the corollaries of Proposition I, which established that centripetal forces describe equal areas in equal times, to Proposition II, which proves the converse: that if a body describes equal areas about a center, it is governed by a centripetal force directed toward that center.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

# PROPOSITION II. THEOREM II.

*Every body that moves in any curved line described in a plane, and which—by a radius drawn to a point either immovable or moving forward with a uniform rectilinear motion—describes areas about that point proportional to the times, is urged by a centripetal force directed to that point.*

CASE 1. For every body that moves in a curved line is (by Law 1) turned aside from its rectilinear course by the action of some force that impels it. And that force—by which the body is turned off from its rectilinear course and is made to describe, in equal times, the equal smallest triangles *SAB, SBC, SCD*, etc., about the immovable point *S*—acts at point *B* according to the direction of a line parallel to *cC* (by Proposition 40, Book 1 of the Elements <note>Euclid's Elements</note> and Law 2). That is, it acts in the direction of the line *BS*; and at point *C*, it acts according to the direction of a line parallel to *dD*, that is, in the direction of the line *CS*, and so on. Therefore, it acts always in the direction of lines tending to the immovable point *S*. *Q.E.D.* <note>Quod Erat Demonstrandum: which was to be demonstrated</note>

CASE 2. And (by Corollary 5 of the Laws) it is indifferent whether the surface in which a body describes a curvilinear figure is at rest, or moves together with the body, the figure described, and its point *S*, uniformly forward in straight lines.

<meta>catchword: COR.</meta>

<summary>This page presents Proposition II, Theorem II, demonstrating that if a body's motion describes areas proportional to time relative to a point, it must be acted upon by a centripetal force directed toward that point. This principle holds true whether the reference point is stationary or moving at a constant velocity in a straight line.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical discussion of centripetal forces following the visual diagrams provided in Plate II on the preceding page, focusing on the relationship between the description of areas and the direction of force.</meta>

<header>Section II: Of Natural Philosophy.</header>

Corollary 1. In non-resisting spaces or mediums, if the areas are not proportional to the times, the forces are not directed to the point in which the radii meet; but they deviate from it *in consequentia*, or toward the parts to which the motion is directed, if the description of the areas is accelerated; but *in antecedentia*, if it is retarded.

Corollary 2. And even in resisting mediums, if the description of the areas is accelerated, the directions of the forces deviate from the point in which the radii meet, toward the parts to which the motion tends.

## S C H O L I U M.

A body may be urged by a <term>centripetal force</term> compounded of several forces. In this case, the meaning of the proposition is that the force which results from all of them tends to the point *S*. But if any force acts perpetually in the direction of lines perpendicular to the described surface, this force will make the body deviate from the plane of its motion; but it will neither increase nor diminish the quantity of the described surface, and is therefore to be neglected in the composition of forces.

<meta>catchword: PROP.</meta>

<summary>This page outlines two corollaries concerning how forces deviate from a central point when the areas described by a body's motion are not proportional to time. It also includes a scholium explaining that while multiple forces can compose a single centripetal force, forces acting perpendicular to the plane of motion do not affect the area described and can be ignored in those calculations.</summary>

<keywords>centripetal force, in consequentia, in antecedentia, Scholium, resisting mediums, motion, radii, vector composition, orbital mechanics</keywords>

### PROPOSITION III. THEOREM III.

*Every body that, by a radius drawn to the center of another body however it moves, describes areas about that center proportional to the times, is urged by a force compounded of the centripetal force tending toward that other body and of all the accelerative force by which that other body is impelled.*

Let *L* represent the first body and *T* the other; and (by Cor. 6 of the laws), if both bodies are urged in the direction of parallel lines by a new force equal and contrary to that by which the second body *T* is urged, the first body *L* will continue to describe about the other body *T* the same areas as before. But the force by which that other body *T* was urged will now be destroyed by an equal and contrary force; and therefore (by Law 1), that other body *T*, now left to itself, will either rest or move uniformly forward in a straight line. The first body *L*, impelled by the difference of the forces—that is, by the force remaining—will continue to describe areas proportional to the times about the other body *T*. Therefore (by Theorem 2), the difference of the forces is directed to the other body *T* as its center. Q.E.D.

COROLLARY 1. Hence, if the one body *L*, by a radius drawn to the other body *T*, describes areas proportional to the times, and from the whole force by which the first body *L* is urged (whether that force is
simple,

<summary>Proposition III demonstrates that a body describing areas proportional to time around a moving center is acted upon by a combined force consisting of a centripetal force toward that center and the accelerative force of the center itself. This is proven by applying an equal and opposite force to both bodies to simplify the frame of reference.</summary>

<keywords>Proposition III, Theorem III, centripetal force, accelerative force, relative motion, areas proportional to times, Newton's Laws, orbital mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the corollaries to Proposition III regarding the calculation of forces between two moving bodies and introduces a Scholium on the relationship between area description and centers of force.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>SECT. II. of *Natural Philosophy*.</header>

simple, or, according to corollary 2 of the laws, compounded from several forces) we subtract (by the same corollary) that entire accelerative force by which the other body is urged; the entire remaining force by which the first body is urged will tend toward the other body *T* as its center.

Cor. 2. And, if these areas are nearly proportional to the times, the remaining force will nearly tend toward the other body *T*.

Cor. 3. And *vice versa*, if the remaining force tends nearly toward the other body *T*, those areas will be nearly proportional to the times.

Cor. 4. If the body *L*, by a radius drawn to the other body *T*, describes areas which, compared with the times, are very unequal; and that other body *T* is either at rest or moves uniformly forward in a straight line: the action of the centripetal force tending toward that other body *T* is either non-existent, or it is mixed and compounded with very powerful actions of other forces; and the whole force compounded of them all, if they are many, is directed toward another center (whether immobile or mobile). The same thing holds true when the other body is moved by any motion whatsoever, provided that the centripetal force considered is that which remains after subtracting the entire force acting upon that other body *T*.
