<meta>catchword: COR.</meta>

Why may we not be allowed in the following discourse to use the uniform description of areas as an indication of a center, about which all circular motion is performed in free spaces?

## PROPOSITION IV. THEOREM IV.

*The centripetal forces of bodies, which by uniform motions describe different circles, tend toward the centers of those same circles; and are related to one another as the squares of the arcs described in equal times divided by the radii of the circles.*

These forces tend toward the centers of the circles (by Proposition 2 and Corollary 2, Proposition 1) and are related to one another as the versed sines of the smallest arcs described in equal times (by Corollary 4, Proposition 1); that is, as the squares of those same arcs divided by the diameters of the circles (by Lemma 7). Therefore, since those arcs are proportional to arcs described in any equal times, and the diameters are proportional to the radii, the forces will be as the squares of any arcs described in the same time divided by the radii of the circles. *Q.E.D.*

**COROLLARY 1.** Therefore, since those arcs are as the velocities of the bodies, the centripetal forces are in a ratio compounded of the square <note>duplicate ratio</note> of the velocities directly, and of the simple ratio of the radii inversely.

<vocab>centripetal forces, uniform motion, centers, versed sines, diameters, radii, duplicate ratio, inverse ratio</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a Scholium on centers of motion and presents Proposition 4, proving that centripetal force in circular motion is proportional to the square of the velocity divided by the radius.</summary>
<keywords>centripetal force, circular motion, uniform motion, radii, velocities, Proposition IV, Theorem IV, versed sines</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the corollaries of Proposition 4, specifically detailing the mathematical relationships between periodic times, velocities, and radii in circular motion, and extending these principles to other similar figures.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>Sect. II. of *Natural Philosophy*.</header>
<sig>F</sig>
<meta>catchword: COR.</meta>

**COROLLARY 2.** And, since the periodic times are in a ratio compounded of the ratio of the radii directly and the ratio of the velocities inversely; the centripetal forces are in a ratio compounded of the ratio of the radii directly and the square <note>duplicate ratio</note> of the periodic times inversely.

**COROLLARY 3.** From this, if the periodic times are equal, and the velocities are therefore proportional to the radii, the centripetal forces will also be proportional to the radii; and vice versa.

**COROLLARY 4.** If both the periodic times and the velocities are in the square root <note>subduplicate ratio</note> of the radii, the centripetal forces will be equal to one another; and vice versa.

**COROLLARY 5.** If the periodic times are proportional to the radii, and therefore the velocities are equal, the centripetal forces will be inversely proportional to the radii; and vice versa.

**COROLLARY 6.** If the periodic times are in the three-halves power <note>sesquiplicate ratio</note> of the radii, and therefore the velocities are inversely in the square root <note>subduplicate ratio</note> of the radii, the centripetal forces will be inversely proportional to the square <note>duplicate ratio</note> of the radii; and vice versa.

**COROLLARY 7.** And universally, if the periodic time is proportional to any power *$R^n$* of the radius *$R$*, and therefore the velocity is inversely proportional to the power *$R^{n - 1}$* of the radius; the centripetal force will be inversely proportional to the power *$R^{2n - 1}$* of the radius: and vice versa.

**COROLLARY 8.** All these same principles hold concerning the times, the velocities, and forces by which bodies describe similar parts of any similar figures that have their centers in a similar position within those figures; as appears by applying the demonstration of the preceding cases to those. The application is easy by merely substituting the uniform description of areas in place of uniform motion, and using the distances of the bodies from the centers instead of the radii.

COROLLARY 9. From the same demonstration it likewise follows that the arc which a body, revolving uniformly in a circle by means of a given centripetal force, describes in any time, is a mean proportional between the diameter of the circle and the space which the same body, falling by the same given force, would descend through in the same given time.

### S C H O L I U M.

The case of the 6th corollary applies to the celestial bodies (as *Sir Christopher Wren*, *Dr. Hooke*, and *Dr. Halley* have each observed), and therefore in what follows, I intend to treat more at length those things which relate to centripetal force decreasing in the square <note>duplicate ratio</note> of the distances from the centers.

Moreover, by means of the preceding proposition and its corollaries, we may discover the proportion of a centripetal force to any other known force, such as that of gravity. For if a body, by means of its gravity, revolves in a circle concentric to the Earth, this gravity is the centripetal force of that body. But from the descent of heavy bodies, the time of one entire revolution, as well as the arc described in any given time, is given (by Corollary 9 of this proposition). And by such propositions, *Mr. Huygens*, in his excellent book *De Horologio Oscillatorio*, has compared the force of gravity with the centrifugal forces of revolving bodies.

The preceding proposition may be likewise demonstrated in this manner. In any circle, suppose a polygon of any number of sides is inscribed. And if a body, moved with a given velocity along the sides of the polygon, is reflected from the circle at the several angular points, the force with which at

at every reflection it strikes the circle, it will be in proportion to its velocity: and therefore the sum of the forces, in a given time, will be as that velocity and the number of reflections combined; that is, (if the type of polygon is given) as the length described in that given time, increased or diminished in the ratio of the same length to the radius of the circle; that is, as the square of that length divided by <note>applied to</note> the radius. And therefore if the polygon, by having its sides diminished *to infinity*, coincides with the circle, the force will be as the square of the arc described in a given time divided by the radius. This is the centrifugal force with which the body impels the circle; and to which the contrary force, wherewith the circle continually repels the body towards the center, is equal.

## PROPOSITION V. PROBLEM I.

> *Given the velocity with which a body describes a given figure in any locations, by means of forces directed to some common center; to find that center.*
> *Plate 3. Figure 1.*

Let the three straight lines *PT*, *TQV*, and *VR* touch the figure described in as many points *P*, *Q*, and *R*, and meet at *T* and *V*. On the tangents, erect the perpendiculars *PA*, *QB*, and *RC*, reciprocally proportional to the velocities of the body at the points *P*, *Q*, and *R*, from which the perpendiculars were raised; that is, so that *PA* may be to *QB* as the velocity at *Q* is to the velocity at *P*, and *QB* to *RC* as the velocity at *R* is to the velocity at *Q*. meeting at *D* and *E*: And the straight lines *TD* and *VE*, when extended, will meet at *S*, the required center.

For the perpendiculars dropped from the center *S* onto the tangents *PT* and *QT* are inversely proportional to the velocities of the bodies at points *P* and *Q* (by Corollary 1, Proposition 1). Therefore, by construction, they are directly proportional to the perpendiculars *AP* and *BQ*; that is, as the perpendiculars dropped from point *D* onto the tangents. From this, it is easy to infer that the points *S*, *D*, and *T* lie on a single straight line. By a similar argument, the points *S*, *E*, and *V* are also on a single straight line; and therefore the center *S* is at the point where the straight lines *TD* and *VE* meet. Q. E. D.

## PROPOSITION VI. THEOREM V.

*In a space free of resistance, if a body revolves in any orbit around an immovable center, and in the shortest time describes an arc just then beginning to form (<term>nascent</term>); and if the <term>versed sine</term> of that arc is drawn, bisecting the chord and extended so that it passes through the center of force: the centripetal force at the middle of the arc will be directly proportional to the versed sine and inversely proportional to the square of the time.*

For the <term>versed sine</term> in a given time is proportional to the force (by Corollary 4, Proposition 1). If the time is increased in any ratio, then because the arc will be increased in the same ratio, the versed sine will be increased by the square of that ratio (by Corollaries 2 and 3, Lemma 11); and therefore it is proportional to the force and the square of the time. Subtract from both sides the square of the time, and the force

**COR. 4.** Under the same conditions, the centripetal force is directly proportional to the square of the velocity and inversely proportional to that chord. This is because the velocity is inversely proportional to the perpendicular $SY$, according to Corollary 1 of Proposition 1.

**COR. 5.** Hence, if any curved figure $APQ$ is given, and a point $S$ is also given toward which a centripetal force is constantly directed, the law of centripetal force can be found. This law describes how the body $P$ is continually pulled away from a straight path and, being held within the perimeter of that figure, describes the figure through a constant revolution. Specifically, we must find by calculation either the quantity $\frac{SP^2 \times QT^2}{QR}$ or the quantity $SY^2 \times PV$, both of which are inversely proportional to this force. We shall provide examples of this in the following problems.

*Cᴏʀ. 1.* Hence, if the given point *S*, to which the centripetal force always tends, is placed on the circumference of the circle, as at *V*, the centripetal force will be inversely as the <term>quadrato-cube</term> (or fifth power) of the distance *SP*.

*Cᴏʀ. 2.* The force by which the body *P* in the circle *APTV* (Plate 3, Figure 4) revolves about the center of force *S* is to the force by which the same body *P* may revolve in the same circle and in the same periodic time about any other center of force *R*, as $RP^2 \times SP$ is to the cube of the straight line *SG*, which is drawn from the first center of force *S* parallel to the distance *PR* of the body from the second center of force *R*, meeting the tangent *PG* of the orbit at *G*. For by the construction of this proposition, the first force is to the second as $RP^2 \times PT^3$ is to $SP^2 \times PV^3$; that is, as $SP \times RP^2$ is to $\frac{SP^3 \times PV^3}{PT^3}$—or (because of the similar triangles *PSG, TPV*) to $SG^3$.

*Cᴏʀ. 3.* The force by which the body *P* in any orbit revolves about the center of force *S* is to the force by which the same body may revolve in the same orbit, and in the same periodic time, about any other center of force *R*, as the solid $SP \times RP^2$ (formed by the distance of the body from the first center of force *S* and the square of its distance from the second center of force *R*) is to the cube of the straight line *SG*, drawn from the first center of force *S* parallel to the distance *RP* of the body from the second center of force *R*, meeting the tangent *PG* of the orbit at *G*. For the force in this orbit at any point *P* is the same as in a circle of the same curvature.

# PROPOSITION VIII. PROBLEM III.

> *If a body moves in the semi-circumference* **P Q A**; *it is proposed to find the law of the centripetal force tending to a point* **S**, *so remote, that all the lines* **P S**, **R S** *drawn thereto, may be taken for parallels. Pl. 3. Fig. 5.*

From **C**, the center of the semi-circle, let the semi-diameter **C A** be drawn, cutting the parallels at right angles at **M** and **N**, and join **C P**. Because of the similar triangles **C P M**, **P Z T**, and **R Z Q**, we shall have **C P²** to **P M²** as **P R²** to **Q T²**; and, from the nature of the circle, **P R²** is equal to the rectangle **Q R** × (**R N + Q N**), or, as the points **P** and **Q** coincide, to the rectangle **Q R** × **2 P M**. Therefore **C P²** is to **P M²** as **Q R** × **2 P M** is to **Q T²**; and, $\frac{Q T^2}{Q R} = \frac{2 P M^3}{C P^2}$, and $\frac{Q T^2 \times S P^2}{Q R} = \frac{2 P M^3 \times S P^2}{C P^2}$. And therefore (by corollaries 1 and 5 of Proposition 6) the centripetal force is inversely as $\frac{2 P M^3 \times S P^2}{C P^2}$; that is, (neglecting the given ratio $\frac{2 S P^2}{C P^2}$) inversely as **P M³**. **Q. E. I.**

And the same result is likewise easily inferred from the preceding Proposition.

### S C H O L I U M.

And by a similar reasoning, a body will be moved in an ellipse, or even in a hyperbola or parabola, by a centripetal force which is inversely as the cube of the ordinate directed toward an infinitely remote center of force.

Sect. II. of Natural Philosophy.

### *The same, proven differently.*

The perpendicular $ST$ let fall upon the tangent, and the chord $PV$ of the circle concentrically cutting the spiral, are in given ratios to the height $SP$; and therefore $SP^3$ is proportional to $ST^2 \times PV$, that is (by Corollaries 3 and 5 of Proposition 6) inversely proportional to the centripetal force.

# LEMMA XII.

*All parallelograms circumscribed around any conjugate diameters of a given ellipse or hyperbola are equal to each other.*

This is demonstrated by the writers on conic sections.

# PROPOSITION X. PROBLEM V.

*If a body revolves in an ellipse: it is proposed to find the law of the centripetal force tending toward the center of the ellipse.*

Substitute *QR* for *Pv*, and (by Lemma 12) substitute *BC* $\times$ *CA* for *CD* $\times$ *PF*. Also (as the points *P* and *Q* coincide), substitute 2 *PC* for *vG*. Therefore (by Corollary 5, Proposition 6), the centripetal force is inversely proportional to $\frac{2 BC^2 \times CA^2}{PC}$. That is (because 2 *BC*² $\times$ *CA*² is a constant), the force is inversely proportional to $\frac{1}{PC}$, which is to say, it is directly proportional to the distance *PC*. ***Q. E. $\frac{2 DC^2}{PC} \times PF^2$ (by corollary 3, proposition 6) that is, (because $2 DC^2 \times PF^2$ is given) directly as $PC$. *Q. E. I.*

**COROLLARY 1.** And therefore the force is proportional to the distance of the body from the center of the ellipse; and *vice versa*, if the force is proportional to the distance, the body will move in an ellipse whose center coincides with the center of force, or perhaps in a circle into which the ellipse may degenerate.

**COROLLARY 2.** And the periodic times of the revolutions made in all ellipses whatsoever about the same center will be equal. For those times in similar ellipses will be equal (by corollary 3 and 8, proposition 4); but in ellipses that have their greater axis in common, they are to one another as the whole areas of the ellipses directly, and the parts of the areas described in the same time inversely; that is, as the lesser axes directly, and the velocities of the bodies in their principal vertices inversely; that is, as those lesser axes directly, and the ordinates to the same point of the common axis inversely; and therefore (because of the equality of the direct and inverse ratios) in the ratio of equality.

->**SCHOLIUM**<-

If the ellipse, by having its center removed to an infinite distance, degenerates into a parabola, the body will move in this parabola; and the force, now tending to a center infinitely remote, will become equable. Which is *Galileo’s* theorem. And if the parabolic section of the cone (by changing the inclination of the cutting plane to the cone) degenerates into a hyperbola, the body will move in the perimeter of this hyperbola, having its centripetal force changed into a centrifugal force. And in like manner as in the circle,

circle, or in the ellipse, if the forces are directed to the center of the figure located on the abscissa, those forces—by increasing or decreasing the ordinates in any given ratio, or even by changing the angle of inclination of the ordinates to the abscissa—are always increased or decreased in the ratio of the distances from the center, provided the periodic times remain equal. So also in all figures whatsoever: if the ordinates are increased or decreased in any given ratio, or if their inclination is changed in any way, while the periodic time remains the same, the forces directed to any center located on the abscissa are, along the several ordinates, increased or decreased in the ratio of the distances from the center.

### PROPOSITION XI. PROBLEM VI.

*If a body revolves in an ellipse: it is required to find the law of the centripetal force tending toward the focus of the ellipse. Plate 4, Figure 2.*

Let *S* be the focus of the ellipse. Draw *SP* cutting the diameter *DK* of the ellipse at *E*, and the ordinate *Qv* at *x*; and complete the parallelogram *QxPR*. It is evident that *EP* is equal to the <term>major semi-axis</term> *AC*: for by drawing *HI* from the other focus *H* of the ellipse parallel to *EC*, because *CS* and *CH* are equal, *ES* and *EI* will also be equal, so that *EP* is the half-sum of *PS* and *PI*; that is (because of the parallels *HI* and *PR*, and the equal angles *IPR* and *HPZ*), the half-sum of *PS* and *PH*, which together are equal to the whole axis 2*AC*. Draw *QT* perpendicular to *SP*, and letting *L* represent the principal <term>latus rectum</term> of the ellipse (or 2*BC*²/ *AC*), we shall have *L* times *QR* to
-> *L* times *Pv* <-

$L \cdot Pv$ as $QR$ to $Pv$, that is, as $PE$ or $AC$ to $PC$;
and $L \cdot Pv$ to $Gv \cdot P$ as $L$ to $Gv$; and $Gv \cdot P$ to
$Qv^2$ as $PC^2$ to $CD^2$; and (by corollary 2, lemma 7),
as the points $Q$ and $P$ coincide, $Qv^2$ is to $Qx^2$ in the
ratio of equality; and $Qx^2$ or $Qv^2$ is to $QT^2$ as
$EP^2$ to $PF^2$, that is, as $CA^2$ to $PF^2$ or (by lemma 12)
as $CD^2$ to $CB^2$. And compounding all those ratios together,
we shall have $L \cdot QR$ to $QT^2$ as $AC \cdot L \cdot PC^2 \cdot CD^2$ or $2 CB^2 \cdot PC^2 \cdot CD^2$ to $PC \cdot Gv \cdot CD^2 \cdot CB^2$, or as $2 PC$ to $Gv$. But as the points $Q$ and $P$ coincide, $2 PC$ and $Gv$ are equal. And
therefore the quantities $L \cdot QR$ and $QT^2$, proportional to these, will also be equal. Let those equals be multiplied by
   $\frac{SP^2}{QR}$,
and $L \cdot SP^2$ will become equal to
 $\frac{SP^2 \cdot QT^2}{QR}$.
And therefore (by corollaries 1 and 5 of proposition 6), the centripetal force is inversely as $L \cdot SP^2$, that is, inversely in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>the square</note> of the distance $SP$. *Q. E. I.* <note>Quod Erat Inveniendum: Which was to be found.</note>

->*The same otherwise.*<-

Since the force tending to the center of the ellipse, by which the body $P$ may revolve in that ellipse, is (by corollary 1, proposition 10) proportional to the distance $CP$ of the body from the center $C$ of the ellipse; let $CE$ be drawn parallel to the tangent $PR$ of the ellipse; and the force, by which the same body $P$ may revolve about any other point $S$ of the ellipse, if $CE$ and $PS$ intersect at $E$, will be as
   $\frac{PE^3}{SP^2}$
(by corollary 3, proposition 7); that is, if the point $S$ is the focus of the ellipse, and therefore $PE$ is a given value, the force is inversely as $SP^2$.
*Q. E. I.*

# SECTION III. *Of Natural Philosophy.*

With the same brevity with which we simplified the fifth problem to the parabola and hyperbola, we could do the same here. But because of the importance of the problem and its use in what follows, I shall confirm the other cases by specific demonstrations.

## PROPOSITION XII. PROBLEM VII.

*Suppose a body moves in a hyperbola: it is required to find the law of the centripetal force directed toward the focus of that figure.* Plate 5. Figure 1.

Let *CA* and *CB* be the semi-axes of the hyperbola; *PG* and *KD* other conjugate diameters; *PF* a line perpendicular to the diameter *KD*; and *Qv* an ordinate to the diameter *GP*. Draw *SP* cutting the diameter *DK* at *E*, and the ordinate *Qv* at *x*, and complete the parallelogram *QRPx*. It is evident that *EP* is equal to the semi-transverse axis *AC*; for, drawing *HI* from the other focus *H* of the hyperbola parallel to *EC*, since *CS* and *CH* are equal, *ES* and *EI* will also be equal. Thus, *EP* is half the difference of *PS* and *PI*; that is (because of the parallel lines *IH* and *PR*, and the equal angles *IPR* and *HPZ*), half the difference of *PS* and *PH*, the difference of which is equal to the entire axis 2 *AC*. Draw *QT* perpendicular to *SP*. And setting *L* for the principal <term>latus rectum</term> of the hyperbola (that is, for $\frac{2 BC^2}{AC}$), we shall have *L* $\times$ *QR* to *L* $\times$ *Pv* as *QR* is to *Pv*, or *Px* is to *Pv*; that is (because of the similar triangles *Pxv* and *PEC*), as *PE* is to *PC*, or *AC* is to *PC*. And

*L* $\times$ *Pv* will be to *Gv* $\times$ *Pv* as *L* is to *Gv*; and (by the properties of conic sections) the rectangle *GvP* is to *Qv*² as *PC*² is to *CD*²; and (by corollary 2, lemma 7), as the points *Q* and *P* coincide, the ratio of *Qv*² to *Qx*² becomes a ratio of equality; and *Qx*² or *Qv*² is to *QT*² as *EP*² is to *PF*², that is, as *CA*² is to *PF*², or (by lemma 12) as *CD*² is to *CB*²: and, compounding all those ratios together, we shall have *L* $\times$ *QR* to *QT*² as *AC* $\times$ *L* $\times$ *PC*² $\times$ *CD*² or 2 *CB*² $\times$ *PC*² $\times$ *CD*² is to *PC* $\times$ *Gv* $\times$ *CD*² $\times$ *CB*², or as 2 *PC* is to *Gv*.

But since the points *P* and *Q* coincide, 2 *PC* and *Gv* are equal. And therefore the quantities *L* $\times$ *QR* and *QT*², which are proportional to them, will also be equal.

Multiply those equals by $\frac{SP^2}{QR}$, and we shall have *L* $\times$ *SP*² equal to $\frac{SP^2 \times QT^2}{QR}$. And therefore (by corollaries 1 and 5, proposition 6), the centripetal force is inversely as *L* $\times$ *SP*², that is, inversely in the duplicate ratio (the square) of the distance *SP*. *Q. E. I.*

->*The same proven another way.*<-

Determine the force tending from the center *C* of the hyperbola. This will be proportional to the distance *CP*. But from there (by corollary 3, proposition 7), the force tending to the focus *S* will be as $\frac{PE^3}{SP^2}$; that is, because *PE* is a given value, inversely as *SP*². *Q. E. I.*

In the same way, it may be demonstrated that a body, having its centripetal force changed into a centrifugal force, will move in the conjugate hyperbola.

<header>SECT. III. *of Natural Philosophy.*</header>

## LEMMA XIII.

*The latus rectum of a parabola belonging to any vertex is four times the distance of that vertex from the focus of the figure.*

This is demonstrated by writers on conic sections.

## LEMMA XIV.

*The perpendicular dropped from the focus of a parabola to its tangent is a mean proportional between the distances from the focus to the point of contact and from the focus to the principal vertex of the figure.* Plate 5. Figure 2.

For, let *AP* be the parabola, *S* its focus, *A* its principal vertex, *P* the point of contact, *PO* an ordinate to the principal diameter, *PM* the tangent meeting the principal diameter at *M*, and *SN* the perpendicular from the focus to the tangent. Join *AN*; because of the equal lines *MS* and *SP*, *MN* and *NP*, and *MA* and *AO*, the straight lines *AN* and *OP* will be parallel. From this, the triangle *SAN* will be right-angled at *A* and similar to the equal triangles *SNM* and *SNP*. Therefore, *PS* is to *SN* as *SN* is to *SA*. *Q.E.D.*

COROLLARY I. *PS*² is to *SN*² as *PS* is to *SA*.

->*If a body moves in the perimeter of a parabola: it is required to find the law of the centripetal force tending to the focus of that figure. Plate 5. Figure 3.*<-

## PROPOSITION XIV. THEOREM VI.

*If several bodies revolve about one common center, and the centripetal force is inversely proportional to the square of the distance of the places from the center; I say that the principal <term>latera recta</term> of their orbits are in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>The ratio of the squares.</note> of the areas which the bodies, by radii drawn to the center, describe in the same time.*
Plate 6. Figure 1.

For (by Corollary 2, Proposition 13) the <term>latus rectum</term> *L* is equal to the quantity $\frac{QT^2}{QR}$ in its ultimate state when the points *P* and *Q* coincide. But the <term>lineola</term> *QR* in a given time is proportional to the generating centripetal force; that is (by supposition) inversely proportional to $SP^2$. And therefore $\frac{QT^2}{QR}$ is proportional to $QT^2 \times SP^2$; that is, the <term>latus rectum</term> *L* is in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the area $QT \times SP$. *Q. E. D.*

**Cor.** Hence the whole area of the ellipse, and the rectangle of the axes which is proportional to it, is in the ratio compounded of the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> <note>The ratio of the square roots.</note> of the <term>latus rectum</term> and the ratio of the periodic time. For the whole area is proportional to the area $QT \times SP$, described in a given time, multiplied by the periodic time.

<meta>catchword: PRO-</meta>

<summary>Proposition XIV demonstrates that for bodies orbiting a common center under an inverse-square force, the latus rectum of each orbit is proportional to the square of the area swept out in a given time. The corollary further relates the total area of the orbit and the periodic time to the latus rectum.</summary>

*If several bodies revolve about one common center, and the centripetal force is inversely proportional to the square of the distance of the positions from the center; I state that the principal <term>latera recta</term> of their orbits are in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>The ratio of the squares.</note> of the areas which the bodies, by radii drawn to the center, describe in the same time.*

### PROPOSITION XV. THEOREM VII.

> *The same things being assumed, I say that the periodic times in ellipses are in the sesquiplicate ratio of their greater axes.*

For the lesser axis is a mean proportional between the greater axis and the <term>latus rectum</term>; and therefore the rectangle formed by the axes is in the ratio compounded of the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the <term>latus rectum</term> and the <term>sesquiplicate ratio</term> of the greater axis. But this rectangle (by the corollary of proposition 14) is in a ratio compounded of the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the <term>latus rectum</term> and the ratio of the periodic time. Subtract from both sides the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the <term>latus rectum</term>, and there will remain the <term>sesquiplicate ratio</term> of the greater axis, equal to the ratio of the periodic time. Q. E. D.

**COR.** Therefore the periodic times in ellipses are the same as in circles whose diameters are equal to the greater axes of the ellipses.

**Corollary 1.** The principal <term>latera recta</term> are in a ratio compounded of the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the perpendiculars and the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the velocities.

For those perpendiculars are now the distances.

**Corollary 4.** The velocities of bodies revolving in <term>ellipses</term>, at their mean distances from the common focus, are the same as those of bodies revolving in circles at the same distances; that is (by Corollary 6, Proposition 4), inversely in the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the distances.

<meta>catchword: COR.</meta>

For the perpendiculars are now the lesser semi-axes, and these are as mean proportionals between the distances and the <term>latera recta</term>. Let this ratio inversely be compounded with the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> <note>the ratio of the square roots</note> of the <term>latera recta</term> directly, and we shall have the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the distances inversely.

**COR. 5.** In the same figure, or even in different figures, whose principal <term>latera recta</term> are equal, the velocity of a body is reciprocally as the perpendicular let fall from the focus on the tangent.

**COR. 6.** In a parabola, the velocity is reciprocally in the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the distance of the body from the focus of the figure; it is more variable in the ellipse, and less in the hyperbola, than according to this ratio. For (by Cor. 2, Lem. 14) the perpendicular let fall from the focus on the tangent of a parabola is in the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the distance. In the hyperbola the perpendicular is less variable, and in the ellipse it is more variable.

**COR. 7.** In a parabola, the velocity of a body at any distance from the focus is to the velocity of a body revolving in a circle at the same distance from the center, in the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the number 2 to 1; in the ellipse it is less, and in the hyperbola greater, than according to this ratio. For (by Cor. 2 of this proposition) the velocity at the vertex of a parabola is in this ratio, and (by Cor. 6 of this proposition and Proposition 4) the same proportion holds at all distances. And hence also in a parabola, the velocity is everywhere equal to the velocity of a body revolving in a circle at half the distance; in the ellipse it is less, and in the hyperbola greater.

**COR. 8.** The velocity of a body revolving in any conic section is to the velocity of a body revolving in a circle at a distance of half the principal <term>latus rectum</term> of the section, as that distance is to the perpendicular dropped from the focus to the tangent of the section. This is evident from Cor. 5.

**COR. 9.** Therefore, since (by Cor. 6, Prop. 4) the velocity of a body revolving in this circle is to the velocity of another body revolving in any other circle inversely as the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> <note>the square root</note> of the distances; it follows *ex æquo* <note>from equal proportions</note> that the velocity of a body revolving in a conic section will be to the velocity of a body revolving in a circle at the same distance, as a mean proportional between that common distance and half the principal <term>latus rectum</term> of the section is to the perpendicular dropped from the common focus upon the tangent of the section.

Suppose likewise that the straight line $pr$ touches the orbit $pq$ at $p$; and if you suppose perpendiculars dropped from $S$ onto those tangents, the principal <term>latus rectum</term> of the conic section (by Cor. 1, Prop. 16) will be to the principal <term>latus rectum</term> of that orbit in a ratio compounded of the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>the ratio of the squares</note> of the perpendiculars and the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the velocities; and it is therefore determined. Drop $SK$ perpendicular to $PH$, and erect the conjugate semi-axis $BC$; this done, we shall have $SP^2 - 2KPH + PH^2 = SH^2 = 4CH^2 = 4BH^2 - 4BC^2 = (SP + PH)^2 - L \times (SP + PH) = SP^2 + 2SPH + PH^2 - L \times (SP + PH)$. Adding to both sides $2KPH - SP^2 - PH^2 + L \times (SP + PH)$, we shall have $L \times (SP + PH) = 2SPH + 2KPH$, or $SP + PH$ is to $PH$ as $2SP + 2KP$ is to $L$. From this, $PH$ is determined both in length and position. That is, if the velocity of the body at $P$ is such that the latus rectum $L$ is less than $2SP + 2KP$, $PH$ will lie on the same side of the tangent $PR$ as the line $SP$; and therefore the figure will be an ellipse, which is also determined from the given foci $S, H,$ and the principal axis $SP + PH$. But if the velocity of the body is so great that the latus rectum $L$ becomes equal to $2PS + 2KP$, the length $PH$ will be infinite; and therefore the figure will be a parabola, which has its axis $SH$ parallel to the

**COROLLARY 1.** Hence in every conic section, given the principal vertex *D*, the <term>latus rectum</term> *L*, and the focus *S*, the other focus *H* is determined by setting the ratio of *DH* to *DS* as the latus rectum is to the difference between the latus rectum and 4*DS*. For the proportion *SP* + *PH* to *PH* as 2*PS* + 2*KP* to *L* becomes, in the case of this corollary, *DS* + *DH* to *DH* as 4*DS* to *L*, and by division *DS* to *DH* as 4*DS* — *L* to *L*.

**COROLLARY 2.** Therefore, if the velocity of a body at the principal vertex *D* is given, the orbit may be readily found; namely, by setting the ratio of its latus rectum to twice the distance *DS* as the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>the ratio of the squares</note> of this given velocity to the velocity of a body revolving in a circle at the distance *DS* (by Cor. 3, Prop. 16), and then setting *DH* to *DS* as the latus rectum is to the difference between the latus rectum and 4*DS*.

### S C H O L I U M.

If a body *P* (*Plate 6, Figure 4*) moves in the perimeter of any given <term>conic section</term> whose center is *C* by means of a centripetal force tending to any given point *R*, and the law of the centripetal force is required: Draw *CG* parallel to the radius *RP*, meeting the tangent *PG* of the orbit at *G*; and the required force (by Corollary 1 and the Scholium of Proposition 10, and Corollary 3 of Proposition 7) will be as $\frac{CG^3}{RP^2}$.

### PROPOSITION XVIII. PROBLEM X.

*Given a focus and the principal axes, to describe elliptic and hyperbolic trajectories which shall pass through given points and touch straight lines given by position. Plate 7. Figure 2.*

Let $S$ be the common focus of the figures; $AB$ the length of the principal axis of any trajectory; $P$ a point through which the trajectory should pass; and $TR$ a straight line which it should touch. Around the center $P$, with the radius $AB - SP$ if the orbit is an ellipse, or $AB + SP$ if the orbit is a hyperbola, describe the circle $HG$. From the tangent $TR$, drop the perpendicular $ST$ and extend it to $V$ so that $TV$ is equal to $ST$; and around $V$ as a center, with the radius $AB$, describe the circle $FH$. In this manner, whether two points $P, p$ are given, or two tangents $TR, tr$, or a point $P$ and a tangent $TR$, we are to describe two circles. Let $H$ be their common intersection, and from the foci $S$ and $H$ with the given axis, describe the trajectory. I say the task is complete. For (because $PH + SP$ in the ellipse, and $PH - SP$ in the hyperbola, is equal to the axis), the described trajectory will pass through the point $P$ and (by the preceding lemma) will touch the straight line $TR$. And by the same argument, it

### PROPOSITION XIX. PROBLEM XI.

*To describe a parabolic trajectory about a given focus, which shall pass through given points and touch straight lines given by position. Plate 7. Figure 3.*

Let *S* be the focus, *P* a point, and *T R* a tangent of the trajectory to be described. Around *P* as a center, with the distance *P S*, describe the circle *F G*. From the focus, drop a perpendicular *S T* onto the tangent, and extend it to *V*, so that *T V* is equal to *S T*. In the same manner, another circle *fg* is to be described if another point *p* is given; or another point *v* is to be found if another tangent *tr* is given; then draw the straight line *I F*, which shall touch the two circles *F G, fg* if two points *P, p* are given, or pass through the two points *V, v* if two tangents *T R, tr* are given, or touch the circle *F G* and pass through the point *V* if the point *P* and the tangent *T R* are given. Drop the perpendicular *S I* onto *F I*, and bisect it at *K*; and with the axis *S K* and principal vertex *K*, describe a parabola. I say the task is complete. For this parabola (because *S K* is equal to *I K*, and *S P* to *F P*) will pass through the point *P*; and (by corollary 3, lemma 14), because *S T* is equal to *T V* and *S T R* is a right angle, it will touch the straight line *T R*. **Q. E. F.**

### PROPOSITION XX. PROBLEM XII:

->*About a given focus, to describe any trajectory of a given species, which shall pass through given points and touch straight lines given by position.*<-

**CASE 1.** About the focus *S (Pl. 7. Fig. 4.)* it is required to describe a trajectory *ABC*, passing through two points *B, C*. Because the trajectory is given in species <note>of a specific type or eccentricity</note>, the ratio of the principal axis to the distance between the foci will be given. In that ratio, take *KB* to *BS* and *LC* to *CS*. Around the centers *B, C*, with the distances *BK, CL*, describe two circles, and on the straight line *KL* that touches the same at *K* and *L*, drop the perpendicular *SG*. Divide this perpendicular at *A* and *a*, so that *GA* may be to *AS*, and *Ga* to *aS*, as *KB* is to *BS*; and with the axis *Aa* and vertices *A, a*, describe a trajectory. I say the task is complete. For let *H* be the other focus of the described figure; and since *GA* is to *AS* as *Ga* is to *aS*, then by division [of ratios] we shall have *Ga — GA* (or *Aa*) to *aS — AS* (or *SH*) in the same ratio, and therefore in the ratio which the principal axis of the figure to be described has to the distance of its foci. Therefore, the described figure is of the same species as the figure which was to be described. And since *KB* to *BS* and *LC* to *CS* are in the same ratio, this figure will pass through the points *B, C*, as is evident from the properties of conic sections.

**CASE 2.** About the focus *S (Pl. 7. Fig. 5.)* it is required to describe a trajectory which shall somewhere touch two straight lines *TR, tr*. From the focus onto those tangents drop the perpendiculars *ST, St,*

Therefore, $VH$ will be to $SH$ as $VX$ is to $XH$, and therefore as $VK$ is to $KS$. And since $VH$ and $vH$ are equal to the principal axis, and $VS$ and $vS$ are perpendicularly bisected by the straight lines $TR$ and $tr$, it is evident (by Lemma 15) that those straight lines are tangent to the described trajectory. ***Q. > *From three given points, to draw to a fourth point (which is not given) three straight lines whose differences shall be either given or none at all.*

**C A S E I.** Let the given points be *A*, *B*, and *C* (*Plate 8, Figure 1*), and *Z* be the fourth point which we are to find. **CASE 3.** If all three are equal, the point *Z* will be placed in the center of a circle that passes through the points *A, B, and C. *Q.E.I.*

## S C H O L I U M.

When the trajectory is a hyperbola, I do not include its conjugate hyperbola under the name of this trajectory. For a body moving with a continuous motion can never pass out of one hyperbola into its conjugate hyperbola.

The case when three points are given is more readily solved in this way. Let *B*, *C*, and *D* (*Plate 8, Figure 3*) be the given points. Join *BC* and *CD*, and extend them to *E* and *F*, so that *EB* is to *EC* as *SB* is to *SC*, and *FC* is to *FD* as *SC* is to *SD*. On the line *EF* drawn and extended, drop the perpendiculars *SG* and *BH*; and in *GS* extended indefinitely, take *GA* to *AS*, and *Ga* to *aS*, in the same ratio as *HB* is to *BS*. Then *A* will be the vertex, and *Aa* the principal axis of the trajectory: which, according to whether *GA* is greater than, equal to, or less than *AS*, will be either an ellipse, a para-

For if on *GF*, the perpendiculars *CI* and *DK* are dropped, *IC* will be to *HB* as *EC* is to *EB*; that is, as *SC* is to *SB*; and by permutation, *IC* is to *SC* as *HB* is to *SB*, or as *GA* is to *SA*. And, by the same argument, we may prove that *KD* is to *SD* in the same ratio. Therefore, the points *B*, *C*, and *D* lie in a conic section described about the focus *S*, in such a manner that all the right lines drawn from the focus *S* to the several points of the section, and the perpendiculars dropped from the same points onto the right line *GF*, are in that given ratio.

That excellent geometer *Mr. De la Hire* has solved this problem in much the same way in his conics, book 8, proposition 25.

->*How the orbits are to be found when neither focus is given.*<-

## LEMMA XVII.

*If from any point* **P** *of a given conic section, to the four extended sides* **AB**, **CD**, **AC**, **DB** *of any trapezium* **ABDC** *inscribed in that section, as many straight lines* **PQ**, **PR**, **PS**, **PT** *are drawn at given angles, each line to each side; the rectangle* **PQ** x **PR** *of those on the opposite sides* **AB**, **CD**, *will be to the rectangle* **PS** x **PT** *of those on the other two opposite sides* **AC**, **BD**, *in a given ratio.*

**CASE I.** Let us suppose first that the lines drawn to one pair of opposite sides are parallel to either of the other sides; as *PQ* and *PR* (*Pl. 8. Fig. 4*) to the side *AC*, and *PS* and *PT* to the side *AB*. And further, that one pair of the opposite sides, such as *AC* and *BD*, are parallel to each other; then the

Extend *PO* to *K*, so that *OK* is equal to *PO*; then *OK* will be an ordinate on the other side of that diameter. Since, therefore, the points *A*, *B*, *P*, and *K* are located on the conic section, and *PK* intersects *AB* at a given angle, the rectangle *PQK* (by Propositions 17, 19, 21, and 23 of Book 3 of *Apollonius*'s Conics) will have a given ratio to the rectangle *AQB*. But *QK* and *PR* are equal, being the differences of the equal lines *OK*, *OP*, and *OQ*, *OR*; from which the rectangles *PQK* and *PQ* x *PR* are equal; and therefore the rectangle *PQ* x *PR* is to the rectangle *AQB*—that is, to the rectangle *PS* x *PT*—in a given ratio. *Q.E.D.*

**CASE 2.** Let us next suppose that the opposite sides *AC* and *BD* (*Plate 8, Figure 5*) of the trapezium are not parallel. Therefore, by multiplying the antecedents by the antecedents and the consequents by the consequents, as the rectangle *PQ* x *Rr* is to the rectangle *PS* x *Tt*, so will the rectangle *NDM* be to the rectangle *ANB*; and (by Case 1) so is the rectangle *PQ* x *Pr* to the rectangle *PS* x *Pt*; and by division, so is the rectangle *PQ* x *PR* to the rectangle *PS* x *PT*. *Q.E.D.*

## LEMMA XVIII.

*Assuming the same conditions, if the rectangle PQ x PR (formed by the lines drawn to the two opposite sides of the trapezium) is to the rectangle PS x PT (formed by those drawn to the other two sides) in a given ratio, then the point P, from which those lines are drawn, will be located on a conic section described around the trapezium. (Plate 8, Figure 7.)*

Imagine a conic section described passing through the points A, B, C, D, and any one of the infinite number of points P, such as *p*. I say that the point P will always be located in this section. If you deny this, join AP, cutting this conic section at some other point than P, if possible, such as at *b*. Therefore, if from those points *p* and *b*, at the given angles to the sides of the trapezium, we draw the straight lines *pq*, *pr*, *ps*, *pt*, and *bk*, *bn*, *bf*, *bd*, we shall have

<summary>This page concludes Lemma XVII by proving that the ratio of products of lines drawn to opposite sides of a trapezium is constant even when not parallel, and begins Lemma XVIII to prove that such points must lie on a conic section.</summary>
<keywords>Lemma XVII, Lemma XVIII, trapezium, conic section, geometric ratios, rectangles, point P, mathematical proof</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Lemma XVIII, demonstrating that a point P, which maintains a given ratio between the products of lines drawn to the sides of a trapezium, must lie on a specific conic section. It also introduces a corollary regarding tangency.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>SECTION V. of Natural Philosophy.</header>

as *bk* × *bn* is to *bf* × *bd*, so (by Lemma 17) *pq* × *pr* is to *ps* × *pt*; and so (by assumption) *PQ* × *PR* is to *PS* × *PT*. And because of the similar trapezia *bkAf* and *PQAS*, as *bk* is to *bf*, so *PQ* is to *PS*. Therefore, by dividing the terms of the preceding proportion by the corresponding terms of this one, we shall have *bn* to *bd* as *PR* is to *PT*. And therefore the equiangular trapezia *Dnbd* and *DRPT* are similar, and consequently their diagonals *Db* and *DP* coincide. Therefore, *b* falls at the intersection of the straight lines *AP* and *DP*, and consequently coincides with the point *P*. And therefore the point *P*, wherever it is taken, falls on the designated conic section. *Q.E.D.*

**COROLLARY.** Hence, if three straight lines *PQ*, *PR*, and *PS* are drawn from a common point *P* to three other straight lines given in position, *AB*, *CD*, and *AC*, each respectively at given angles, and the product *PQ* × *PR* of any two of the lines drawn is to the square *PS*² of the third in a given ratio: the point *P*, from which the straight lines are drawn, will be located on a conic section that touches the lines *AB* and *CD* at *A* and *C*; and the converse is also true. For, while the position of the three straight lines *AB*, *CD*, and *AC* remains the same, let the line *BD* approach and coincide with the line *AC*; then let the line *PT* likewise come to coincide with the line *PS*; then the product *PS* × *PT* will become *PS*², and the straight lines *AB* and *CD*, which previously intersected the curve at points *A* and *B*, and *C* and *D*, can no longer intersect, but only touch the curve at those coinciding points.

<meta>catchword: SCHO-</meta>

<keywords>conic section, trapezium, Lemma XVIII, geometric ratio, tangency, straight lines, mathematical proof, Isaac Newton</keywords>

### S C H O L I U M.

In this lemma, the name <term>conic section</term> is to be understood in a broad sense, encompassing the rectilinear section through the vertex of the cone as well as the circular section parallel to the base. For if the point *p* happens to lie on a straight line by which the points *A* and *D* or *C* and *B* are joined, the conic section will be changed into two straight lines: one of which is that straight line upon which the point *p* falls, and the other is a straight line that joins the other two of the four points. If the two opposite angles of the <term>trapezium</term> taken together are equal to two right angles, and if the four lines *PQ*, *PR*, *PS*, and *PT* are drawn to its sides at right angles, or any other equal angles, and the product *PQ* $\times$ *PR* of two of the lines drawn, *PQ* and *PR*, is equal to the product *PS* $\times$ *PT* of the other two, *PS* and *PT*, the conic section will become a circle. And the same thing will happen if the four lines are drawn at any angles, and the product *PQ* $\times$ *PR* of one pair of the lines drawn is to the product *PS* $\times$ *PT* of the other pair, as the product of the sines of the angles *S* and *T* (in which the last two lines *PS* and *PT* are drawn) is to the product of the sines of the angles *Q* and *R* (in which the first two *PQ* and *PR* are drawn). In all other cases, the <term>locus</term> of the point *P* will be one of the three figures which commonly pass by the name of the conic sections. But instead of the trapezium *ABCD*, we may substitute a quadrilateral figure whose two opposite sides cross one another like diagonals. And one or two of the four points *A*, *B*, *C*, and *D* may be supposed to be removed

<summary>This scholium defines "conic section" broadly to include circles and degenerate straight lines, explaining how specific geometric conditions and ratios of line products determine the shape of the resulting locus.</summary>

## L E M M A XIX.

*To find a point P (Plate 8, Figure 8) from which, if four straight lines PQ, PR, PS, PT are drawn to four other straight lines AB, CD, AC, BD given by position—each to each, at given angles—the product PQ $\times$ PR of any two of the lines drawn shall be to the product PS $\times$ PT of the other two in a given ratio.*

Suppose the lines *AB* and *CD*, to which the two straight lines *PQ* and *PR* (forming one of the products) are drawn, meet two other lines, given by position, at points *A, B, C,* and *D*. From one of those points, such as *A*, draw any straight line *AH* in which you wish to find the point *P*. Let this line intersect the opposite lines *BD* and *CD* at *H* and *I*. Because all the angles of the figure are given, the ratio of *PQ* to *PA*, and *PA* to *PS*, and therefore the ratio of *PQ* to *PS*, will also be given. Subtracting this ratio from the given ratio of the product *PQ* $\times$ *PR* to the product *PS* $\times$ *PT*, the ratio of *PR* to *PT* will be given. By adding the given ratios of *PI* to *PR* and *PT* to *PH*, the ratio of *PI* to *PH*, and therefore the point *P*, will be given. *Q.E.I.* <note>Quod Erat Invenienbum: Which was to be found.</note>

**COROLLARY 1.** Hence, a tangent may also be drawn to any point *D* of the <term>locus</term> of all the points *P*. For

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page describes how conic sections behave when points move to infinity and provides a geometric proof for finding a point P based on a constant ratio between the products of lines drawn to the sides of a quadrilateral.</summary>
<keywords>Lemma XIX, conic section, locus, ratio, geometric construction, tangents, Isaac Newton, Principia</keywords>

<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

<margin>

p
</margin>
the chord $PD$, where the points $P$ and $D$ meet—that is, where $AH$ is drawn through the point $D$—becomes a tangent. In this case, the ultimate ratio of the vanishing lines $IP$ and $PH$ will be found as above. Therefore, draw $CF$ parallel to $AD$, meeting $BD$ at $F$, and divide it at $E$ in the same ultimate ratio; then $DE$ will be the tangent, because $CF$ and the vanishing line $IH$ are parallel and similarly divided at $E$ and $P$.

**COROLLARY 2.** Hence, the locus of all points $P$ may also be determined. Through any of the points $A, B, C, D$, such as $A$ (Plate 9, Figure 1), draw $AE$ touching the locus, and through any other point $B$ parallel to the tangent, draw $BF$ meeting the locus at $F$; and find the point $F$ by this lemma. Bisect $BF$ at $G$, and by drawing the indefinite line $AG$, this will be the position of the diameter to which $BG$ and $FG$ are ordinates. Let this line $AG$ meet the locus at $H$, and $AH$ will be its diameter or <term>latus transversum</term>, to which the <term>latus rectum</term> will be as $BG^2$ to the product $AG \times GH$. If $AG$ nowhere meets the locus, the line $AH$ being infinite, the locus will be a parabola; and its <term>latus rectum</term> corresponding to the diameter $AG$ will be $\frac{BG^2}{AG}$. But if it does meet it anywhere, the locus will be an hyperbola when the points $A$ and $H$ are placed on the same side of point $G$, and an ellipsis <note>An ellipse.</note> if the point $G$ falls between the points $A$ and $H$; unless perhaps the angle $AGB$ is a right angle and, at the same time, $BG^2$ is equal to the rectangle $AG \times GH$, in which case the locus will be a circle.

And so in this corollary, we have given a solution to that famous problem of the ancients concerning four lines, begun by *Euclid* and carried on by *Apollonius*; and this is not an analytical calculus, but a geometrical composition such as the ancients required.

## LEMMA XX.

*If the two opposite vertices* A *and* P (*Pl.* 9. *Fig.* 2.) *of any parallelogram* ASPQ *touch any conic section at the points* A *and* P; *and the sides* AQ *and* AS *of one of those angles, extended indefinitely, meet the same conic section at* B *and* C; *and from the points of intersection* B *and* C *to any fifth point* D *of the conic section, two straight lines* BD *and* CD *are drawn meeting the two other sides* PS *and* PQ *of the parallelogram, extended indefinitely, at* T *and* R; *the segments* PR *and* PT, *cut off from the sides, will always have a constant ratio to each other. And conversely, if those segments cut off have a constant ratio to each other, the locus of the point* D *will be a conic section passing through the four points* A, B, C, *and* P.

**Case 1.** Connect *BP* and *CP*, and from the point *D* draw the two straight lines *DG* and *DE*, of which the first, *DG*, shall be parallel to *AB* and meet *PB, PQ,* and *CA* at *H, I,* and *G*; and the other, *DE*, shall be parallel to *AC* and meet *PC, PS,* and *AB* at *F, K,* and *E*. Then (by *Lem.* 17) the rectangle *DE* × *DF* will be to the rectangle *DG* × *DH* in a given ratio. But *PQ* is to *DE* (or *IQ*) as *PB* is to *HB*, and consequently as *PT* is to *DH*; and by permutation, *PQ*

**CASE 2.** But if *PR* and *PT* are supposed to be in a given ratio to each other, then by reasoning backward in a similar way, it will follow that the rectangle *DE* × *DF* is to the rectangle *DG* × *DH* in a given ratio; and so the point *D* (by Lemma 18) will lie in a conic section passing through the points *A, B, C, P* as its locus. *Q. For suppose the point *D* coincides with the point *B*, so that the chord *BD* vanishes, *BT* shall become a tangent, and *CD* and *BT* will coincide with *CB* and *Bt*.

**COROLLARY 2.** And vice versa, if *Bt* is a tangent, and the lines *BD* and *CD* meet in any point *D* of a conic section, *PR* will be to *PT* as *Pr* is to *Pt*. And on the contrary, if *PR* is to *PT* as *Pr* is to *Pt*, then *BD* and *CD* will meet in some point *D* of a conic section.

**COROLLARY 3.** One conic section cannot intersect another conic section in more than four points. For, if it were possible, let two conic sections pass through the five points *A, B, C, P, O*; and let the straight line *BD* cut them at the points *D* and *d*, and the straight line *Cd* cut the straight line *PQ* at *q*. Therefore *PR* is to *PT* as *Pq* is to *PT*:

### Lemma XXI.

*If two movable and indefinite straight lines $BM, CM$ are drawn through given points $B, C$ as poles, and by their point of intersection $M$ they describe a third straight line $MN$ given by position; and two other indefinite straight lines $BD, CD$ are drawn, making given angles $MBD, MCD$ with the first two lines at those given points $B, C$: I say that those two straight lines $BD, CD$ will, by their point of intersection $D$, describe a conic section passing through the points $B$ and $C$. And conversely, if the straight lines $BD, CD$ describe a conic section passing through the given points $B, C, A$ by their point of intersection $D$, and the angle $DBM$ is always equal to the given angle $ABC$, just as the angle $DCM$ is always equal to the given angle $ACB$: the point $M$ will lie on a straight line given by position, as its locus. Plate 9, Figure 3.*

For in the straight line $MN$ let a point $N$ be given, and when the movable point $M$ falls on the immovable point $N$, let the movable point $D$ fall on an immovable point $P$. Join $CN, BN, CP,$

## PROPOSITION XXII. PROBLEM XIV.

->*To describe a trajectory that shall pass through five given points. Plate 9, Figure 5.*<-

Let the five given points be *A, B, C, P, D*. From any one of them, such as *A*, to any other two, such as *B* and *C* (which may be called the poles), draw the straight lines *AB* and *AC*, and parallel to those, the lines *TPS* and *PRQ* through the fourth point *P*. Then from the two poles *B* and *C*, draw through the fifth point *D* two indefinite lines *BDT* and *CRD*, meeting with the last drawn lines *TPS* and *PRQ* (the former with the former, and the latter with the latter) at *T* and *R*. Then, drawing the straight line *tr* parallel to *TR*, cutting off from the straight lines *PT* and *PR* any segments *Pt* and *Pr* proportional to *PT* and *PR*; and if through their endpoints *t* and *r*, and the poles *B* and *C*, the straight lines *Bt* and *Cr* are drawn, meeting at *d*, that point *d* will be placed on the trajectory required. For (by Lemma 20) that point *d* is placed in a conic section passing through the four points *A, B, C, P*; and as the lines *Rr* and *Tt* vanish, the point *d* comes to coincide with the point *D*. Therefore the conic section passes through the five points *A, B, C, P, D. Q. E. D.*

### *The same otherwise.* Pl. 9. Fig. 6.

From the given points, join any three, such as *A, B, and C*; and using two of them, *B* and *C*, as poles, revolve the angles *ABC* and *ACB* of a given magnitude. Apply the legs *BA* and *CA* first to point *D*, then to point *P*, and mark the points *M* and *N* where the other legs, *BL* and *CL*, intersect each other in both cases. Draw the indefinite straight line *MN*, and let those movable angles revolve about their poles *B* and *C* in such a way that the intersection (now designated as *m*) of the legs *BL* and *CL* (or *BM* and *CM*) always falls on that indefinite straight line *MN*. Then the intersection (now designated as *d*) of the legs *BA* and *CA* (or *BD* and *CD*) will describe the required trajectory *PAD db*. For (by Lemma 21), the point *d* will be located on a conic section passing through the points *B* and *C*; and when the point *m* coincides with the points *L, M*, and *N*, the point *d* will (by construction) coincide with the points *A, D*, and *P*. Therefore, a conic section will be described that passes through the five points *A, B, C, P*, and *D*. *Q. E. F.*

COROLLARY 1. From this, a straight line can be easily drawn that is tangent to the trajectory at any given point *B*. Let the point *d* coincide with the point *B*, and the straight line *Bd* will become the required tangent.

COROLLARY 2. From this also, the centers, diameters, and <term>latera recta</term> of the trajectories may be found, as in Corollary 2 of Lemma 19.

### S C H O L I U M.

The first of these constructions (*Fig. 5.*) will become somewhat simpler by joining *BP*, and in that line (extended if necessary), taking *Bp* to *BP* in the same ratio as *PR* is to *PT*; and through *p* drawing the indefinite straight line *pe* parallel to *SPT*; and in that line *pe* always taking *pe* equal to *Pr*; and drawing the straight lines *Be* and *Cr* to meet at *d*. For since the ratios of *Pr* to *Pt*, *PR* to *PT*, *pB* to *PB*, and *pe* to *Pt* are all the same, *pe* and *Pr* will always be equal. In this manner the points of the trajectory are most easily found, unless you would rather describe the curve mechanically as in the second construction.

## PROPOSITION XXIII. PROBLEM XV.

> *To describe a trajectory that shall pass through four given points, and touch a straight line given in position. Pl. 10. Fig. 1.*

**Case 1.** Suppose that *HB* is the given tangent, *B* the point of contact, and *C, D, and P* the three other given points. Join *BC*, and by drawing *PS* parallel to *BH* and *PQ* parallel to *BC*, complete the parallelogram *BSPQ*. Draw *BD* intersecting *SP* at *T*, and *CD* intersecting *PQ* at *R*. Lastly, by drawing any line *tr* parallel to *TR*, cutting off from *PQ* and *PS* the segments *Pr* and *Pt* proportional to *PR* and *PT* respectively, and drawing *Cr* and *Bt*, their point of intersection *d* will (by Lemma 20) always fall on the trajectory to be described.

<summary>This page provides a simplified method for constructing a trajectory through five points and introduces Case 1 of Proposition 23, which describes how to find a trajectory passing through four points with one given tangent.</summary>
<keywords>Scholium, Proposition 23, trajectory, tangent, parallelogram, Lemma 20, geometric construction, ratios</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alternative construction for Proposition 23, Case 1, and begins the explanation for Case 2, which involves finding a point of contact on a tangent line given four other points.</meta>

### *The same construction performed differently. Pl. 10. Fig. 2.*

Let the angle $CBH$ of a given magnitude revolve about the pole $B$, and let the straight-line radius $DC$ (extended in both directions) revolve about the pole $C$. Mark the points $M$ and $N$ where the side $BC$ of the angle intersects that radius when $BH$, the other side of the angle, meets the same radius at points $P$ and $D$. Then, drawing the infinite line $MN$, let that radius $CP$ or $CD$ and the side $BC$ of the angle continually meet on this line; the point where the other side $BH$ meets the radius will trace the required trajectory.

For if, in the constructions of the previous problem, point $A$ coincides with point $B$, the lines $CA$ and $CB$ will coincide, and line $AB$ in its final position will become the tangent $BH$. Therefore, the constructions provided there will be the same as the constructions described here. Thus, the intersection of the side $BH$ with the radius will describe a conic section passing through the points $C, D,$ and $P$, and touching the line $BH$ at point $B$. *Q. E. F.*

**CASE 2.** Suppose the four points $B, C, D,$ and $P$ (*Pl. 10. Fig. 3.*) are given, being situated outside the tangent $HI$. ## LEMMA XXII.

->*To transform figures into other figures of the same kind. Plate 10, Figure 5.*<-

Suppose that any figure *HGI* is to be transformed. Draw, as you please, two parallel lines *AO* and *BL*, intersecting any third line *AB* given by position, at *A* and *B*. From any point *G* of the figure, draw any straight line *GD*, parallel to *OA*, until it meets the straight line *AB*. Then from any given point *O* in the line *OA*, draw to the point *D* the straight line *OD*, meeting *BL* at *d*; and from the point of intersection, construct the straight line *dg* making any given angle with the straight line *BL*, and having the same ratio to *Od* as *DG* has to *OD*. Then *g* will be the point in the new figure *hgi* corresponding to the point *G*. In the same way, the various points of the first figure will yield as many corresponding points in the new figure. If we, therefore, imagine the point *G* to be carried along by a continuous motion through all the points of the first figure, the point *g* will likewise be carried along by a continuous motion through all the points of the new figure, and describe it. For the sake of distinction, let us call *DG* the first <term>ordinate</term>, *dg* the new <term>ordinate</term>, *AD* the first <term>abscissa</term>, *ad* the new <term>abscissa</term>; *O* the <term>pole</term>, *OD* the <term>abscinding radius</term>, *OA* the first ordinate radius, and *Oa* (by which the parallelo-

I say, then, that if the point *G* is placed on a straight line given in position, the point *g* will also be placed on a straight line given in position. If the point *G* is placed on a conic section, the point *g* will likewise be placed on a conic section. And here I understand the circle as one of the conic sections. But further, if the point *G* is placed on a line of the third analytical order, the point *g* will also be placed on a line of the third order, and so on for curve lines of higher orders. The two lines on which the points *G* and *g* are placed will always be of the same analytical order. For as *ad* is to *OA*, so *od* is to *OD*, *dg* is to *DG*, and *AB* is to *AD*; and therefore *AD* is equal to

->$\frac{OA \times AB}{ad}$ and *DG* is equal to<-

->$\frac{OA \times dg}{ad}$.<-

Now, if the point *G* is placed on a straight line, and therefore, in any equation by which the relation between the abscissa *AD* and the ordinate *DG* is expressed, those indeterminate lines *AD* and *DG* rise no higher than to one dimension, then by writing this equation

->$\frac{OA \times AB}{ad}$ in place of *AD*, and<-

->$\frac{OA \times dg}{ad}$ in place of *DG*,<-

a new equation will be produced, in which the new abscissa *ad* and new ordinate *dg* rise only to one dimension; and which therefore must represent a straight line. But if *AD* and *DG* (or either of them) had risen to two dimensions in the first equation, *ad* and *dg* would likewise have risen to two dimensions in the second equation. And so on for three or more dimensions. The indeter-

<summary>Newton demonstrates that the geometric transformation defined in Lemma XXII preserves the analytical order of a figure, meaning straight lines remain straight and conic sections remain conic sections. He proves this by showing that substituting the transformed coordinates into the equation of a curve does not change the highest degree (dimension) of the variables.</summary>

mined lines *ad*, *dg* in the second equation, and *AD*, *DG* in the first will always rise to the same number of dimensions; and therefore the lines in which the points *G*, *g* are placed are of the same analytical order.

I say further, that if any straight line touches the curve line in the first figure, the same straight line transferred the same way with the curve into the new figure will touch that curve line in the new figure, and *vice versa*. For if any two points of the curve in the first figure are supposed to approach one another until they coincide, the same points transferred will approach one another until they coincide in the new figure; and therefore the straight lines with which those points are joined will together become tangents of the curves in both figures. I could have given demonstrations of these assertions in a more geometrical form, but I strive to be brief.

Therefore, if one rectilinear figure is to be transformed into another, we need only transfer the intersections of the straight lines of which the first figure consists, and through the transferred intersections draw straight lines in the new figure. But if a curvilinear figure is to be transformed, we must transfer the points, the tangents, and other straight lines by means of which the curve line is defined. This lemma is useful in the solution of the more difficult problems. For by this means, we may transform the proposed figures, if they are intricate, into others that are simpler. Thus, any straight lines converging to a point are transformed into parallels by taking for the first ordinate radius any straight line that passes through the point of concourse of the converging lines; and that is because their point of concourse is by this means made 

<summary>Newton proves that his transformation method preserves the analytical order and tangency of curves, allowing complex figures to be simplified for easier calculation. He specifically notes that converging lines can be transformed into parallel lines using this technique.</summary>

to go off to infinity, and parallel lines are those that tend toward a point infinitely remote. After the problem is solved in the new figure, if we transform the new figure back into the first one by inverse operations, we will have the required solution.

This lemma is also useful in solving solid problems. For whenever two conic sections intersect to solve a problem, any one of them—if it is a hyperbola or a parabola—may be transformed into an ellipse, and then this ellipse may be easily changed into a circle. Similarly, a straight line and a conic section in the construction of plane problems may be transformed into a straight line and a circle.

## * PROPOSITION XXVI. PROBLEM XVIII.

->*To describe a <term>trajectory</term> that shall pass*<-
->*through a given point, and touch four*<-

## L E M M A XXIII.

*If two straight lines such as* **AC** *and* **BD** *are given in position, and ending at given points* **A** *and* **B**, *are in a given ratio to one another, and the straight line* **CD**, *by which the variable points* **C** *and* **D** *are joined, is divided at* **K** *in a given ratio; I say that the point* **K** *will be located on a straight line given in position. Plate 11. Figure 2.*

For let the straight lines *AC* and *BD* meet at *E*, and in *BE* take *BG* to *AE* in the same ratio as *BD* is to *AC*, and let *FD* be always equal to the given line *EG*. By construction, *EC* will be to *GD* (that is, to *EF*) as *AC* is to *BD*, and therefore in a given ratio; consequently, the triangle *EFC* will be given in kind <note>meaning its shape and angles are fixed</note>. Let *CF* be divided at *L* so that *CL* is to *CF* in the ratio of *CK* to *CD*; and because that is a given ratio, the triangle *EFL* will be given in kind, and therefore the point *L* will be located on the straight line *EL* given in position. Join *LK*, and the triangles *CLK* and *CFD* will be similar; and because *FD* is a given line, and *LK* is to *FD* in a given ratio, *LK* will also be given. To this length let *EH* be taken as an equal, and *ELKH* will always be a parallelogram. Therefore, the point *K* is always located on the side *HK* (given in position) of that parallelogram. *Q. E. D.*

<meta>catchword: COR.</meta>

<summary>Lemma 23 provides a geometric proof that a point dividing a line connecting two variable points on fixed lines in a constant ratio will itself trace a straight line. The proof utilizes properties of similar triangles and parallelograms to establish the fixed position of the resulting line.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, Lemma XXIII, fixed ratio, straight lines, similar triangles, parallelogram, locus, mathematical proof</keywords>

## LEMMA XXIV.

*If three straight lines, two of which are parallel and fixed in position, touch any conic section, I say that the semidiameter of the section which is parallel to those two is a mean proportional between the segments of those two that are intercepted between the points of contact and the third tangent. Plate 11. Figure 3.*

Let *AF* and *GB* be the two parallels touching the conic section *ADB* at *A* and *B*; let *EF* be the third straight line touching the conic section at *I*, and meeting the two former tangents at *F* and *G*; and let *CD* be the semidiameter of the figure parallel to those tangents. I say that *AF*, *CD*, and *BG* are in continuous proportion.

For if the conjugate diameters *AB* and *DM* meet the tangent *FG* at *E* and *H*, and intersect each other at *C*, and the parallelogram *IKCL* is completed; from the nature of conic sections, *EC* will be to *CA* as *CA* is to *CL*. Thus, by division <note>a geometric method of comparing ratios by taking the difference of the terms</note>, *EC* – *CA* is to *CA* – *CL* (which is *EA* to *AL*) as *EC* is to *CA*. By composition <note>a method of comparing ratios by taking the sum of the terms</note>, *EA* is to *EA* + *AL* (or *EL*) as *EC* is to *EC* + *CA* (or *EB*); and therefore (because of the

the similarity of the triangles *EAF*, *ELI*, *ECH*, and *EBG*) *AF* is to *LI* as *CH* is to *BG*. Likewise, from the nature of conic sections, *LI* (or *CK*) is to *CD* as *CD* is to *CH*; and therefore (by equality in perturbed proportion <note>a method of comparing ratios in a different order, often used in Euclidean geometry</note>) *AF* is to *CD* as *CD* is to *BG*.
*Q. E. D.* <note>Quod Erat Demonstrandum: Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

**COR. 1.** Hence, if two tangents *FG* and *PQ* meet two parallel tangents *AF* and *BG* at *F* and *G*, and *P* and *Q*, and cross each other at *O*; *AF* (by equality in perturbed proportion) will be to *BQ* as *AP* is to *BG*, and by division <note>the ratio of the differences of the terms</note>, as *FP* is to *GQ*, and therefore as *FO* is to *OG*.

**COR. 2.** From this it also follows that the two straight lines *PG* and *FQ*, drawn through the points *P* and *G*, and *F* and *Q*, will meet on the straight line *ACB* passing through the center of the figure and the points of contact *A* and *B*.

### LEMMA XXV.

*If four sides of a parallelogram, extended indefinitely, touch any conic section and are intersected by a fifth tangent; I say that if we take those segments of any two adjacent <note>sharing a boundary</note> sides that end in opposite angles of the parallelogram, either segment is to the side from which it is cut off as that part of the other adjacent side intercepted between the point of contact and the third side <note>referring here to the fifth tangent acting as a transversal</note> is to the other segment. Plate 11. Figure 4.*

Let the four sides *ML*, *IK*, *KL*, and *MI* of the parallelogram *MLIK* touch the conic section at

<sig>K</sig>
<meta>catchword: A, B,</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the proof of Lemma 24 and its corollaries concerning tangents to conic sections, then introduces Lemma 25 regarding the proportional segments created when a fifth tangent intersects a parallelogram circumscribed around a conic section.</summary>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I:</header>

*A, B, C, D*; and let the fifth tangent *FQ* cut those sides at *F, Q, H*, and *E*. Taking the segments *ME* and *KQ* of the sides *MI* and *KI*, or the segments *KH* and *MF* of the sides *KL* and *ML*, I say that *ME* is to *MI* as *BK* is to *KQ*, and *KH* is to *KL* as *AM* is to *MF*. For, by Corollary 1 of the preceding lemma, *ME* is to *EI* as (*AM* or) *BK* is to *BQ*; and, by composition <note>a mathematical operation where the sum of the antecedent and consequent is compared to the consequent</note>, *ME* is to *MI* as *BK* is to *KQ*. *Q. E. D.* <note>Quod Erat Demonstrandum: Which was to be demonstrated.</note> Also, *KH* is to *HL* as (*BK* or) *AM* is to *AF*, and by division <note>a mathematical operation where the difference of the antecedent and consequent is compared to the consequent</note>, *KH* is to *KL* as *AM* is to *MF*. *Q. E. D.*

**Cor. 1.** Hence, if a parallelogram *IKLM* described about a given conic section is given, the rectangle *KQ* x *ME*, as well as the rectangle *KH* x *MF* equal to it, will be given. For, by reason of the similar triangles *KQH* and *MFE*, those rectangles are equal.

**Cor. 2.** And if a sixth tangent *eq* is drawn meeting the tangents *KI* and *MI* at *q* and *e*, the rectangle *KQ* x *ME* will be equal to the rectangle *Kq* x *Me*. Therefore, *KQ* will be to *Me* as *Kq* is to *ME*, and by division as *Qq* is to *Ee*.

**Cor. 3.** Hence also, if *Eq* and *eQ* are joined and bisected, and a straight line is drawn through the points of bisection, this straight line will pass through the center of the conic section. For since *Qq* is to *Ee* as *KQ* is to *Me*, the same straight line will pass through the middle of all the lines *Eq, eQ*, and *MK* (by Lemma 22), and the middle point of the straight line *MK* is the center of the section.

## PROPOSITION XXVII. PROBLEM XIX.

### *To describe a trajectory that may touch five straight lines given by position.* Pl. 11. Fig. 5.

Assuming *ABG*, *BCF*, *GCD*, *FDE*, and *EA* to be the tangents given by position. Bisect the diagonals *AF* and *BE* of the quadrilateral figure *ABFE* (formed by any four of them) at points *M* and *N*; and (by Corollary 3, Lemma 25) the straight line *MN* drawn through these points of bisection will pass through the center of the trajectory. Again, bisect the diagonals (if I may call them that) *BD* and *GF* of the quadrilateral figure *BGDF* (formed by any other four tangents) at points *P* and *Q*, and the straight line *PQ* drawn through these points of bisection will also pass through the center of the trajectory. Therefore, the center will be located at the intersection of these bisecting lines. Suppose it to be *O*. Parallel to any tangent *BC*, draw line *KL* at such a distance that the center *O* is placed exactly in the middle between the parallels; this *KL* will touch the trajectory to be described. Let this line cut any other two tangents, *GCD* and *FDE*, at points *L* and *K*. Through the points *C* and *K*, and *F* and *L*, where the non-parallel tangents *CL* and *FK* meet the parallel tangents *CF* and *KL*, draw lines *CK* and *FL* meeting at *R*; and the straight line *OR*, drawn and extended, will intersect the parallel tangents *CF* and *KL* at the points of contact. This is evident from Corollary 3, Lemma 24. By the same method, the other points of contact may be found, and then the trajectory may be described by Problem 14. *Q. E. F.* <note>Quod Erat Faciendum: Which was to be done.</note>

## S C H O L I U M.

Under the preceding propositions are included those problems in which either the centers or asymptotes of the trajectories are given. For when points, tangents, and the center are given, just as many other points and as many other tangents are given at an equal distance on the other side of the center. And an asymptote is to be considered as a tangent, and its infinitely remote extremity (if we may say so) is a point of contact. Imagine the point of contact of any tangent removed *to infinity*, and the tangent will degenerate into an asymptote, and the constructions of the preceding problems will be changed into the constructions of those problems in which the asymptote is given.

After the trajectory is described, we may find its axes and foci in this manner. In the construction and figure of Lemma 21 (*Pl. 12. Fig. 1*), let those legs *BP* and *CP* of the movable angles *PBN* and *PCN* (by the intersection of which the trajectory was described) be made parallel to one another; and, retaining that position, let them revolve about their poles *B* and *C* in that figure. Meanwhile, let the other legs *CN* and *BN* of those angles, by their intersection *K* or *k*, describe the circle *BKGC*. Let *O* be the center of this circle; and from this center, let fall the perpendicular *OH* upon the ruler *MN* (where those legs *CN* and *BN* intersected while the trajectory was being described), meeting the circle at *K* and *L*. And when those other legs *CK* and *BK* meet at the point *K* that is nearest to the ruler, the first legs *CP* and *BP* will be parallel to the major axis
