and perpendicular to the lesser; and the opposite will happen if those legs meet at the farthest point *L*. Therefore, if the center of the trajectory is given, the axes will be given; and once those are given, the foci will be easily found.

But the squares of the axes are to one another as *KH* is to *LH*, and from this it is easy to describe a trajectory of a given kind through four given points. For if two of the given points are made the poles *C* and *B*, the third will provide the movable angles *PCK* and *PBK*; and since those are given, the circle *BGKC* may be described. Then, because the trajectory is of a given kind, the ratio of *OH* to *OK*, and therefore *OH* itself, will be given. Around the center *O*, with the distance *OH*, describe another circle, and the straight line that touches this circle and passes through the intersection of the legs *CK* and *BK* (when the first legs *CP* and *BP* meet at the fourth given point) will be the ruler *MN*, by means of which the trajectory may be described. From this also, on the other hand, a trapezium of a given kind (except for a few cases that are impossible) may be inscribed in a given conic section.

There are also other lemmas by the help of which trajectories of a given kind may be described through given points and touching given lines. An example of this is: if a straight line is drawn through any point fixed by position so that it cuts a given conic section in two points, and the distance between the intersections is bisected, the point of bisection will touch another conic section of the same kind as the former, and having its axes parallel to the axes of the former. But I hasten to matters of greater use.

## LEMMA XXVI.

*To place the three angles of a triangle, given both in shape and size, in relation to as many straight lines given by position—provided they are not all parallel to each other—in such a way that the individual angles may touch the individual lines. Plate 12. Figure 2.*

Three indefinite straight lines *AB, AC, BC*, are given by position, and it is required to place the triangle *DEF* so that its angle *D* may touch the line *AB*, its angle *E* the line *AC*, and its angle *F* the line *BC*. Upon *DE, DF,* and *EF*, describe three segments of circles *DRE, DGF, EMF*, containing angles equal to the angles *BAC, ABC, ACB* respectively. But those segments are to be described on such sides of the lines *DE, DF, EF*, that the letters *DRED* follow the same order as the letters *BACB*; the letters *DGFD* in the same order as the letters *ABCA*; and the letters *EMFE* in the same order as the letters *ACBA*. Then, completing those segments into entire circles, let the two former circles intersect one another at *G*, and suppose *P* and *Q* to be their centers. Then, joining *GP* and *PQ*, take *Ga* in proportion to *AB*, as *GP* is to *PQ*; and around the center *G*, with the radius *Ga*, describe a circle that may intersect the first circle *DGE* at *a*. # L E M M A XXVII.

### *To describe a trapezium of a given shape, the angles of which may be so placed with respect to four straight lines given in position, that are neither all parallel to each other nor converge to one common point, that the several angles may touch the several lines. Pl. 13. Fig. 1.*

Let the four straight lines *ABC*, *AD*, *BD*, *CE*, be given in position; the first intersecting the second at *A*, the third at *B*, and the fourth at *C*; and suppose a trapezium *fghi* is to be described, which is similar to the trapezium *FGHI*; and whose angle *f*, equal to the given angle *F*, may touch the straight line *ABC*; and the other angles *g*, *h*, *i*, equal to the other given angles *G*, *H*, *I*, may touch the other lines *AD*, *BD*, *CE*, respectively. Join *FH*, and upon *FG*, *FH*, *FI* describe as many segments of circles *FSG*, *FTH*, *FVI*; the first of which *FSG* may contain an angle equal to the angle *BAD*; the second *FTH* containing an angle equal to the angle *CBD*; and the third *FVI* of an angle equal to the angle *ACE*. But the segments are to be described towards those sides of the lines *FG*, *FH*, *FI*, so that the circular order of the letters *FSGF* may be the same as that of the letters *BADB*, and that the letters *FTHF* may turn about in the same order as the letters *CBDC*, and the letters *FVIF* in the same order

*BR* is to *BD* as *FH* is to *FG*. Therefore, *fh* is to *fg* as *FH* is to *FG*. Since, therefore, *gi* to *hi* is likewise as *Mi* to *Li*—that is, as *GI* to *HI*—it is clear that the lines *FI* and *fi* are divided similarly at *G* and *H*, and at *g* and *h*. **Q. E. F.**

In the construction of this corollary, after the line *LK* is drawn cutting *CE* at *i*, we may extend *iE* to *V*, so that *EV* is to *Ei* as *FH* is to *HI*, and then draw *Vf* parallel to *BD*. The result will be the same if, around the center *i* with a radius *IH*, we describe a circle cutting *BD* at *X*, and extend *iX* to *Y* so that *iY* is equal to *IF*, and then draw *Yf* parallel to *BD*.

*Sir Christopher Wren* and *Dr. Wallis* long ago provided other solutions to this problem.

# SECTION VI.

### How the motions are to be found in given orbits.

### PROPOSITION XXX. PROBLEM XXII.

#### *To find at any assigned time the position of a body moving in a given parabolic trajectory.*

Let *S (Plate 14. Figure 1.)* be the focus, and *A* the principal vertex of the parabola; and suppose 4*AS* × *M* is equal to the parabolic area *APS* to be cut off, which was either described by the radius *SP* since the body's departure from the vertex, or is to be described by it before its arrival there. Now the quantity of that area to be cut off is known from the time, which is proportional to it. Bisect *AS* at *G*, and erect the perpendicular *GH* equal to 3 *M*, and a circle described about the center *H*, with the radius *HS*, will cut the parabola at the position *P* required. For, dropping the perpendicular *PO*...

<meta>catchword: perpen-</meta>

<summary>This page introduces a geometric method for determining the position of a body moving along a parabolic trajectory at any given time. It uses the relationship between the time elapsed and the area swept out by the radius vector from the focus to find the exact point on the curve.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, parabola, trajectory, focus, vertex, orbital motion, geometry, Isaac Newton, Section VI</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric proof of Proposition 30, following the construction of point P in a parabolic orbit using the properties of areas and proportions.</meta>

<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

perpendicular to the axis, and drawing *PH*, we have *AG*² + *GH*² (= *HP*² = (*AO* — *AG*)² + (*PO* — *GH*)²) = *AO*² + *PO*² — 2*GAO* — 2*GH* $\times$ *PO* + *AG*² + *GH*². From this, 2*GH* $\times$ *PO* (= *AO*² + *PO*² — 2*GAO*) = *AO*² + 3/4 *PO*². If we substitute *AO* $\times$ $\frac{PO^2}{4AS}$ for *AO*², and then divide all the terms by 3*PO* and multiply them by 2*AS*, we shall have 4/3 *GH* $\times$ *AS* (= 1/6 *AO* $\times$ *PO* + 1/2 *AS* $\times$ *PO* = $\frac{AO + 3 AS}{6} \times PO = \frac{4 AO - 3 SO}{6} \times PO$, which is the area *APO* — *SPO*) = the area *APS*. But *GH* was 3*M*, and therefore 4/3 *GH* $\times$ *AS* is 4*AS* $\times$ *M*. Therefore, the area cut off *APS* is equal to the area that was to be cut off, 4*AS* $\times$ *M*. Q.E.D.

**COROLLARY 1.** Hence *GH* is to *AS* as the time in which the body described the arc *AP* is to the time in which the body described the arc between the vertex *A* and the perpendicular erected from the focus *S* upon the axis.

**COROLLARY 2.** And supposing a circle *ASP* to pass continually through the moving body *P*, the velocity of the point *H* is to the velocity which the body had at the vertex *A* as 3 is to 8; and therefore, the line *GH* is in the same ratio to the straight line <note>"right line" in the original</note> which the body, in the time of its moving from *A* to *P*, would describe with the velocity it had at the vertex *A*.

**COROLLARY 3.** Hence also, on the other hand, the time may be found in which the body has described any given arc *AP*. Join *AP*, and at its midpoint erect a perpendicular meeting the straight line *GH* at *H*.

<header>SECTION VI. Of Natural Philosophy.</header>

## LEMMA XXVIII.

*There is no oval figure whose area, cut off by straight lines <note>"right lines" in the original</note> at will, can be universally found by means of equations of any number of finite terms and dimensions.*

Suppose that within the oval any point is given, about which as a pole a straight line is perpetually revolving with a uniform motion, while in that straight line a movable point starting from the pole moves always forward with a velocity proportional to the square of that straight line within the oval. By this motion, that point will describe a spiral with infinite turns. Now, if a portion of the area of the oval cut off by that straight line could be found by a finite equation, the distance of the point from the pole, which is proportional to this area, might be found by the same equation; and therefore all the points of the spiral might be found by a finite equation also. Consequently, the intersection of a straight line given in position with the spiral might also be found by a finite equation. But every straight line infinitely extended cuts a spiral in an infinite number of points; and the equation by which any one intersection of two lines is found simultaneously represents all their intersections by as many roots, and therefore rises to as many dimensions as there are intersections. Because two circles mutually cut one

<sig>L</sig>
<meta>catchword: another</meta>

<summary>This Lemma presents a geometric argument stating that the area of an oval figure cannot be determined by a finite algebraic equation. The proof relies on the relationship between the area of the oval and the properties of a spiral, which would require an equation with infinite roots.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, oval figures, area, finite equations, spirals, intersections, algebraic roots, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric proof from Lemma XXVIII, explaining how the number of intersections between curves determines the degree (or "dimensions") of the algebraic equations required to find them.</meta>

<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

another in two points, one of those intersections cannot be found except by an equation of two dimensions, by which the other intersection may also be found. Because there may be four intersections of two conic sections, any one of them cannot be found universally except by an equation of four dimensions, by which they may all be found together. For if those intersections are sought separately, because the law and condition of each is the same, the calculation will be the same in every case, and therefore the conclusion will always be the same; this conclusion must therefore include all those intersections at once within itself and represent them all without distinction. Hence it is that the intersections of the conic sections with third-order curves, because they may amount to six, result together from equations of six dimensions; and the intersections of two third-order curves, because they may amount to nine, result together from equations of nine dimensions. If this did not necessarily happen, we could reduce all solid problems to plane problems, and those higher than solid to solid problems. But here I speak of curves irreducible in power. For if the equation by which the curve is defined can be reduced to a lower power, the curve will not be a single curve, but composed of two or more whose intersections may be found separately by different calculations. In the same manner, the two intersections of straight lines with conic sections always result from equations of two dimensions; the three intersections of straight lines with irreducible third-order curves result from equations of three dimensions; the four intersections of straight lines with irreducible fourth-order curves,

<summary>This page explains that the number of intersections between geometric curves dictates the degree of the equation needed to solve for them, noting that irreducible curves require equations proportional to their intersections. This mathematical principle supports the previous argument that figures with infinite intersections, like spirals, cannot be defined by finite equations.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, algebraic equations, dimensions, conic sections, irreducible curves, intersections, plane problems, solid problems</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric reasoning from the previous discussion, extending the logic of curve intersections and the dimensionality of equations to spirals and oval figures.</meta>

<header>SECT. VI. *of Natural Philosophy.*</header>

by equations of four dimensions, and so on to infinity. Therefore, the innumerable intersections of a straight line with a spiral—since this is but one simple curve and not reducible to more curves—require equations with an infinite number of dimensions and roots, by which they may all be represented together. For the law and calculus for all of them is the same. For if a perpendicular is dropped from the pole upon that intersecting straight line, and that perpendicular, together with the intersecting line, revolves about the pole, the intersections of the spiral will mutually pass from one into the other; and that which was first or nearest will, after one revolution, be the second; after two, the third, and so on. Nor will the equation in the meantime be changed, except as the magnitudes of those quantities are changed by which the position of the intersecting line is determined. Therefore, since those quantities return to their original magnitudes after every revolution, the equation will return to its original form, and consequently, one and the same equation will represent all the intersections, and will therefore have an infinite number of roots, by which they may all be displayed. And therefore the intersection of a straight line with a spiral cannot be universally found by any finite equation; and consequently, there is no oval figure whose area, cut off by straight lines at will, can be universally represented by any such equation.

By the same argument, if the distance between the pole and the point by which the spiral is described is taken as proportional to that part of the perimeter of the oval which is cut off, it may be proved that the length of the perimeter cannot be universally represented by any finite equation. But here I speak

**Cor.** Hence the area of an ellipse, described by a radius drawn from the focus to the moving body, cannot be found from a given time using a finite equation; therefore, it cannot be determined by the description of geometrically rational curves. I call those curves "geometrically rational" if all their points can be determined by lengths that are definable by equations—that is, by the complex ratios of lengths. Other curves (such as spirals, quadratrices, and cycloids) I call "geometrically irrational." For lengths that are or are not in the proportion of number to number (according to the tenth book of Euclid's *Elements*) are arithmetically rational or irrational. Therefore, I determine an area of an ellipse proportional to the time in which it is described using a geometrically irrational curve, in the following manner.

### SECT. VI. Of Natural Philosophy.

...was described, or equal to what is called the mean motion. Let an angle *V* (the first equation of the mean motion) be to the angle *Y* (the greatest first equation) as the sine of double the angle *T* is to the radius; and let an angle *X* (the second equation) be to the angle *Z* (the second greatest equation) as the cube of the sine of the angle *T* is to the cube of the radius. Then take the angle *BHP* (the equated mean motion) equal to *T* + *X* + *V* (the sum of the angles *T*, *V*, and *X*) if the angle *T* is less than a right angle; or equal to *T* + *X* — *V* (the difference of the same) if that angle *T* is greater than one and less than two right angles. If *HP* meets the ellipse at *P*, draw *SP*, and it will cut off the area *BSP* nearly proportional to the time.

This practice seems efficient enough because the angles *V* and *X* (taken in seconds, if you wish) are very small; it will be sufficient to find two or three of their first digits. But it is also sufficiently accurate to correspond to the theory of planetary motions. For even in the orbit of *Mars*, where the greatest equation of the center amounts to ten degrees, the error will scarcely exceed one second. But when the angle of the equated mean motion *BHP* is found, the angle of the true motion *BSP* and the distance *SP* are easily obtained by known methods.

This concludes the discussion concerning the motion of bodies in curved lines. But it may also happen that a moving body shall ascend or descend in a straight line; and I shall now proceed to explain what belongs to such kinds of motion.

**COROLLARY 1.** When the points $B$ and $S$ coincide, $TC$ will be to $TS$ as $AC$ is to $AO$.

**COROLLARY 2.** A body revolving in any circle at a given distance from the center, if its motion is directed upward, will ascend to double its distance from the center.

### PROPOSITION XXXIV. THEOREM X.

*If the figure BED is a parabola, I say that the velocity of a falling body in any place C is equal to the velocity by which a body may uniformly describe a circle about the center B at half the distance BC. Plate 15. Figure 5.*

For (by corollary 7, proposition 16) the velocity of a body describing a parabola $RPB$ about the center $S$, at any point $P$, is equal to the velocity of a body uniformly describing a circle about the same center $S$ at half the distance $SP$. Let the breadth $CP$ of the parabola be diminished to infinity, so that the parabolic arc $PfB$ coincides with the straight line $CB$, the center $S$ with the vertex $B$, and the distance $SP$ with the distance $BC$, and the proposition will be clear. $Q. E. D.$

<header>SECTION VII. Of Natural Philosophy.</header>

## PROPOSITION XXXV. THEOREM XI.

*Assuming the same things, I state that the area of the figure DES, described by the indefinite radius SD, is equal to the area which a body with a radius equal to half the latus rectum of the figure DES, by uniformly revolving about the center S, may describe in the same time. *Pl. 16. Fig. 1.**

For suppose a body C, in the smallest moment of time, describes the infinitely small line Cc while falling, while another body K, uniformly revolving about the center S in the circle OKk, describes the arc Kk. Erect the perpendiculars CD and cd, meeting the figure DES at D and d. Join SD, Sd, SK, and Sk, and draw the line Dd to meet the axis AS at T, and upon it drop the perpendicular SY.

CASE 1. If the figure DES is a circle or a rectangular hyperbola, bisect its transverse diameter AS at O, and SO will be half the <term>latus rectum</term>. And because the ratio of TC to TD is the same as Cc to Dd, and TD to TS is as CD to SY; *ex aequo* <note>from equal proportions</note> the ratio of TC to TS will be as CD $\times$ Cc is to SY $\times$ Dd. But (by corollary 1 of proposition 33) TC is to TS as AC is to AO; that is, if the ultimate ratios of the lines are taken as points D and d coincide. Therefore AC is to AO or SK as CD $\times$ Cc is to SY $\times$ Dd. Furthermore, the velocity of the descending body at C is to the velocity of a body describing a circle about

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This theorem demonstrates that the area swept by a radius to a falling body is equal to the area swept by a body revolving uniformly at a distance of half the latus rectum. The proof uses geometric properties of circles and rectangular hyperbolas to establish equal ratios between these motions over time.</summary>

<keywords>Natural Philosophy, latus rectum, rectangular hyperbola, ultimate ratio, velocity, Proposition XXXV, Theorem XI, geometric proof, area description</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition 35, Theorem 11, which concludes the proof for Case 1 (circles and rectangular hyperbolas) and addresses Case 2 (parabolas).</meta>

<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

the center *S*, at the distance *SC*, in the square root ratio <note>subduplicate ratio</note> of *AC* to *AO* or *SK* (by Proposition 33). And this velocity is to the velocity of a body describing the circle *OKk* in the square root ratio of *SK* to *SC* (by Corollary 6, Proposition 4). And from equal proportions <note>ex æquo</note>, the ratio of the first velocity to the last—that is, the small line *Cc* to the arc *Kk*—is in the square root ratio of *AC* to *SC*, which is the ratio of *AC* to *CD*. Therefore, *CD* $\times$ *Cc* is equal to *AC* $\times$ *Kk*, and consequently *AC* is to *SK* as *AC* $\times$ *Kk* is to *SY* $\times$ *Dd*. From there, *SK* $\times$ *Kk* is equal to *SY* $\times$ *Dd*, and $\frac{1}{2}$ *SK* $\times$ *Kk* is equal to $\frac{1}{2}$ *SY* $\times$ *Dd*—that is, the area *KSk* is equal to the area *SDd*. Therefore, in every moment of time, two equal segments of area, *KSk* and *SDd*, are generated. If their magnitude is diminished and their number increased to infinity, they reach the ratio of equality, and consequently (by the Corollary to Lemma 4) the total areas generated together are always equal. *Q. E. D.*

**CASE 2.** But if the figure *DES* (*Fig. 2.*) is a parabola, we shall find (as above) that the ratio of *CD* $\times$ *Cc* to *SY* $\times$ *Dd* is as *TC* to *TS*—that is, as 2 to 1; and that therefore $\frac{1}{4}$ *CD* $\times$ *Cc* is equal to $\frac{1}{2}$ *SY* $\times$ *Dd*. But the velocity of the falling body at *C* is equal to the velocity with which a circle may be uniformly described at the distance $\frac{1}{2}$ *SC* (by Proposition 34). And the ratio of this velocity to the velocity with which a circle may be described with the radius *SK*—that is, the ratio of the small line *Cc* to the arc *Kk*—is (by Corollary 5, Proposition 4) in the square root ratio of *SK* to $\frac{1}{2}$ *SC*; that is, in the ratio of *SK* to $\frac{1}{2}$ *CD*. Therefore, $\frac{1}{2}$ *SK* $\times$ *Kk* is equal to $\frac{1}{4}$ *CD* $\times$ *Cc*, and therefore equal to $\frac{1}{2}$ *SY* $\times$ *Dd*; that is, the area *KSk* is equal to the area *SDd*, just as before. *Q. E. D.*

### PROPOSITION XXXVI. PROBLEM XXV.

*To determine the times of the de-*
*scent of a body falling from a given*
*place A. Pl. 16. Fig. 3.*

On the diameter $AS$, the distance of the
body from the center at the beginning, describe
the semicircle $ADS$, as also the semicircle
$OKH$ equal to it, around the center $S$. From
any position $C$ of the body, erect the ordinate $CD$.
Join $SD$, and make the sector $OSK$ equal to the
area $ASD$. It is evident by Proposition 35 that the
body, while falling, will cover the distance $AC$ in the
same time in which another body, revolving uni-
formly around the center $S$, would describe the arc
$OK$. *Q.E.F.*

center $S$ at the given interval $SG$, take $GA$ to 1/2 $AS$. If that ratio is the same as the ratio of 2 to 1, the point $A$ is infinitely distant; in which case a parabola is to be described with any latus rectum to the vertex $S$ and axis $SG$, as shown in Proposition 34. With the interval *AS*, describe the quadrant of a circle *AE*; and let *CD* be the sine of any arc *AD*. Then the body *A*, in the time *AD*, will describe the space *AC* in falling, and at the position *C* will acquire the velocity *CD*.

This is demonstrated from Proposition 10 in the same way that Proposition 32 was demonstrated from Proposition 11.

**COROLLARY 1.** Hence, the times are equal in which one body falling from position *A* arrives at the center *S*, and another revolving body describes the quadrantal arc *ADE*.

**COROLLARY 2.** Therefore, all the times are equal in which bodies falling from any place arrive at the center. For all the periodic times of revolving bodies are equal, according to Corollary 3, Proposition 4.

### PROPOSITION XL. THEOREM XIII.

*If a body, acted upon by any centripetal force, moves in any manner, and another body ascends or descends in a straight line; and if their velocities are equal in any one case of equal altitudes, then their velocities will also be equal at all equal altitudes.*

Let a body descend from A (*Pl. 17. Fig. 3.*) through D and E, toward the center C; and let another body move from V along the curved line VIKk. From the center C, at any distances, describe the concentric circles DI and EK, meeting the straight line AC at D and E, and the curve VIK at I and K.

<summary>This page introduces Section VIII, focusing on the discovery of orbits under various centripetal forces. It presents a theorem stating that bodies moving under the same centripetal force will have identical velocities at identical altitudes, regardless of whether their path is a straight line or a curve.</summary>

# Sect. VIII. *of Natural Philosophy.*

Draw *IC* meeting *KE* in *N*, and on *IK* let fall the perpendicular *NT*; and let the interval *DE* or *IN*, between the circumferences of the circles, be very small; and imagine the bodies at *D* and *I* to have equal velocities. Then because the distances *CD* and *CI* are equal, the centripetal forces at *D* and *I* will also be equal. Let those forces be expressed by the equal small lines *DE* and *IN*; and let the force *IN* (by Corollary 2 of the laws of motion) be resolved into two others, *NT* and *IT*. Then the force *NT*, acting in the direction of the line *NT* perpendicular to the path *ITK* of the body, will not at all affect or change the velocity of the body in that path, but only draw it aside from a rectilinear course, and make it deflect perpetually from the tangent of the orbit, and proceed in the curvilinear path *I l Kk*. That whole force, therefore, will be spent in producing this effect; but the other force *IT*, acting in the direction of the course of the body, will be all employed in accelerating it; and in the least given time, it will produce an acceleration proportional to itself. Therefore the accelerations of the bodies at *D* and *I* produced in equal times are as the lines *DE* and *IT* (if we take the first ratios of the nascent lines *DE*, *IN*, *IK*, *IT*, and *NT*); and in unequal times, as those lines and the times jointly. But the times in which *DE* and *IK* are described are, by reason of the equal velocities (at *D* and *I*), as the spaces described *DE* and *IK*; and therefore the accelerations in the course of the bodies through the lines *DE* and *IK* are as *DE* and *IT*, and *DE* and *IK* jointly; that is, as the square of *DE* to the rectangle of *IT* and *IK*. **Cor.** 1. Therefore, if a body either oscillates by hanging from a string, or is forced to move in a curved line by any polished and perfectly smooth impediment; and another body ascends or descends in a straight line, and their velocities are equal at any one equal altitude; then their velocities will also be equal at all other equal altitudes. For, by the string of the hanging body, or by the impediment of a perfectly smooth vessel, the same effect will be produced as by the transverse force *NT*. The body is neither accelerated nor retarded by it, but is only obliged to leave its straight course.

The areas *VDBa* and *VIC* are always equal; and the nascent particles *DcxE* and *XCy* of the areas *VDca* and *VCX* are always equal; therefore the generated area *VDBa* will be equal to the generated area *VIC*, and therefore proportional to the time; and the generated area *VDca* is equal to the generated sector *VCX*. **COROLLARY 1.** Hence the greatest and least altitudes of the bodies, that is the <term>apsides</term> of the trajectories, may be found very readily. For the apsides are those points in which a straight line *IC* drawn through the centre falls perpendicularly upon the trajectory *VIK*; which comes to pass when the straight lines *IK* and *NK* become equal; that is, when the area *ABFD* is equal to $Z^2$.

**COROLLARY 2.** So also, the angle *KIN* in which the trajectory at any place cuts the line *IC*, may be readily found by the given altitude *IC* of the body: namely, by making the sine of that angle to the radius as *KN* is to *IK*; that is, as *Z* is to the square root of the area *ABFD*.

T; then, joining *CR*, let the straight line *CP* be drawn, equal to the abscissa *CT*, making an angle *VCP* proportional to the sector *VCR*. If a centripetal force, inversely proportional to the cubes of the distances of the locations from the center, is directed toward the center *C*; and if from position *V* a body starts out with a correct velocity in a direction perpendicular to the straight line *CV*: that body will proceed in a trajectory *VPQ*, which the point *P* will always touch. Therefore, if the conic section *VRS* is a hyperbola, the body will descend to the center; but if it is an ellipsis, it will ascend perpetually, going further and further away toward infinity. Conversely, if a body endowed with any velocity moves away from position *V*, and depending on whether it begins to descend obliquely toward the center or ascend obliquely from it, the figure *VRS* is either a hyperbola or an ellipsis, the trajectory may be found by increasing or decreasing the angle *VCP* in a given ratio. And if the centripetal force becomes centrifugal, the body will ascend obliquely in the trajectory *VPQ*, which is found by taking the angle *VCP* proportional to the elliptic sector *VRC*, and the length *CP* equal to the length *CT*, as before. All these things follow from the preceding proposition by the <term>quadrature</term> <note>the process of determining the area of a surface</note> of a certain curve, the discovery of which I omit for the sake of brevity, as it is simple enough.

### PROPOSITION XLII. PROBLEM XXIX.

> *The law of centripetal force being given, it is required to find the motion of a body setting out from a given place, with a given velocity, in the direction of a given straight line.*

Suppose the same things as in the three preceding propositions; and let the body set out from the place *I*, (*Plate 17, Figure 6*) in the direction of the small line *IK*, with the same velocity as another body might acquire in *D* by falling with a uniform centripetal force from the place *P*; and let this uniform force be to the force with which the body is first urged at *I*, as *DR* is to *DF*. Let the body proceed toward *k*; and about the center *C* with the distance *Ck*, describe the circle *ke*, meeting the straight line *PD* at *e*, and let there be erected the lines *eg*, *ev*, and *ew*, drawn as ordinates to the curves *BFg*, *abv*, and *acw*. From the given rectangle *PDRQ* and the given law of centripetal force by which the first body is acted on, the curve line *BFg* is also given by the construction of Problem 27 and its first corollary. Then, from the given angle *CIK*, the proportion of the <term>nascent lines</term> <note>infinitesimal lines representing the very beginning of motion</note> *IK* and *KN* is given; and from that, by the construction of Problem 28, the quantity *Q* is given, along with the curve lines *abv* and *acw*. Therefore, at the end of any time *Dbve*, both the altitude of the body (*Ce* or *Ck*) and the area *Dcwe* are given, along with the sector

In these propositions, we assume that the centripetal force varies as it moves away from the center according to some law that anyone may imagine as they please; however, at equal distances from the center, the force is the same everywhere.

Up to this point, I have considered the motions of bodies in stationary orbits. It remains now to add something concerning their motions in orbits that revolve around the centers of force.

# SECTION IX.

## *Of the motion of bodies in movable orbits; and of the motion of the apsides.*

---

### PROPOSITION XLIII. PROBLEM XXX.

*It is required to make a body move in a trajectory that revolves about the center of force, in the same manner as another body in the same trajectory at rest.*

In the orbit *VPK* (*Plate* 18. *Fig.* 1.) given by its position, let the body *P* revolve, moving from *V* toward *K*. From the center *C*, let there be continually drawn a line *Cp*, equal to *CP*, making the angle *VCp* proportional to the angle *VCP*; and the area which the line *Cp* describes will be to the area *VCP*, which the line *CP* describes at the same time, as the velocity of the describing line *Cp*,

It establishes a geometric relationship between a body in a stationary orbit and one in a moving orbit by comparing their relative areas and angular velocities.</summary>

<page-num>178</page-num>

to the velocity of the describing line CP; that is, as the angle VCp is to the angle VCP, and therefore in a given ratio, and consequently proportional to the time. Since the area described by the line Cp in a stationary plane is proportional to the time, it is clear that a body, being acted upon by an appropriate amount of centripetal force, may revolve with the point p in the curved line which the same point p, by the method just explained, may be made to describe on a stationary plane. Make the angle VCu equal to the angle PCp, and the line Cu equal to CV, and the figure uCp equal to the figure VCP; then the body, being always at the point p, will move along the perimeter of the revolving figure uCp, and will describe its (revolving) arc *up* in the same time that the other body P describes the similar and equal arc VP in the stationary figure VPK. Find then, by Corollary 5 of Proposition 6, the centripetal force by which a body may be made to revolve in the curved line which the point p describes in a stationary plane, and the problem will be solved. *Q.E.F.*

### PROPOSITION XLIV. THEOREM XIV.

*The difference of the forces by which two bodies may be made to move equally—one in a stationary orbit, the other in the same orbit revolving—is in an inverse triplicate ratio of their common altitudes.*

Let the parts of the stationary orbit VP, PK, (Plate 18, Figure 2) be similar and equal to the

Drop a perpendicular *kr* from the point *k* to the straight line *pC*, and extend it to *m*, so that *mr* is to *kr* as the angle *VCp* is to the angle *VCP*. Because the altitudes of the bodies, *PC* and *pC*, as well as *KC* and *kC*, are always equal, it is obvious that the increases or decreases of the lines *PC* and *pC* are always equal. Therefore, if each of the motions of the bodies at the locations *P* and *p* is resolved into two components (by Corollary 2 of the Laws of Motion)—one of which is directed toward the center, along the lines *PC* and *pC*, and the other, transverse to the former, has a direction perpendicular to the lines *PC* and *pC*—the motions toward the center will be equal. Additionally, the transverse motion of body *p* will be to the transverse motion of body *P* as the angular motion of the line *pC* is to the angular motion of the line *PC*; that is, as the angle *VCp* is to the angle *VCP*. Therefore, at the same time that body *P*, by both its motions, comes to the point *K*, body *p*, having an equal motion toward the center, will be moved an equal distance from *p* toward *C*. Consequently, once that time has elapsed, it will be found somewhere on the line *mkr*, which, passing through the point *k*, is perpendicular to the line *pC*. Through its transverse motion, it will gain a distance from the line *pC* that will be to the distance which the other body *P* gains from the line *PC* as the transverse motion of body *p* is to the transverse motion of the other body *P*. **Cor.** 5. Therefore, the motion of a body in an immovable orbit being given, its angular motion around the center of the forces may be increased or diminished in a given ratio, and from there, new immovable orbits may be found in which bodies may revolve with new centripetal forces.

Therefore, if there is erected (*Pl.* 18. *Fig.* 3.) the line *VP* of an indeterminate length, perpendicular to the line *CV* given by position, and *CP* is drawn, and *Cp* is made equal to it, making the angle *VCp* have a given ratio to the angle *VCP*; the force with which a body may revolve in the curved line *Vpk*, which the point *p* is continually describing, will be inversely as the cube of the altitude *Cp*. But this curve *Vpk* is the same as the curve *VPQ* found in Corollary 3, Proposition 41, in which, I said, bodies attracted with such forces would ascend obliquely.

### Section IX. of Natural Philosophy.

...the likeness of this orbit which a body acted upon by a uniform centripetal force describes, and of that orbit which a body performing its circuits in a revolving ellipse will describe in a quiescent plane. By this comparison of the terms, these orbits are made similar; not universally, indeed, but then only when they approach very near to a circular figure. A body, therefore, revolving with a uniform centripetal force in an orbit nearly circular, will always describe an angle of $\frac{180}{\sqrt{3}}$ degrees, or 103 degrees 55 minutes 23 seconds at the center; moving from the upper apsis to the lower apsis when it has once described that angle, and from there returning to the upper apsis when it has described that angle again; and so on infinitely.

**COROLLARY 1.** Hence, if the centripetal force is proportional to any power of the altitude, that power may be found from the motion of the apsides; and vice versa. That is, if the entire angular motion with which the body returns to the same apsis is to the angular motion of one revolution (or 360 degrees) as some number *m* is to another number *n*, and the altitude is called A, the force will be proportional to the power $A^{\frac{n^2}{m^2} - 3}$ of the altitude A; the index of which power is $\frac{n^2}{m^2} - 3$. This is shown by the second examples. Hence, it is clear that the force, as it recedes from the center, cannot decrease in a ratio greater than the cube <note>triplicate ratio</note> of the altitude. A body revolving with such a force and leaving the apsis, if it once begins to descend, can never reach the lower apsis or minimum altitude, but will descend to the center, describing the curve line discussed in Corollary 3 of Proposition 41. But if it should, upon leaving the lower apsis, begin to ascend even slightly, it will ascend infinitely <note>in infinitum</note> and never reach the upper apsis; instead, it will describe the curve line mentioned in the same corollary and in Corollary 6 of Proposition 44. So that where the force, in its recession from the center, decreases

decreases in a ratio greater than the triplicate ratio <note>the cube</note> of the altitude, the body, upon leaving the apsis, will either descend to the center or ascend to infinity <note>in infinitum</note>, depending on whether it descends or ascends at the beginning of its motion. But if the force, as it recedes from the center, either decreases in a ratio less than the triplicate ratio of the altitude, or increases in any ratio of the altitude whatsoever; the body will never descend to the center, but will eventually arrive at the lower apsis; and conversely, if the body, alternately ascending and descending from one apsis to another, never reaches the center, then either the force increases as it recedes from the center, or it decreases in a ratio less than the triplicate ratio of the altitude; and the sooner the body returns from one apsis to another, the farther the ratio of the forces is from the triplicate ratio.

As if the body should return to and from the upper apsis by an alternate descent and ascent in 8 revolutions, or in 4, or 2, or 1 $\frac{1}{2}$; that is, if *m* should be to *n* as 8 or 4 or 2 or 1 $\frac{1}{2}$ to 1, and therefore $\frac{n^2}{m^2} - 3$ be $\frac{1}{64} - 3$, or $\frac{1}{16} - 3$, or $\frac{1}{4} - 3$, or $\frac{4}{9} - 3$; then the force will be as $A^{\frac{1}{64} - 3}$, or $A^{\frac{1}{16} - 3}$, or $A^{\frac{1}{4} - 3}$, or $A^{\frac{4}{9} - 3}$; that is, it will be reciprocally as $A^{3 - \frac{1}{64}}$, or $A^{3 - \frac{1}{16}}$, or $A^{3 - \frac{1}{4}}$, or $A^{3 - \frac{4}{9}}$. ## PROPOSITION XLVII. THEOREM XV.

*Supposing the centripetal force to be proportional to the distance of the body from the center; all bodies revolving in any planes whatsoever will describe ellipses, and complete their revolutions in equal times; and those which move in straight lines, running backward and forward alternately, will complete their several periods of going and returning in the same times.*

For letting all things stand as in the previous proposition, the force *SV*, with which the body *Q* revolving in any plane *PQR* is attracted toward the center *S*, is proportional to the distance *SQ*; and therefore because *SV* and *SQ*, as well as *TV* and *CQ*, are proportional, the force *TV* with which the body is attracted toward the given point *C* in the plane of the orbit is proportional to the distance *CQ*. Therefore the forces with which bodies found in the plane *PQR* are attracted toward the point *C* are in a proportion to the distances equal to the forces with which the same bodies are attracted in every direction toward the center *S*; and therefore the bodies will move in the same times, and in the same figures in any plane *PQR* about the point *C*, as they would do in free space about the center *S*; and therefore (by Corollary 2, Proposition 10, and Corollary 2

<meta>catchword: Proposition</meta>

<summary>Proposition 47 proves that if centripetal force is proportional to distance, all orbiting bodies describe ellipses with equal periods, regardless of their plane of motion. This applies both to revolving bodies and those moving back and forth in a straight line.</summary>

<header>SECT. X. *of Natural Philosophy.* 199</header>

(prop. 38) they will in equal times either describe ellipses in that plane about the center *C*, or move back and forth in straight lines passing through the center *C* in that plane; completing the same periods of time in all cases. *Q. E. D.*

## S C H O L I U M.

The ascent and descent of bodies on curved surfaces <note>The original term is "curve superficies".</note> has a close relation to these motions we have been speaking of. Imagine curved lines to be described on any plane, and to revolve about any given axes passing through the center of force, and by that revolution to describe curved surfaces; and that the bodies move in such a way that their centers may be always found on those surfaces. If those bodies move back and forth with an oblique ascent and descent; their motions will be performed in planes passing through the axis, and therefore in the curved lines by whose revolution those curved surfaces were generated. In those cases, therefore, it will be sufficient to consider the motion in those curved lines.

## PROPOSITION XLVIII. THEOREM XVI.

*If a wheel stands upon the outside of a globe at right angles to it, and revolving about its own axis goes forward in a great circle; the length of the curvilinear path which any point, given in the perimeter of the wheel,*

### PROPOSITION XLIX. THEOREM XVII.

*If a wheel stands upon the inside of a concave globe at right angles to it, and revolving about its own axis goes forward in one of the great circles of the globe, the length of the curvilinear path which any point, given in the perimeter of the wheel, has described since it touched the globe, will be to double the versed sine of half the arc which in all that time has touched the globe in passing over it, as the difference of the diameters of the globe and the wheel is to the semidiameter of the globe.*

Let ***ABL*** (Pl. 19. Fig. 1. 2.) be the globe, ***C*** its centre, ***BPV*** the wheel resting upon it <note>The original term is "insisting thereon."</note>, ***E*** the centre of the wheel, ***B*** the point of contact, and ***P*** the given point in the perimeter of the wheel.

Imagine this wheel to proceed in the great circle *ABL* from *A* through *B* toward *L*, and in its progress to revolve in such a manner that the arcs *AB* and *PB* are always equal to one another, and the given point *P* in the perimeter of the wheel describes in the meantime the curvilinear path *AP*. Let *AP* be the whole curvilinear path described since the wheel touched the globe at *A*; the length of this path *AP* will be to twice the versed sine of the arc 1/2 *PB* as 2 *CE* is to *CB*. For let the straight line *CE* (extended if necessary) meet the wheel at *V*, and join *CP*, *BP*, *EP*, and *VP*; extend *CP*, and let the perpendicular *VF* fall upon it. Let *PH* and *VH*, meeting at *H*, touch the circle at *P* and *V*, and let *PH* cut *VF* at *G*; let the perpendiculars *GI* and *HK* fall to *VP*. From the center *C* with any radius let the circle *nom* be described, cutting the straight line *CP* at *n*, the perimeter of the wheel *BP* at *o*, and the curvilinear path *AP* at *m*; and from the center *V* with the radius *Vo* let a circle be described cutting *VP* extended at *q*.

Because the wheel in its progress always revolves about the point of contact *B*, it is manifest that the straight line *BP* is perpendicular to that curved line *AP* which the point *P* of the wheel describes, and therefore that the straight line *VP* will touch this curve at point *P*. Let the radius of the circle *nom* be gradually increased or diminished so that at last it becomes equal to the distance *CP*; and by reason of the similarity of the evanescent figure *Pnomq* and the figure *PFGVI*, the ultimate ratio of the evanescent lines *Pm*, *Pn*, *Po*, *Pq*—that is, the ratio of the momentary changes

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof from page 201, establishing the ratio between the length of a cycloid and the versed sine of its generating arc.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

of the curve *AP*, the straight line *CP*, the circular arc *BP*, and the straight line *VP*, will be the same as the ratios of the lines *PV*, *PF*, *PG*, and *PI*, respectively. But since *VF* is perpendicular to *CF*, and *VH* is perpendicular to *CV*, the angles *HVG* and *VCF* are therefore equal; and because the angle *VHG* (due to the angles of the quadrilateral figure *HVEP* being right angles at *V* and *P*) is equal to the angle *CEP*, the triangles *VHG* and *CEP* will be similar. From this it follows that as *EP* is to *CE*, so *HG* is to *HV* (or *HP*), and likewise *KI* is to *KP*. By composition or division, as *CB* is to *CE*, so *PI* is to *PK*; and by doubling the consequents, as *CB* is to 2 *CE*, so *PI* is to *PV*, and so *Pq* is to *Pm*. Therefore, the decrease of the line *VP*—that is, the increase of the line *BV — VP*—relative to the increase of the curve line *AP*, is in the given ratio of *CB* to 2 *CE*. Consequently (by Corollary to Lemma 4), the lengths *BV — VP* and *AP*, generated by those increments, are in the same ratio. But if *BV* is the radius, *VP* is the cosine of the angle *BVP* (or 1/2 *BEP*), and therefore *BV — VP* is the <term>versed sine</term> <note>The versed sine, or versine, is 1 minus the cosine of an angle.</note> of the same angle. Therefore, in this wheel whose radius is 1/2 *BV*, *BV — VP* will be double the versed sine of the arc 1/2 *BP*. Therefore, *AP* is to double the versed sine of the arc 1/2 *BP* as 2 *CE* is to *CB*. *Q. E. D.*

For the sake of distinction, we shall name the line *AP* in the former of these propositions the <term>cycloid without the globe</term>, and the other in the latter proposition the <term>cycloid within the globe</term>.

## PROPOSITION L. PROBLEM XXXIII.

### *To cause a pendulous body to oscillate in a given cycloid.*

Let there be given within the globe *QVS* (Plate 19, Figure 3), described with the center *C*, the cycloid *QRS*, bisected at *R*, and meeting the surface of the globe at its extreme points *Q* and *S* on either side. Let *CR* be drawn bisecting the arc *QS* at *O*, and let it be extended to *A* in such a way that *CA* is to *CO* as *CO* is to *CR*. Around the center *C*, with the distance *CA*, let an exterior globe *DAF* be described; and within this globe, by a wheel whose diameter is *AO*, let two semi-cycloids *AQ* and *AS* be described, touching the interior globe at *Q* and *S*, and meeting the exterior globe at *A*. From that point *A*, with a thread *APT* equal in length to the line *AR*, let the body *T* hang and oscillate between the two semi-cycloids *AQ* and *AS* in such a way that as often as the pendulum moves away from the perpendicular *AR*, the upper part of the thread *AP* is applied to that semi-cycloid *APS* toward which the motion tends, and wraps itself around that curved line as if it were a solid obstacle,

stacle; the remaining part of the same thread **PT**, which has not yet touched the semi-cycloid, continues in a straight line. Then the weight **T** will oscillate in the given cycloid **QRS**. *Q.E.F.* <note>Quod erat faciendum: Which was to be done.</note>

For let the thread **PT** meet the cycloid **QRS** at **T**, and the circle **QOS** at **V**, and let **CV** be drawn; and to the straight part of the thread **PT**, let the perpendiculars **BP** and **TW** be erected from the end points **P** and **T**, meeting the straight line **CV** at **B** and **W**. It is evident from the construction and generation of the similar figures **AS** and **SR** that those perpendiculars **PB** and **TW** cut off from **CV** the lengths **VB** and **VW**, which are equal to the diameters of the wheels **OA** and **OR**. Therefore, **TP** is to **VP** (which is twice the sine of the angle **VBP** when $\frac{1}{2}$ **BV** is the radius) as **BW** is to **BV**, or **AQ** + **OR** to **AO**. That is (since **CA** and **CO**, **CO** and **CR**, and by division **AO** and **OR** are proportional) as **CA** + **CO** is to **CA**, or, if **BV** is bisected at **E**, as 2 **CE** is to **CB**. Therefore (by Corollary 1, Proposition 49), the length of the straight part of the thread **PT** is always equal to the arc of the cycloid **PS**, and the entire thread **APT** is always equal to half of the cycloid **APS**, which is (by Corollary 2, Proposition 49) equal to the length **AR**. And therefore, conversely, if the string always remains equal to the length **AR**, the point **T** will always move in the given cycloid **QRS**. *Q.E.D.* <note>Quod erat demonstrandum: Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

**COROLLARY.** The string **AR** is equal to the semi-cycloid **AS**, and therefore has the same ratio to **AC** (the radius of the exterior globe) as the similar semi-cycloid **SR** has to **CO** (the radius of the interior globe).

### PROPOSITION LI. THEOREM XVIII.

*If a centripetal force tending on all sides to the center* C *of a globe (Pl. 19. Fig. 4.) is in all places proportional to the distance of the place from the center, and by this force alone acting upon it, the body* T *oscillates (in the manner described above) in the perimeter of the cycloid* QRS*; I say, that all the oscillations, however unequal they may be in themselves, will be performed in equal times.*

For upon the tangent *TW* infinitely produced, let fall the perpendicular *CX* and join *CT*. Because the centripetal force with which the body *T* is impelled toward *C* is proportional to the distance *CT*, let this (by Corollary 2 of the Laws) be resolved into the parts *CX* and *TX*. Of these, *CX*—impelling the body directly from *P*—stretches the thread *PT*, and by the resistance the thread makes to it, is totally employed, producing no other effect; but the other part *TX*, impelling the body transversely or toward *X*, directly accelerates the motion in the cycloid. Then it is plain that the acceleration of the body, proportional to this accelerating force, will be every moment proportional to the length *TX*; that is (because *CV*, *WV*, and *TX*, *TW* proportional to them are given), proportional to the length *TW*, which is (by Corollary 1, Proposition 49) proportional to the length of the arc of the cycloid *TR*. If

<meta>catchword: there-</meta>

<summary>Newton demonstrates that in a centripetal force field proportional to distance, oscillations within a cycloid are isochronous, meaning they take the same amount of time regardless of their size. He proves this by showing that the accelerating force along the cycloid is proportional to the body's distance from the center of the oscillation along the arc.</summary>
<keywords>centripetal force, cycloid, oscillation, isochronism, acceleration, geometry, Newton, Proposition 51</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>This page completes the proof of Proposition 51, Theorem 18, demonstrating that the time taken for a pendulum to reach the equilibrium point in a cycloidal path is independent of the initial displacement.</meta>

# Mathematical Principles Book I.

therefore if two pendulums *APT* and *Apt* are unequally drawn aside from the perpendicular *AR* and released together, their accelerations will always be proportional to the arcs to be described, *TR* and *tR*. But the parts described at the beginning of the motion are proportional to the accelerations—that is, proportional to the whole lengths that are to be described at the beginning; and therefore the parts which remain to be described, and the subsequent accelerations proportional to those parts, are also proportional to the wholes, and so on. Therefore the accelerations, and consequently the velocities generated, the parts described with those velocities, and the parts remaining to be described, are always proportional to the wholes; and therefore the parts to be described, maintaining a given ratio to each other, will vanish together—that is, the two oscillating bodies will arrive together at the perpendicular *AR*. And since, on the other hand, the ascent of the pendulums from the lowest point *R* through the same cycloidal arcs with a reverse motion is retarded in the several places they pass through by the same forces by which their descent was accelerated, it is plain that the velocities of their ascent and descent through the same arcs are equal, and consequently performed in equal times; and therefore since the two parts of the cycloid *RS* and *RQ* lying on either side of the perpendicular are similar and equal, the two pendulums will perform both the whole oscillations and the halves of their oscillations in the same times. *Q. E. D.*

COROLLARY. The force with which the body *T* is accelerated or retarded in any place *T* of the cycloid is to the whole weight of the same body in the highest place *S* or *Q* as the arc of the cycloid *TR* is to the arc *SR* or *QR*.

<summary>Newton concludes the proof that cycloidal oscillations are isochronous by showing that acceleration remains proportional to the arc distance remaining. He adds a corollary relating the accelerating force at any point to the body's total weight at its highest point.</summary>

About any center *G* (Plate 20, Figure 1) with the distance *GH* equal to the arc of the cycloid *RS*, describe a semicircle *HKM* bisected by the radius *GK*. **Cor. 1.** From this also, the times of oscillating, fal-

themselves. For if the diameter of the wheel with

supposed equal to the radius of the globe, the

center of the globe, and the oscillation will be changed

line. From this, there is given both the time of the

describes an arc of a quadrant. For this time (by
case 2) is to the time of half the oscillation in any
cycloid $QRS$ as 1 to $\sqrt{\frac{AR}{AC}}$.

<summary>This page concludes Proposition 52 by establishing that the period of oscillation depends on the length of the string, the distance to the center of the globe, and the absolute force. <meta>This page continues the second corollary of Proposition 52, concluding the discussion on how cycloidal motion relates to spherical surfaces and the Earth's gravity.</meta>

# Mathematical Principles Book I.

the common cycloid. For if the diameter of the globe is infinitely increased, its spherical surface will be changed into a plane, and the centripetal force will act uniformly in the direction of lines perpendicular to that plane; and this cycloid of ours will become the same as the common cycloid. But in that case, the length of the arc of the cycloid between that plane and the describing point will become equal to four times the versed sine of half the arc of the wheel between the same plane and the describing point, as was discovered by Sir *Christopher Wren*. And a pendulum between two such cycloids will oscillate in a similar and equal cycloid in equal times, as Mr. *Huygens* demonstrated. The descent of heavy bodies also, in the time of one oscillation, will be the same as Mr. *Huygens* showed.

The propositions demonstrated here are adapted to the true constitution of the Earth, insofar as wheels moving in any of its great circles will describe cycloids outside the globe by the motions of nails fixed in their perimeters; and pendulums in mines and deep caverns of the Earth must oscillate in cycloids within the globe so that those oscillations may be performed in equal times. For gravity (as will be shown in the third book) decreases in its progress from the surface of the Earth: upwards in a <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>an inverse square ratio</note> of the distances from the center of the Earth; downwards in a simple ratio of the same.

<summary>This page concludes the comparison between general cycloids and the common cycloid, noting how the theories of Wren and Huygens apply when a globe's surface is treated as a plane. It also explains how these principles apply to Earth's gravity, which changes differently above and below the surface.</summary>

## PROPOSITION LIII. PROBLEM XXXV.

Assuming the quadratures <note>the calculation of areas or lengths related to curves</note> of curvilinear figures, it is required to find the forces with which bodies moving in given curve lines may always complete their oscillations in equal times.

Let the body *T* (Plate 20, Figure 2) oscillate in any curve line *STRQ*, whose axis is *AR* passing through the center of force *C*. Draw *TX* tangent to that curve at any position of the body *T*, and on that tangent *TX* take *TY* equal to the arc *TR*. The length of that arc is known from the common methods used for the quadratures of figures. From the point *Y*, draw the straight line *YZ* perpendicular to the tangent. Draw *CT* meeting that perpendicular at *Z*, and the centripetal force will be proportional to the straight line *TZ*.
Q. E. I. <note>Quod Erat Inveniendum: Which was to be found.</note>

For if the force with which the body is attracted from *T* towards *C* is expressed by the straight line *TZ* taken proportional to it, that force will be resolved into two forces *TY* and *YZ*. Of these, *YZ*, pulling the body in the direction of the length of the thread *PT*, does not change its motion at all; whereas the other force *TY* directly accelerates or decelerates its motion in the curve *STRQ*. Therefore, since that force is proportional to the distance to be described *TR*, the accelerations or decelerations of the body in describing two proportional parts (a greater and a

**COROLLARY 1.** Hence, if a body *T* (*Pl. 20. Fig. 3.*) hanging by a straight thread *AT* from the center *A* describes the circular arc *STRQ*, and in the meantime is acted on by any force tending downwards in parallel directions, which is to the uniform force of gravity as the arc *TR* is to its sine *TN*, the times of the various oscillations will be equal. Because *TZ* and *AR* are parallel, the triangles *ATN* and *ZTY* are similar; and therefore *TZ* will be to *AT* as *TY* is to *TN*. That is, if the uniform force of gravity is expressed by the given length *AT*, the force *TZ* by which the oscillations become <term>isochronous</term> will be to the force of gravity *AT* as the arc *TR* (equal to *TY*) is to *TN*, the sine of that arc.

**COROLLARY 2.** Therefore, in clocks, if forces were applied by some mechanism to the pendulum that maintains the motion, and combined with the force of gravity so that the total force tending downwards is always proportional to a line produced by applying the rectangle of the arc *TR* and the radius *AR* to the sine *TN* <note>meaning the area of the rectangle formed by the arc and radius, divided by the sine</note>, all the oscillations will become <term>isochronous</term>.

### PROPOSITION LIV. PROBLEM XXXVI.

*Assuming the quadratures of curvilinear figures are known, it is required to find the times in which bodies, by means of any centripetal force, will descend or ascend along any curved lines described in a plane passing through the center of force.*

Let the body descend from any place *S* (*Pl. 20. Fig. 4.*) and move along any given curve *STtR* in a plane passing through the center of force *C*. From the center *C*, with the distances *CD* and *Cd*, let circles *DT* and *dt* be described, intersecting the curved line *STtR* at *T* and *t*. And because the law of centripetal force is known, as well as the altitude *CS* from which the body originally fell, the velocity of the body at any other altitude *CT* will be known (by Proposition 39). But the time in which the body describes the <term>lineola</term> <note>a very small line segment</note> *Tt* is proportional to the length of that <term>lineola</term>—that is, directly proportional to the secant of the angle *tTC* and inversely proportional to the velocity. Let the ordinate *DN*, which is proportional to this time, be drawn perpendicular to the straight line *CS* at point *D*; and since *Dd* is given, the rectangle *Dd* × *DN* (that is, the area *DNnd*) will be proportional to the same time. Therefore, if *PNn* is a curved line on which the point *N* is always found, and its

But with such a force (by Proposition 1), the area *AOP* will be swept out proportional to the time. *Q. E. D.*

## SECTION XI.

### Of the motions of bodies tending to each other with centripetal forces.

I have until now been treating the attractions of bodies toward an immovable center, though very probably there is no such thing existing in nature. For attractions are made toward bodies; and the actions of the bodies attracted and attracting are always reciprocal and equal by Law 3. So that if there are two bodies, neither the attracted nor the attracting body is truly at rest, but both (by Corollary 4 of the laws of motion), being as it were mutually attracted, revolve about a common center of gravity. And if there are more bodies—which are either attracted by a single one that is attracted by them in turn, or which all attract each other mutually—these bodies will move among themselves in such a way that their common center of gravity will either be at rest or move uniformly forward in a straight line. I shall therefore now proceed to treat the motion of bodies mutually attracting each other, considering the centripetal forces as attractions, although perhaps in a physical strictness they may more truly be called impulses.

<vocab>attractions, centripetal forces, center of gravity, laws of motion, impulses</vocab>

<summary>Newton introduces Section XI, shifting focus from bodies orbiting a fixed, immovable center to the more realistic scenario where bodies mutually attract one another. He explains that according to the third law of motion, all interacting bodies must move relative to their common center of gravity.</summary>
<keywords>centripetal forces, mutual attraction, Law 3, Corollary 4, center of gravity, impulses, orbital mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on centripetal forces, transitioning into the mathematical proofs of Proposition 57 regarding the motion of two mutually attracting bodies.</meta>

<header>SECT. XI. of Natural Philosophy.</header>
<meta>catchword: PROP-</meta>

impulses. But these propositions are to be considered as purely mathematical; and therefore, laying aside all physical considerations, I make use of a familiar way of speaking to make myself more easily understood by a mathematical reader.

### PROPOSITION LVII. THEOREM XX.

*Two bodies attracting each other mutually describe similar figures about their common center of gravity, and about each other mutually.*

For the distances of the bodies from their common center of gravity are reciprocally proportional to the bodies, and therefore in a given ratio to each other; and from there, by the composition of ratios, in a given ratio to the whole distance between the bodies. Now these distances revolve about their common endpoint with a uniform angular motion because, lying in the same straight line, they never change their inclination toward each other. But straight lines that are in a given ratio to each other, and revolve about their endpoints with an equal angular motion, describe figures on planes—which either remain at rest with those endpoints or move with any motion that is not angular—that are entirely similar around those endpoints. Therefore, the figures described by the revolution of these distances are similar. *Q. E. D.*

<vocab>Natural Philosophy, center of gravity, ratio, angular motion, similar figures, attraction</vocab>

<summary>Newton clarifies that his propositions are intended as mathematical models rather than physical descriptions and proves that two mutually attracting bodies describe similar paths relative to their common center of gravity.</summary>
<keywords>Proposition 57, Theorem 20, center of gravity, mutual attraction, similar figures, angular motion, mathematical principles, orbital geometry</keywords>

### PROPOSITION LVIII. THEOREM XXI.

*If two bodies attract each other mutually with forces of any kind and meanwhile revolve around the common center of gravity, I say that by those same forces, one can describe around either body (considered stationary) a figure similar and equal to the figures which the bodies so moving describe relative to each other.*

Let the bodies $S$ and $P$ ($Pl. 20. Fig. 7.$) revolve about their common center of gravity $C$, moving from $S$ to $T$ and from $P$ to $Q$. From a given point $s$, let there be continually drawn lines $sp$ and $sq$ that are equal and parallel to $SP$ and $TQ$. The curve $pqv$, which the point $p$ describes in its revolution around the stationary point $s$, will be similar and equal to the curves that the bodies $S$ and $P$ describe relative to each other; therefore, by Theorem 20, it is similar to the curves $ST$ and $PQV$ which the same bodies describe about their common center of gravity $C$. This is because the ratios of the lines $SC, CP,$ and $SP$ (or $sp$) to each other are fixed.

### PROPOSITION LIX. THEOREM XXII.

*The <term>periodic time</term> of two bodies S and P revolving around their common <term>center of gravity</term> C, is to the periodic time of one of the bodies P revolving around the other S (while S remains unmoved and P describes a figure similar and equal to those which the bodies describe about each other mutually), in a <term>subduplicate ratio</term> <note>A ratio of the square roots.</note> of the other body S to the sum of the bodies S+P.*

For by the demonstration of the last proposition, the times in which any similar arcs *PQ* and *pq* are described, are in a <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the distances *CP* and *SP* or *sp*, that is, in a subduplicate ratio of the body *S* to the sum of the bodies *S+P*. And by <term>composition of ratios</term>, the sums of the times in which all the similar arcs *PQ* and *pq* are described—that is, the whole times in which the complete similar figures are described—are in the same subduplicate ratio. *Q. E. D.*

<summary>This page defines the mathematical relationship between the orbital periods of two bodies moving around a common center of gravity compared to one body orbiting a stationary one. It proves that this relationship is a subduplicate ratio (the ratio of the square roots) involving the mass of one body and the combined mass of both.</summary>

$S + P$ and $S$ to $S + P$. And inversely, the principal axis of the ellipse described about the movable body will be to the principal axis of the one described around the immovable body as $S + P$ is to the first of two mean proportionals between $S + P$ and $S$.
*Q. E. D.*

## PROPOSITION LXI. THEOREM XXIV.

*If two bodies attracting each other with any kind of forces, and not otherwise disturbed or obstructed, are moved in any manner whatsoever; those motions will be the same as if they did not attract each other mutually at all, but were both attracted with the same forces by a third body placed in their common center of gravity; and the law of the attracting forces will be the same with respect to the distance of the bodies from the common center as it is with respect to the distance between the two bodies.*

For those forces with which the bodies mutually attract each other, by tending toward the bodies, also tend toward the common center of gravity lying directly between them; and therefore, they are the same as if they proceeded from an intermediate body.
*Q. E. D.*

<sig>Q</sig>
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<vocab>Natural Philosophy, Proposition, Theorem, ellipse, principal axis, mean proportionals, attracting forces, center of gravity</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a proof regarding the proportions of orbital axes and introduces a theorem stating that the mutual motion of two bodies can be treated as if they were both attracted by a single point at their common center of gravity. This simplifies the two-body problem by relating the forces to a fixed central point.</summary>
<keywords>Newtonian mechanics, celestial mechanics, two-body problem, center of gravity, elliptical orbits, principal axis, attractive forces</keywords>

--- Page 309 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Theorem XXIV (Proposition LXI), demonstrating that because the ratio of distances between the bodies and their common center of gravity is constant, the mathematical law governing the attracting force remains consistent whether measured from one body to the other or from a body to the common center of gravity.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

And because the ratio of the distance of either body from that common center to the distance between the two bodies is given, it follows that the ratio of any power of one distance to the same power of the other distance is also given. Furthermore, the ratio of any quantity derived in any way from one of the distances, combined in any way with given quantities, to another quantity derived in the same way from the other distance and an equal number of given quantities (having that same given ratio of the distances to the first) is also given. Therefore, if the force with which one body is attracted by another is directly or inversely proportional to the distance between the bodies, or to any power of that distance, or finally to any quantity derived in any way from that distance combined with given quantities; then the same force with which the same body is attracted to the common center of gravity will be, in the same way, directly or inversely proportional to the distance of the attracted body from the common center, or to any power of that distance, or finally to any quantity derived in the same way from that distance combined with analogous given quantities. That is, the law of attracting force will be the same with respect to both distances. *Q. E. D.*

<vocab>ratio, distance, common center, center of gravity, law of attracting force</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the proof that the law of attraction between two bodies remains mathematically identical whether calculated based on the distance between them or their distance from their mutual center of gravity. This allows complex two-body interactions to be treated as simpler central-force problems.</summary>

## PROPOSITION LXII. PROBLEM XXXVIII.

*To determine the motions of two bodies that attract each other with forces inversely proportional to the squares of the distance between them, and are dropped from given locations.*

According to the previous theorem, the bodies will move in the same manner as if they were attracted by a third body placed at their common center of gravity; and by the hypothesis, that center will be at rest at the beginning of their motion, and therefore (by corollary 4 of the laws of motion) will always remain at rest. The motions of the bodies are therefore to be determined (by problem 25) in the same way as if they were impelled by forces tending toward that center; and thus we shall have the motions of the bodies mutually attracting each other. *Q. E. I.*

E. I.*

## PROPOSITION LXV. THEOREM XXV.

**Bodies, whose forces decrease in a duplicate ratio of their distances from their centers, may move among themselves in ellipses; and by radii drawn to the foci may describe areas very nearly proportional to the times.**

In the last proposition, we demonstrated that case in which the motions will be performed exactly in ellipses. The further the law of the forces is from the law in that case, the more the bodies will disturb each other's motions; nor is it possible that bodies attracting each other mutually according to the law supposed in this proposition should move exactly in ellipses, unless they maintain a certain proportion of distances from each other. However, in the following cases, the orbits will not differ much from ellipses.

**CASE I.** Imagine several lesser bodies to revolve about some very great one at different distances from it, and suppose absolute forces tending to every one of the bodies, proportional to each. And because (by Cor. 4 of the laws) the common center of gravity of them all is either at rest or moves uniformly forward in a straight line, suppose the lesser bodies so small that the great body may never be at a perceptible distance from that center; and then the great body will, without any perceptible

**COROLLARY 1.** In the second case, the closer the very great body approaches the system of two or more revolving bodies, the greater the perturbation of the motions of the system's parts among themselves will be; because the inclinations of the lines drawn from that great body to those parts become greater, and the inequality of the proportion is also greater.

For if the accelerative force, acting in parallel directions and equally, causes no perturbation in the motions of the parts of the system, it must of course, when it acts unequally, cause a perturbation somewhere, which will be greater or less as the inequality is greater or less. The excess of the greater impulses acting upon some bodies, and not acting upon others, must necessarily change their situation among themselves. And this perturbation, added to the perturbation arising from the inequality and inclination of the lines, makes the whole perturbation greater.

**COROLLARY 3.** Hence if the parts of this system move in ellipses or circles without any remarkable perturbation, it is manifest that if they are at all impelled by accelerative forces tending toward any other bodies, the impulse is very weak, or else is impressed nearly equally and in parallel directions upon all of them.

With these things established, it is evident that the areas are most nearly proportional to the times when that third force is as small as possible, while the rest maintain their former magnitude; and that the orbit *PAB* approaches nearest to the aforementioned elliptical figure when both the second and third forces—but especially the third—are as small as possible, while the first force remains at its previous magnitude.

# SECT. XI. of *<term>Natural Philosophy</term>*.

(in)clined to the plane of the orbit *PAB*) besides the <term>perturbation</term> of the motion just mentioned regarding <term>longitude</term>, introduces another <term>perturbation</term> regarding <term>latitude</term> as well, attracting the body *P* out of the plane of its orbit. **COR. 1.** Hence it may be easily gathered that if several smaller bodies *P, S, R,* etc. revolve about a very large body *T*; the motion of the innermost revolving body *P* will be least disturbed by the attractions of the others when the large body is attracted and moved by the rest (according to the ratio of the <term>accelerative forces</term>) just as much as the rest are by each other mutually.

**COR. 2.** In a system of three bodies *T, P, S,* if the <term>accelerative forces</term> of any two of them toward a third are inversely proportional to each other as the squares of the distances; the body *P*, by the radius *PT*, will sweep out its area more quickly near the <term>conjunction</term> *A* and the <term>opposition</term> *B*, than it will near the <term>quadratures</term> *C* and *D*. For every force with which the body *P* is acted on and the body *T* is not, and which does not act in the direction of the line *PT*, either accelerates or retards the sweeping out of the area, according to how it is directed, whether <term>*in consequentia*</term> or <term>*in antecedentia*</term>. Such is the force *NM*. This force, in the passage of the body *P* from *C* to *A*, is directed <term>*in consequentia*</term> to its motion, and therefore accelerates it; then as far as *D* <term>*in antecedentia*</term>, and retards

**COR.** 4. The orbit of the body *P*, *<term>ceteris paribus</term>*, is more curved at the <term>quadratures</term> than at the <term>conjunction</term> and <term>opposition</term>. For the swifter bodies move, the less they deflect from a rectilinear path. And besides, the force *KL*, or *NM*, at the <term>conjunction</term> and <term>opposition</term>, is contrary to the force with which the body *T* attracts the body *P*; and therefore diminishes that force; but the body *P* will deflect less from a rectilinear path the less it is impelled toward the body *T*.

**COR.** 5. Hence the body *P*, *<term>ceteris paribus</term>*, goes farther from the body *T* at the <term>quadratures</term> than at the <term>conjunction</term> and <term>opposition</term>. This is said, however, assuming no regard is had for the motion of <term>eccentricity</term>. For if the orbit of the body *P* is eccentric, its <term>eccentricity</term> (as will be shown presently by Cor. 9) will be greatest when the <term>apsides</term> are in the <term>syzygies</term>; and from there it may sometimes come to pass that the body *P*, in its near approach to the farther <term>apsis</term>, may go farther from the body *T* at the <term>syzygies</term> than at the <term>quadratures</term>.
