For the force with which the body *P* is urged toward the body *T* at the quadratures, where the force *MN* vanishes, is composed of the force *LM* and the centripetal force with which the body *T* attracts the body *P*. The first force *LM*, if the distance *PT* is increased, increases in nearly the same proportion as that distance, and the other force decreases in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>the square</note> of that distance; and therefore the sum of these two forces decreases in less than the duplicate ratio of the distance *PT*. Therefore, by Cor. 1, Prop. 45, it will cause the line of the <term>apsides</term>, or, which is the same thing, the upper <term>apsis</term>, to move backward. But at the conjunction and opposition, the force with which the body *P* is urged toward the body *T* is the difference between the force *KL* and the force with which the body *T* attracts the body *P*; and that difference—because the force *KL* increases very nearly in the ratio of the distance *PT*—decreases in more than the duplicate ratio of the distance *PT*. Therefore, by Cor. 1, Prop. 45, it causes the line of the apsides to move forward. In the places between the <term>syzygies</term> and the <term>quadratures</term>, the motion of the line of the apsides depends upon both these causes together, so that it moves either forward or backward in proportion to the excess of one of these causes over the other. Therefore, since the force *KL* in the syzygies is almost twice as great as the force *LM* in the quadratures, the excess will be on the side of the force *KL*, and consequently the line of the apsides will be carried forward. It is manifest that in its motion from the node *C*, the body recedes continually from the former plane *CD* of its orbit until it comes to the next node; and therefore at that node, being now at its greatest distance from the first plane *CD*, it will pass through the plane of the orbit *EST* not at *D*, the other node of that plane, but at a point that lies nearer to the body *S*, which therefore becomes a new place of the node in <term>antecedentia</term> <note>moving backward, opposite the order of the zodiac signs</note> to its former place. And by a similar reasoning, the nodes will continue to recede in their passage from this node to the next. The nodes, therefore, when situated in the <term>quadratures</term> recede perpetually, and at the <term>syzygies</term>, where no perturbation <note>disturbance</note> can be produced in the motion as to latitude, they are quiescent <note>stationary</note>; in the intermediate places they partake of both conditions, and recede more slowly; and therefore, being always either retrograde or stationary, they will be carried backwards, or in <term>antecedentia</term>, during each revolution.

**COR.** 12. All the errors described in these corollaries are a little greater at the conjunction of the bodies *P* and *S* than at their opposition; because the generating forces *NM* and *ML* are greater.

**Cor. 14.** But since the forces $NM$ and $ML$, when the body $S$ is exceedingly distant, are very nearly as the force $SK$ and the ratio of $PT$ to $ST$ combined—that is, if both the distance $PT$ and the absolute force of the body $S$ are given, as the cube of $ST$ reciprocally—and since those forces $NM$ and $ML$ are the causes of all the errors and effects treated of in the previous corollaries, it is manifest that all those effects (if the system of bodies $T$ and $P$ continues as before, and only the distance $ST$ and the absolute force of the body $S$ are changed) will be very nearly in a ratio compounded of the direct ratio of the absolute force of the body $S$ and the triplicate inverse ratio <note>the inverse cube</note> of the distance $ST$. That is, all the linear errors will be proportional to the diameters of the orbits; the angular errors will be the same as before; and the times of similar linear errors, or equal angular errors, will be proportional to the periodic times of the orbits.

**COR. 16.** Therefore, if the shapes of the orbits and their inclination to each other are given, and the magnitudes, forces, and distances of the bodies are changed in any way, we may—from the errors and the timing of those errors in one case—determine very nearly the errors and their timing in any other case. But this may be done more quickly by the following method. The forces *N M* and *M L*, other things remaining unchanged, are proportional to the radius *T P*; and their periodic effects (by Cor. 2, Lem. 10) are proportional to the forces and the square of the periodic time of the body *P* combined. These are the linear errors of the body *P*; and from this, the angular errors as they appear from the center *T* (that is, the motion of the <term>apsides</term> and of the <term>nodes</term>, and all the apparent errors in longitude and latitude) are, in each revolution of the body *P*, nearly proportional to the square of the time of the revolution. The increase or diminution of the <term>eccentricity</term> and <term>inclination</term> of the <term>orbit</term> *PAB* makes no sensible variation in the motions of the <term>apsides</term> and <term>nodes</term>, unless that increase or diminution be very great indeed.

**COR. 17.** Since the line *LM* becomes sometimes greater and sometimes less than the radius *PT*, let the mean quantity of the force *LM* be expressed by that radius *PT*; and then that mean force will be to the mean force *SK* or *SN* (which may be also expressed by *ST*) as the length *PT* to the length *ST*. The periodic times therefore being given, together with the distance *PT*, the mean force *LM* is also given; and that force being given, there is given also the force *MN*

**Cor. 18.** By the same laws by which the body *P* revolves about the body *T*, let us suppose many fluid bodies to move around *T* at equal distances from it; and to be so numerous that they may all become contiguous to each other, so as to form a fluid <term>annulus</term> or ring, of a round shape and concentric with the body *T*. The several parts of this annulus, performing their motions by the same law as the body *P*, will draw nearer to the body *T* and move faster in the <term>conjunction</term> and <term>opposition</term> of themselves and the body *S* than in the <term>quadratures</term>. And the <term>nodes</term> of this annulus, or its intersections with the plane of the orbit of the body *S* or *T*, will remain stationary at the <term>syzygies</term>; but outside of the syzygies, they will be carried backward, or *in antecedentia*, with the greatest speed in the quadratures, and more slowly in other places. The inclination of this annulus will also vary, and its axis will oscillate during each revolution; when the revolution is completed, it will return to its former position, except only that it will be carried around a little by the <term>precession</term> of the nodes.

### SECT. XI. of Natural Philosophy.

The greatest angle of the inclination of the single annulus occurs when the nodes are in the syzygies. From there, as the nodes progress toward the quadratures, it strives to diminish its inclination, and by that effort, it impresses a motion upon the whole globe. The globe retains this impressed motion until the annulus, through a contrary effort, destroys that motion and impresses a new motion in the opposite direction. By this means, the greatest motion of the decreasing inclination happens when the nodes are in the quadratures; and the smallest angle of inclination occurs in the octants after the quadratures. Again, the greatest motion of reclination happens when the nodes are in the syzygies; and the greatest angle of reclination occurs in the following octants. The case is the same for a globe without this annulus, if it is a little higher or a little denser in the equatorial than in the polar regions. For the excess of matter in the regions near the equator takes the place of the annulus.

And even if we should suppose the centripetal force of this globe to be increased in any way so that all its parts tended downward, just as the parts of our Earth gravitate to the center, the phenomena of this and the preceding corollary would scarcely be altered; except that the locations of the greatest and least height of the water would be different. For the water is now no longer sustained and kept in its orbit by its centrifugal force, but by the channel in which it flows. Additionally, the force *LM* attracts the water downward most in the quadratures, and the force *KL* or *NM — LM* attracts it upward most in the syzygies. These combined forces cease to attract the water downward and begin to attract it upward in the octants before the syzygies; and

**COR. 21.** For the same reason that excess matter in the equatorial regions of a globe causes the nodes to move backward—and therefore, by the increase of that matter, that <term>retrogradation</term> is increased, by its reduction it is diminished, and by its removal it completely ceases—it follows that if more than that excess matter is removed (that is, if the globe is either more flattened or of a less dense consistency near the equator than near the poles), there will arise a motion of the nodes in <term>*consequentia*</term>.

**COR. 22.** And from there, the composition of the globe is known from the motion of the nodes. That is, if the globe unalterably retains the same poles and the motion of the nodes is in <term>*antecedentia*</term>, there is an excess of matter near the equator; but if in <term>*consequentia*</term>, there is a deficiency. ### SECT. XI. *of Natural Philosophy.*

It is obvious that, by its own force, it will never change its axis or its inclination. Now, let this globe be struck obliquely by a new impulse on the same part of its surface as before; and since the effect of an impulse is not changed at all by whether it occurs sooner or later, it is obvious that these two successively applied impulses will produce the same motion as if they were applied at the same time; that is, the same motion as if the globe had been struck by a single force composed of them both (according to Corollary 2 of the Laws), which is a simple motion about an axis with a specific inclination. And the case is the same if the second impulse were applied to any other location on the equator of the first motion; and also if the first impulse were applied to any location on the equator of the motion that would have been generated by the second impulse alone; and therefore also when both impulses are applied in any locations whatsoever. For these impulses will generate the same circular motion as if they were applied together and at once at the intersection of the equators of those motions that would have been generated by each impulse separately. Therefore, a homogeneous and perfect globe will not maintain several distinct motions but will unite all those applied to it and reduce them into one; revolving, as far as it is able, always with a simple and uniform motion about one single given axis, with an inclination that is perpetually unchanging. And the inclination of the axis or the velocity of the rotation will not be changed by centripetal force. For if the globe is assumed to be divided into two hemispheres by any plane passing

Neither can this enormous <term>evagation</term> <note>wandering or deviation</note> of the poles be corrected, unless by placing that mountain either in one of the poles, in which case by Cor. 21, the nodes of the equator will go forward; or in the equatorial regions, in which case by Cor. 20, the nodes will go backward; or lastly by adding on the other side of the axis a new quantity of matter, by which the mountain may be balanced in its motion; and then the nodes will either go forward or backward, as the mountain and this newly added matter happen to be nearer to the pole or to the equator.

This may be demonstrated in the same way as Proposition 66, but by more lengthy reasoning, which I therefore omit. It will be sufficient to consider it in this way. From the demonstration of the last proposition, it is clear that the center toward which body *S* is urged by the two forces combined is very near the common center of gravity of those two other bodies. If this center were to coincide with that common center, and furthermore if the common center of gravity of all three bodies were at rest, body *S* on one side and the common center of gravity of the other two bodies on the other side would describe true ellipses around that stationary common center. This is shown by Corollary 2 of Proposition 58, compared with what was demonstrated in Propositions 64 and 65. Now, this precise elliptical motion will be slightly disturbed by the distance of the center of the two bodies from the center toward which the third body, *S*, is attracted. If we also add motion to the common center of the three bodies, the perturbation will increase even more. Therefore, the perturbation is least when the common center of the three bodies is at rest—that is, when the innermost and largest body, *T*, is attracted according to the same law as the others. It is always greatest when the common center of the three, through the reduction of the motion of body *T*, begins to move and is more and more agitated.

*absolute forces of the attracting bodies* A *and* B *will be to each other, as*
*those very bodies* A *and* B *to which*
*those forces belong.*

For the accelerative attractions of all the bodies *B, C, D,* towards *A* are, by the supposition, equal to each other at equal distances; and in like manner, the accelerative attractions of all the bodies towards *B* are also equal to each other at equal distances. But the absolute attractive force of the body *A* is to the absolute attractive force of the body *B* as the accelerative attraction of all the bodies towards *A* is to the accelerative attraction of all the bodies towards *B* at equal distances; and so also is the accelerative attraction of the body *B* towards *A* to the accelerative attraction of the body *A* towards *B.* But the accelerative attraction of the body *B* towards *A* is to the accelerative attraction of the body *A* towards *B* as the mass of the body *A* is to the mass of the body *B;* because the motive forces (which, by the 2nd, 7th, and 8th definitions, are as the accelerative forces and the attracted bodies taken together) are here equal to one another by the third law. Therefore, the absolute attractive force of the body *A* is to the absolute attractive force of the body *B* as the mass of the body *A* to the mass of the body *B. Q.E.D.*

COROLLARY 1. Therefore, if each of the bodies of the system *A, B, C, D,* etc. singly attracts all the rest with accelerative forces that are inversely as the squares of the distances from the attracting body, the absolute forces of all those bodies will be to each other as the bodies themselves.

**COROLLARY 2.** By similar reasoning, if each of the bodies of the system *A, B, C, D*, etc., singly attracts all the rest with accelerative forces which are either inversely or directly in the ratio of any power whatever of the distances from the attracting body, or which are defined by the distances from each of the attracting bodies according to any common law; it is clear that the absolute forces of those bodies are as the bodies themselves.

<margin>*</margin>
**COROLLARY 3.** In a system of bodies whose forces decrease in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>The square of the ratio; specifically, the inverse-square law.</note> of the distances, if the smaller bodies revolve about one very large one in ellipses having their common focus in the center of that large body and of an exceedingly accurate figure; and moreover, by radii drawn to that large body, describe areas exactly proportional to the times; the absolute forces of those bodies to each other will be either accurately or very nearly in the ratio of the bodies. And so on the contrary. This appears from the corollary of proposition 68, compared with the first corollary of this proposition.

### SCHOLIUM.

These propositions naturally lead us to the analogy that exists between centripetal forces and the central bodies to which those forces are usually directed. For it is reasonable to suppose that forces which are directed toward bodies should depend upon the nature and quantity of those bodies, as we see they do in magnetical experiments. And when such cases occur, we are to compute the attractions of the bodies by assigning to each of their particles its proper force, and then collecting the sum of

<meta>catchword: them</meta>

<summary>Newton extends the proportionality of absolute force and mass to any common law of distance and relates these mathematical findings to physical analogies like magnetism. He concludes that total attraction should be calculated by summing the individual forces of a body's constituent particles.</summary>
<keywords>centripetal forces, absolute forces, inverse-square law, duplicate ratio, magnetism, particle attraction, Scholium, Isaac Newton</keywords>

--- Page 345 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Scholium from the previous section, where the author clarifies his terminology and methodology before transitioning to the study of spherical bodies.</meta>

# Mathematical Principles Book I.

262

them all. I use the word attraction here in general for any endeavor, of whatever kind, made by bodies to approach each other; whether that endeavor arises from the action of the bodies themselves as they mutually tend toward or agitate each other by emitted spirits; or whether it arises from the action of the ether or of the air, or of any medium whatsoever, whether corporeal or incorporeal, in any way impelling bodies placed within it toward each other. In the same general sense, I use the word impulse, not defining the types or physical qualities of forces in this treatise, but investigating their quantities and mathematical proportions, as I observed before in the definitions.

In mathematics, we are to investigate the quantities of forces and their proportions resulting from any conditions supposed; then, when we enter upon physics, we compare those proportions with the phenomena of Nature, so that we may know what conditions of those forces correspond to the various kinds of attractive bodies. And once this preparation is made, we may argue more safely concerning the physical types, causes, and proportions of the forces. Let us see then with what forces spherical bodies—consisting of particles endowed with attractive powers in the manner spoken of above—must act mutually upon one another, and what kind of motions will follow from that.

<summary>Newton defines "attraction" and "impulse" as mathematical terms for the tendency of bodies to approach each other, regardless of their physical cause. He explains his method of first establishing mathematical proportions before applying them to physical phenomena and introduces the upcoming study of spherical bodies.</summary>

### PROPOSITION LXX. THEOREM XXX.

*If equal centripetal forces, decreasing in the square of the distances from those points, tend toward every point of a spherical surface; I say that a corpuscle placed within that surface will not be attracted by those forces in any way.*

Let HIKL (*Pl. 21. Fig. 4.*) be that spherical surface, and *P* a corpuscle placed within. Through *P* let there be drawn to this surface the two lines *HK* and *IL*, intercepting very small arcs *HI* and *KL*; and because (by Corollary 3, Lemma 7) the triangles *HPI* and *LPK* are similar, those arcs will be proportional

--- Page 347 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition 70, demonstrating that the net gravitational force inside a spherical shell is zero, and begins Proposition 71, which examines the force exerted by a spherical surface on a point outside of it.</meta>

<language>English</language>

proportional to the distances *HF* and *LP*; and any particles at *HI* and *KL* of the spherical surface, bounded by straight lines passing through *P*, will be in the duplicate ratio [the ratio of the squares] of those distances. Therefore, the forces of these particles exerted upon the body *P* are equal to each other. For the forces are directly as the particles and inversely as the squares of the distances. And these two ratios compose the ratio of equality. The attractions, therefore, being made equally towards opposite parts, destroy each other. And by similar reasoning, all the attractions through the entire spherical surface are destroyed by contrary attractions. Therefore, the body *P* will not be impelled in any way by those attractions.
->*Q. E. D.*<-

### PROPOSITION LXXI. THEOREM XXXI.

*Assuming the same conditions as above, I say that a corpuscle placed outside the spherical surface is attracted towards the center of the sphere with a force inversely proportional to the square of its distance from that center.*

For imagine two corpuscles to be separately attracted by two spheres, one by one and the other by the other, and their distances from the centers of the spheres to be proportional to the diameters of the spheres respectively; and the spheres to be broken down into similar particles arranged in a similar position to the corpuscles. Then the attractions of one corpuscle toward the several particles of one sphere will be to the attractions of the other toward as many corresponding particles of the other sphere in a ratio compounded of the ratio of the particles directly and the square ratio <note>duplicate ratio</note> of the distances inversely. But the particles are as the spheres, that is, in a cube ratio <note>triplicate ratio</note> of the diameters, and the distances are as the diameters; and the first ratio directly with the last ratio taken twice inversely, becomes the ratio of diameter to diameter. *Q. E. D.*

### PROPOSITION LXXIII. THEOREM XXXIII.

> *If toward the several points of a given sphere there tend equal centripetal forces decreasing in a square ratio <note>duplicate ratio</note> of the distances from the points; I say that a corpuscle placed within the sphere is attracted by a force proportional to its distance from the center.*

In the sphere *ABCD* (Plate 21, Figure 6) described about the center *S*, let the corpuscle *P* be placed; and about the same center *S*, with the distance *SP*, conceive an interior sphere *PEQF* to be described. It is plain (by Proposition 70) that the concentric spherical surfaces <note>superficies</note> of which the difference *AEBF* of the spheres is composed have no effect at all upon the body *P*; their attractions being destroyed by contrary attractions. There remains, therefore, only the attraction of the interior sphere *PEQF*. And (by Proposition 72) this attraction is proportional to the distance *PS*. *Q. E. D.*

### PROPOSITION LXXIII. THEOREM XXXIII.

> *If toward the various points of a given sphere there tend equal centripetal forces decreasing in a square ratio <note>the square</note> of the distances from those points; I say that a corpuscle placed within the sphere is attracted by a force proportional to its distance from the center.*

It is clear (by Proposition 70) that the concentric spherical surfaces <note>shells</note> which make up the difference *AEBF* between the spheres have no effect at all upon the body *P*, as their attractions are canceled by opposing attractions. There remains, therefore, only the attraction of the interior sphere *PEQF*. And (by Proposition 72) this attraction is proportional to the distance *PS*. *Q. E. D.*

### **PROPOSITION LXXIV. THEOREM XXXIV.**

*Assuming the same conditions, I say that a corpuscle situated outside the sphere is attracted with a force inversely proportional to the square of its distance from the center.*

But this attraction is just as great as the attraction of the same corpuscle would be, on the other hand, if it were itself attracted by the various particles of the attracted sphere with the same force with which they are attracted by it. But that attraction of the corpuscle would be (according to Proposition 74) inversely proportional to the square of its distance from the center of the sphere; therefore, the attraction of the sphere, being equal to it, is also in the same ratio. *Q.E.D.*

**COROLLARY 1.** The attractions of spheres toward other homogeneous spheres are proportional to the masses of the attracting spheres divided by the squares of the distances of their centers from the centers of those which they attract.

**COROLLARY 2.** The case is the same when the attracted sphere also attracts. For the various points of the one attract the various points of the other with the same force with which they themselves are attracted by the others in turn; and therefore, since in all attractions (according to Law 3) the attracted and attracting points are both acted upon equally, the force will be doubled by their mutual attractions, while the proportions remain the same.

**COROLLARY 3.** The various truths demonstrated above regarding the motion of bodies around the focus of conic sections will hold true when an attracting sphere is placed at the focus and the bodies move outside the sphere.

**COROLLARY 4.** Those things which were demonstrated before regarding the motion of bodies around the center of conic sections hold true when the motions are performed within the sphere.

## PROPOSITION LXXVI. THEOREM XXXVI.

*If spheres are in any way dissimilar (regarding the density of matter and the attractive force) in the progress directly from the center to the circumference; but everywhere similar, at every given distance from the center, on all sides around it; and the attractive force of every point decreases in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> of the distance of the body attracted; I say that the entire force with which one of these spheres attracts the other will be inversely proportional to the square of the distance between the centers.*

Then by Proposition 75, these spheres will attract other similar <term>concentric</term> spheres *GH*, *IK*, *LM*, etc., each other, with forces inversely proportional to the square of the distance *SP*. *COR.* 1. Hence, if many spheres of this kind, similar in all respects, attract each other mutually, the <term>accelerative attractions</term> of each toward the other, at any equal distances of the centers, will be proportional to the attracting spheres.

*COR.* 2. And at any unequal distances, they are proportional to the attracting spheres divided by the squares of the distances between the centers.

*COR.* 3. The <term>motive attractions</term>, or the weights of the spheres toward one another, will be at equal distances of the centers proportional to the attracting and attracted spheres combined; that is, as the products resulting from multiplying the masses of the spheres by each other.

*COR.* 4. And at unequal distances, they are directly proportional to those products and inversely proportional to the squares of the distances between the centers.

### PROPOSITION LXXVII. THEOREM XXXVII.

> *If to the several points of spheres there tend <term>centripetal forces</term> proportional to the distances of the points from the attracted bodies; I say that the combined force with which two spheres attract each other mutually is proportional to the distance between the centers of the spheres.*

**CASE 1.** Let *AEBF* (*Pl. 22. Fig. 2.*) be a sphere; *S* its center; *P* a <term>corpuscle</term> attracted; *PASB* the axis of the sphere passing through the center of the corpuscle; *EF*, *ef* two planes cutting the sphere, and perpendicular to the axis, and

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the gravitational attraction of spheres under the inverse square law, noting its application to orbital mechanics and conic sections. <meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition 77, Theorem 37, regarding the gravitational attraction of spheres when the force is proportional to the distance from the center, following the geometric setup established at the bottom of the previous page.</meta>

equidistant, one on one side and the other on the other, from the center of the sphere; *G* and *g* are the intersections of the planes and the axis; and *H* is any point in the plane *EF*. The <term>centripetal force</term> of the point *H* upon the <term>corpuscle</term> *P*, exerted in the direction of the line *PH*, is proportional to the distance *PH*; and (by corollary 2 of the laws) the same force exerted in the direction of the line *PG*, or toward the center *S*, is proportional to the length *PG*. Therefore, the force of all the points in the plane *EF* (that is, of that whole plane) by which the <term>corpuscle</term> *P* is attracted toward the center *S* is proportional to the distance *PG* multiplied by the number of those points—that is, proportional to the solid volume defined by that plane *EF* and the distance *PG*. And in like manner, the force of the plane *ef* by which the <term>corpuscle</term> *P* is attracted toward the center *S* is proportional to that plane multiplied by its distance *Pg*, or as the equal plane *EF* multiplied by that distance *Pg*; and the sum of the forces of both planes is proportional to the plane *EF* multiplied by the sum of the distances *PG + Pg*, which is proportional to that plane multiplied by twice the distance *PS* between the center and the <term>corpuscle</term>; that is, as twice the plane *EF* multiplied by the distance *PS*, or as the sum of the equal planes *EF + ef* multiplied by the same distance. And by similar reasoning, the forces of all the planes in the whole sphere, equidistant on each side from the center of the sphere, are proportional to the sum of those planes multiplied by the distance *PS*; that is, proportional to the whole sphere and the distance *PS* jointly. *Q. E. D.*

**CASE 2.** Now let the <term>corpuscle</term> *P* attract the sphere *AEBF*. And by the same reasoning, it will appear that the force with which the sphere is attracted is proportional to the distance *PS*. *Q. E. D.*

<summary>This page completes the proof that if centripetal force is proportional to distance, the total attraction between a sphere and a corpuscle (or vice versa) is proportional to the distance between their centers. <meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition 77, Theorem 37, regarding the gravitational attraction of spheres when the force is proportional to the distance from the center, following the geometric demonstration of how planes within a sphere contribute to the total force acting on an external or internal point.</meta>

CASE 2. Imagine another sphere composed of innumerable corpuscles *P*; and because the force with which every corpuscle is attracted is proportional to the distance of the corpuscle from the center of the first sphere and to that same sphere jointly, and is therefore the same as if it all proceeded from a single corpuscle situated in the center of the sphere; the entire force with which all the corpuscles in the second sphere are attracted—that is, with which that whole sphere is attracted—will be the same as if that sphere were attracted by a force issuing from a single corpuscle in the center of the first sphere; and it is therefore proportional to the distance between the centers of the spheres. *Q.E.D.* <note>Q.E.D. stands for *Quod Erat Demonstrandum*, meaning "which was to be demonstrated."</note>

CASE 4. Let the spheres attract each other mutually, and the force will be doubled, but the proportion will remain. *Q.E.D.*

CASE 5. Let the <term>corpuscle</term> *p* be placed within the sphere *AEBF* (*Fig. 3*); and because the force of the plane *ef* upon the corpuscle is proportional to the solid volume contained under that plane and the distance *pg*; and the contrary force of the plane *EF* is proportional to the solid volume contained under that plane and the distance *pG*; the force compounded of both will be proportional to the difference of the solid volumes. That is, it is proportional to the sum of the equal planes multiplied by half the difference of the distances—that is, as that sum multiplied by *pS*, the distance of the corpuscle from the center of the sphere. And by similar reasoning, the attraction of all the planes *EF* and *ef* throughout the whole sphere—that is, the attraction of the whole sphere—is jointly proportional to the sum of all the planes (or the whole sphere) and to *pS*, the distance of the corpuscle from the center of the sphere. *Q.E.D.*

# PROPOSITION LXXVIII. THEOREM XXXVIII.

*If spheres, in the progress from the center to the circumference, are in any way dissimilar and unequal, but are similar on every side in all directions at all given distances from the center; and the attractive force of every point is proportional to the distance of the attracted body; I say that the entire force with which two spheres of this kind attract each other mutually is proportional to the distance between the centers of the spheres.*

This is demonstrated from the preceding proposition in the same manner as the 76th proposition was demonstrated from the 75th.

## SCHOLIUM.

I have now explained the two principal cases of attractions: namely, when the centripetal forces decrease in a duplicate ratio <note>the square of the ratio; an inverse square law</note> of the distances, or increase in a simple ratio <note>directly proportional</note> of the distances. In both cases, these forces cause the bodies to revolve in conic sections and compose spherical bodies whose centripetal forces observe the same law of increase or decrease in the recession from the center as the forces of the particles themselves do; which is very remarkable. It would be tedious to run over the other cases, whose conclusions are less elegant and important, in such detail as I have done these. I choose rather to comprehend and determine them all by one general method as follows.

## LEMMA XXIX.

*If around the center S (Pl. 22. Fig. 4.) there is described any circle such as AEB, and around the center P there are also described two circles EF and ef, intersecting the first in E and e, and the line PS in F and f; and if perpendiculars ED and ed are dropped to PS; I say that if the distance of the arcs EF and ef is supposed to be infinitely diminished, the ultimate ratio of the evanescent*

*evanescent line Dd to the evanescent line Ff is the same as that of the line PE to the line PS.*

For if the line $Pe$ intersects the arc $EF$ at $q$; and the straight line $Ee$, which coincides with the evanescent arc $Ee$, is produced to meet the straight line $PS$ at $T$; and a perpendicular $SG$ is dropped from $S$ to $PE$; then because of the similar triangles $DTE$, $dTe$, and $DES$, it will be as $Dd$ is to $Ee$, so $DT$ is to $TE$, or $DE$ is to $ES$. And because the triangles $Eeq$ and $ESG$ (by Lem. 8 and Cor. 3, Lem. 7) are similar, it will be as $Ee$ is to $eq$ (or $Ff$), so $ES$ is to $SG$. Therefore, *ex æquo* <note>by equality of ratios</note>, as $Dd$ is to $Ff$, so $DE$ is to $SG$; that is (because of the similar triangles $PDE$ and $PGS$), as $PE$ is to $PS$. *Q. E. D.*

### PROPOSITION LXXIX. THEOREM XXXIX.

*Suppose a surface <term>superficies</term> such as EFfe (Plate 22, Figure 5) has its breadth infinitely diminished until it is just vanishing; and that the same surface, by its revolution around the axis PS, describes a spherical concavo-convex solid, to the several equal particles of which equal centripetal forces tend; I say that the force with which that solid attracts a corpuscle situated at P, is in a ratio compounded of the ratio of the solid DE$^2$ $\times$ Ff and the ratio of the force with*

<summary>Newton completes the proof of Lemma 29 using similar triangles and then introduces Proposition 79, which begins to calculate the attractive force exerted by a thin spherical shell on a point mass.</summary>

<keywords>Lemma XXIX, Proposition LXXIX, centripetal force, similar triangles, evanescent arc, spherical solid, corpuscle, geometry, Principia</keywords>

--- Page 365 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition 79, which calculates the total force exerted by a thin spherical shell on an external point by integrating the forces of individual rings.</meta>

*with which a given particle in the place* $Ff$ *would attract the same corpuscle.*

For if we first consider the force of the spherical surface <term>superficies</term> $FE$, which is generated by the revolution of the arc $FE$ and is cut anywhere, as at $r$, by the line $de$; the ring-shaped <term>annular</term> part of the surface generated by the revolution of the arc $rE$ will be as the small line <term>lineola</term> $Id$, provided the radius of the sphere $PE$ remains the same, as *Archimedes* has demonstrated in his book *On the Sphere and Cylinder*. And the force of this surface exerted in the direction of the lines $PE$ or $Pr$, situated all around the conical surface, will be as this annular surface itself; that is, as the small line $Dd$, or what is the same, as the rectangle formed by the given radius $PE$ of the sphere and the small line $Dd$. But that force, exerted in the direction of the line $PS$ tending toward the center $S$, will be less in the ratio of $PD$ to $PE$ <note>The OCR text reads "ID to IE," but based on the geometric logic of the Principia and the resulting expression $PD \times Dd$, this corresponds to the ratio of $PD$ to $PE$.</note>, and therefore will be as $PD \times Dd$. 

Suppose now the line $DF$ is divided into innumerable small, equal particles, each of which we call $Dd$; then the surface $FE$ will be divided into as many equal rings <term>annuli</term>, whose forces will be as the sum of all the rectangles $PD \times Dd$, that is, as $\frac{1}{2} PF^2 - \frac{1}{2} PD^2$, and therefore as $DE^2$. 

Now, let the surface $FE$ be multiplied by <note>"drawn into" here means multiplied by the thickness</note> the altitude $Ff$; then the force of the solid $FFfe$ exerted upon the corpuscle $P$ will be as $DE^2 \times Ff$, provided that the force is given which any given particle, such as $Ff$, exerts upon the corpuscle $P$ at the distance $PF$. But if that force is not given, the force of the solid $FFfe$ will be in a ratio compounded of the solid $DE^2 \times Ff$ and that unknown force, jointly. *Q. E. D.*

### PROPOSITION LXXX. THEOREM XL.

*If equal centripetal forces tend toward the various equal parts of a sphere **A B E**, (Pl. 22. Fig. 6.) described around the center **S**; and from the various points **D** in the axis of the sphere **AB** on which a corpuscle, such as **P**, is placed, there are erected perpendiculars **DE** meeting the sphere at **E**; and if on those perpendiculars the lengths **DN** are taken as the quantity*
->**DE**² × **PS** / **PE**<-
*and as the force which a particle of the sphere situated in the axis exerts at the distance **PE** upon the corpuscle **P**, jointly; I say that the whole force with which the corpuscle **P** is attracted toward the sphere is as the area **ANB**, contained under the axis of the sphere **AB** and the curve line **ANB**, which is the locus of the point **N**.*

For, assuming the construction in the last lemma and theorem to stand, conceive the axis of the sphere *AB* to be divided into innumerable equal particles *Dd*, and the whole sphere to be divided into as many spherical concavo-convex laminae *EFfe*;

and erect the perpendicular $dn$. By the last theorem, the force with which the lamina $EFfe$ attracts the corpuscle $P$ is proportional to $DE^2 \times Ff$ and the force of one particle exerted at the distance $PE$ or $PF$ jointly. But (by the last lemma) $Dd$ is to $Ff$ as $PE$ is to $PS$, and therefore $Ff$ is equal to:
$\frac{PS \times Dd}{PE}$;
and $DE^2 \times Ff$ is equal to $Dd \times \frac{DE^2 \times PS}{PE}$;
and therefore the force of the lamina $EFfe$ is as:
$Dd \times \frac{DE^2 \times PS}{PE}$
and the force of a particle exerted at the distance $PF$ jointly, that is, by the hypothesis, as $DN \times Dd$, or as the infinitesimal area $DNnd$. Therefore, the forces of all the laminae exerted upon the corpuscle $P$ are as all the areas $DNnd$; that is, the whole force of the sphere will be as the whole area $ANB$. *Q. E. D.*

**COR. 1.** Hence, if the centripetal force tending toward the several particles remains always the same at all distances, and $DN$ is made as:
$\frac{DE^2 \times PS}{PE}$;
the whole force with which the corpuscle is attracted by the sphere is as the area $ANB$.

**COR. 2.** If the centripetal force of the particles is inversely proportional to the distance of the corpuscle attracted by it, and $DN$ is made as:
$\frac{DE^2 \times PS}{PE^2}$;
the force with which the corpuscle $P$ is attracted by the whole sphere will be as the area $ANB$.

## PROPOSITION LXXXI. PROBLEM XLI.

*With the previous conditions remaining the same, it is required to measure the area ANB. (Plate 23. Figure 1.)*

From the point *P*, let the straight line *PH* be drawn touching the sphere at *H*; and dropping the perpendicular *HI* to the axis *PAB*, bisect *PI* at *L*; then (by Proposition 12, Book 2 of the *Elements* <note>Euclid's Elements of Geometry</note>) *PE*² is equal to *PS*² + *SE*² + 2 *PS* · *SD*. But because the triangles *SPH* and *SHI* are similar, *SE*² or *SH*² is equal to the rectangle *PS* · *SI*. Therefore, *PE*² is equal to the rectangle formed by *PS* and (*PS* + *SI* + 2 *SD*); that is, by *PS* and (2 *LS* + 2 *SD*); which is to say, by *PS* and 2 *LD*. Furthermore, *DE*² is equal to *SE*² - *SD*², or *SE*² - (*LS*² - 2 *SL* · *LD* + *LD*²), that is, 2 *SL* · *LD* - *LD*² - (*LS*² - *SE*²). For *LS*² - *SE*² or *LS*² - *SA*² (by Proposition 6, Book 2 of the *Elements*) is equal to the rectangle *AL* · *LB*. The quantity $\frac{DE^2 \times PS}{PE \times V}$ which (by Corollary 4 of the preceding Proposition) is proportional to the length of the ordinate $DN$, will now resolve itself into three parts: $\frac{2 SL \times LD \times PS}{PE \times V}$ — $\frac{LD^2 \times PS}{PE \times V}$ — $\frac{ALB \times PS}{PE \times V}$; where if instead of $V$ we write the inverse ratio of the centripetal force, and instead of $PE$ the mean proportional between $PS$ and $2 LD$, those three parts will become ordinates to several curved lines whose areas are found by common methods. **EXAMPLE 1.** If the centripetal force tending toward the several particles of the sphere is inversely proportional to the distance, instead of $V$ write $PE$, the distance; then $2 PS \times LD$ for $PE^2$; and $DN$ will become proportional to $SL - \frac{1}{2} LD - \frac{ALB}{2 LD}$. At $L, A, S, B$ (Fig. 3), erect the perpendiculars $Ll, Aa, Ss, Bb$, of which suppose $Ss$ is equal to $SI$. **Example 3.** If the centripetal force tending toward the individual particles of the sphere decreases in a quadruplicate ratio <note>the fourth power</note> of the distance from the particles, substitute $\frac{PE^4}{2 AS^3}$ for $V$, then $\sqrt{2 PS - LD}$ for $PE$, and $DN$ will become as $\frac{SI^2 \times SL}{\sqrt{2 SI}} \times \frac{1}{\sqrt{LD^3}} - \frac{SI^2}{2 \sqrt{2 SI}} \times \frac{1}{\sqrt{LD}} - \frac{SI^2 \times ALB}{2 \sqrt{2 SI}} \times \frac{1}{\sqrt{LD^5}}$. These three parts, multiplied by the length $AB$, produce as many areas, namely: $\frac{2 SI^2 \times SL}{\sqrt{2 SI}}$ multiplied by $\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{LA}} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{LB}}\right)$; $\frac{SI^2}{\sqrt{2 SI}}$ multiplied by $(\sqrt{LB} - \sqrt{LA})$; and $\frac{SI^2 \times ALB}{3 \sqrt{2 SI}}$ multiplied by $\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{LA^3}} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{LB^3}}\right)$. After proper simplification, these result in $\frac{2 SI^2 \times SL}{LI}$, $SI^2$, and $SI^2 + \frac{2 SI^3}{3 LI}$. By subtracting the last of these from the first, they become $\frac{4 SI^3}{3 LI}$. Therefore, the entire force with which the corpuscle <note>a small particle of matter</note> $P$ is attracted toward the center of the sphere is proportional to $\frac{SI^3}{PI^3}$; that is, it varies inversely as $PI^3$.

By the same method, one may determine the attraction of a <term>corpuscle</term> <note>a small particle of matter</note> situated within the sphere, but more expeditiously by the following theorem.

# PROPOSITION LXXXII. THEOREM XLI.

*In a sphere described around the center S (Pl. 23. Fig. 4.) with the interval SA, if SI, SA, and SP are taken to be <term>continually proportional</term> <note>forming a geometric progression where SI/SA = SA/SP</note>; I say that the attraction of a corpuscle within the sphere at any place I is to its attraction outside the sphere at place P in a ratio compounded of the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> <note>the ratio of the square roots</note> of IS and PS (the distances from the center) and the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the centripetal forces tending toward the center at those places P and I.*

For example, if the <term>centripetal forces</term> of the particles of the sphere are inversely as the distances of the corpuscle attracted by them, the force with which the corpuscle situated at I is attracted by the entire sphere will be to the force with which it is attracted at P in a ratio compounded of the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the distance SI to the distance SP, and the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> of the centripetal force at place I arising from any particle at the center, to the centripetal force at place P

arising from the same particle in the center; that is, in the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> <note>the ratio of the square roots</note> of the distances *SI* and *SP* to each other inversely. These two subduplicate ratios compose the ratio of equality, and therefore the attractions in *I* and *P* produced by the whole sphere are equal. By a similar calculation, if the forces of the particles of the sphere are inversely in a <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>the ratio of the squares; i.e., the inverse-square law</note> of the distance, it will be found that the attraction in *I* is to the attraction in *P* as the distance *SP* is to the semi-diameter *SA* of the sphere. If those forces are inversely in a <term>triplicate ratio</term> <note>the ratio of the cubes</note> of the distances, the attractions in *I* and *P* will be to each other as *SP*$^2$ is to *SA*$^2$; if in a quadruplicate ratio, as *SP*$^3$ is to *SA*$^3$. Therefore, since the attraction in *P* was found in this last case to be inversely as *PS*$^3 \times PI$, the attraction in *I* will be inversely as *SA*$^3 \times PI$; that is, because *SA*$^3$ is given, inversely as *PI*. And the progression continues the same way to infinity.

The demonstration of this theorem is as follows.

With the construction remaining as established above, and a <term>corpuscle</term> being in any place *P*, the <term>ordinate</term> *DN* was found to be as $\frac{DE^2 \times PS}{PE \times V}$. Therefore, if *IE* is drawn, that ordinate for any other place of the corpuscle, such as *I*, will become (*mutatis mutandis* <note>with the necessary changes having been made</note>) as $\frac{DE^2 \times IS}{IE \times V}$. Suppose the <term>centripetal forces</term> flowing from any point of the sphere, such as *E*, to be to each other at the distances *IE* and *PE*, as *PE*$^n$ is to *IE*$^n$ (where the number *n* denotes the index of the powers of *PE* and *IE*), and those ordinates will become as $\frac{DE^2 \times PS}{PE \times PE^n}$ and $\frac{DE^2 \times IS}{IE \times IE^n}$, whose ratio

...ratio to each other is as *PS* × *IE* × *IE*^n to *IS* × *PE* × *PE*^n. Because *SI*, *SE*, and *SP* are in <term>continued proportion</term> <note>a sequence of ratios where the second term of one is the first term of the next</note>, the triangles *SIE* and *SEI* are similar; and from this, *IE* is to *PE* as *IS* is to *SE* or *SA*. In place of the ratio of *IE* to *PE*, substitute the ratio of *IS* to *SA*; and the ratio of the ordinates becomes that of *PS* × *IE*^n to *SA* × *PE*^n. But the ratio of *PS* to *SA* is the <term>subduplicate</term> <note>the ratio of the square roots</note> of the ratio of the distances *PS* and *SI*; and the ratio of *IE*^n to *PE*^n (because *IE* is to *PE* as *IS* is to *SA*) is the <term>subduplicate</term> <note>the ratio of the square roots</note> of the ratio of the forces at the distances *PS* and *IS*. Therefore the ordinates, and consequently the areas which the ordinates describe, and the attractions proportional to them, are in a ratio compounded of those <term>subduplicate ratios</term>. *Q. E. D.*

## PROPOSITION LXXXIII. PROBLEM XLII.

### *To find the force with which a corpuscle placed in the center of a sphere is attracted towards any segment of that sphere whatsoever.*

Let **P** (*Pl. 23. Fig. 5.*) be a body in the center of that sphere, and *RBSD* a segment of it bounded by the plane *RDS* and the spherical surface *RBS*. Let *DB* be cut at *F* by a spherical surface *EFG* described from the center **P**, and let the segment be divided into the parts *BREFGS* and *FEDG*. Let us suppose that segment to be not a purely mathematical, but a physical surface, having some thickness, though completely negligible. Let that thickness be called **O**.

<sig>U</sig>

and (by what *Archimedes* has demonstrated) that surface area will be as $PF \times DF \times O$. Let us also suppose the attractive forces of the particles of the sphere to be inversely as that power of the distances of which $n$ is the exponent; and the force with which the surface $EFG$ attracts the body $P$ will be (by Proposition 79) as $\frac{2 DF \times O}{PF^{n-1}} - \frac{DF^2 \times O}{PF^n}$. Let the perpendicular $FN$ multiplied by $O$ be proportional to this quantity; and the curvilinear area $BDI$, which the ordinate $FN$ (moved continuously through the length $DB$) will describe, will be as the total force with which the entire segment $RBSD$ attracts the body $P$. *Q. E. I.*

### PROPOSITION LXXXIV. PROBLEM XLIII.

->*To find the force with which a corpuscle, placed outside the center of a sphere in the axis of any segment, is attracted by that segment.*<-

Around the center $P$ with the radius $PE$, let the spherical surface $EFK$ be described; and let it divide the segment into two parts $EBKFE$ and $EFKDE$. Find the force of the first of those parts by Proposition 81, and the force of the latter part by Proposition 83; the sum of the forces will be the force of the entire segment $EBKDE$. *Q. E. I.*

### SCHOLIUM.

The attractions of spherical bodies having now been explained, it comes next in order to treat the laws of attraction in other bodies consisting in a similar manner of attractive particles; but to treat them particularly is not necessary to my design. It will be sufficient to add some general propositions relating to the forces of such bodies, and the motions arising from them, because knowledge of these will be of some little use in philosophical inquiries.

---

## SECTION XIII.

### *Of the attractive forces of bodies which are not of a spherical figure.*

## PROPOSITION LXXXV. THEOREM XLII.

*If a body is attracted by another, and its attraction is vastly stronger when it is contiguous to the attracting body than when they are separated from one another by a very small interval; the forces of the particles of the attracting body decrease, as the attracted body moves away, in more than a duplicate ratio of the distance of the particles.*

For if the forces decrease in a duplicate ratio of the distances from the particles, the attraction toward a spherical body, being (by Proposition 74) reciprocally
<meta>catchword: reciprocally</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Section XIII, focusing on the attraction of non-spherical bodies. Proposition 85 states that if an attraction is significantly stronger upon contact than at a small distance, the forces of the individual particles must decrease at a rate greater than the square of the distance.</summary>
<keywords>attraction, non-spherical bodies, Proposition LXXXV, Theorem XLII, duplicate ratio, particle forces, contiguous bodies</keywords>

--- Page 380 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition 85, demonstrating that the relationship between the intensity of attraction at contact and the rate of force decrease applies to bodies of any shape, not just spheres. It then transitions to Proposition 86.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>293</page-num>
<header>SECT. XIII. of Natural Philosophy.</header>
<sig>U 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: vastly</meta>

reciprocally as the square of the distance of the attracted body from the center of the sphere, will not be noticeably increased by contact; and it will be increased even less if the attraction, as the attracted body moves away, decreases in an even smaller proportion. The proposition, therefore, is evident concerning attractive spheres. And the case is the same for concave spherical shells attracting external bodies. And it appears much more clearly in shells that attract bodies placed within them, because there the attractions spread through the cavities of those shells are (by Proposition 70) canceled by opposing attractions, and therefore have no effect even at the point of contact. Now, if from these spheres and spherical shells we remove any parts far from the point of contact and add new parts anywhere at will, we may change the shapes of the attractive bodies as we please; but the parts added or removed, being far from the point of contact, will cause no significant increase in the attraction arising from the contact of the two bodies. Therefore, the proposition holds true for bodies of all shapes.
Q. E. D.

vastly stronger at the point of contact than when the attracting and attracted bodies are separated from each other, even by the smallest interval.

For it appears by the solution of Problem 41, shown in the second and third examples, that the attraction is infinitely increased when the attracted corpuscle comes to touch an attracting sphere of this kind. The same will also appear (by comparing those examples and Theorem 41 together) regarding the attractions of bodies toward concavo-convex orbs, whether the attracted bodies are placed outside the orbs or in the cavities within them. And by adding to or taking from those spheres and orbs any attractive matter anywhere outside the place of contact, so that the attractive bodies may take on any assigned shape, the proposition will hold true for all bodies universally. *Q. <language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<header>Section XIII. *Of Natural Philosophy.*</header>

*the accelerative attractions of the corpuscles toward particles of the bodies are proportional to the whole bodies, and are similarly situated within them.*

For if the bodies are divided into particles proportional to the whole bodies and similarly situated within them, it will be that as the attraction toward any particle of one of the bodies is to the attraction toward the corresponding particle in the other body, so are the attractions toward the various particles of the first body to the attractions toward the various corresponding particles of the other body; and by composition, so is the attraction toward the first whole body to the attraction toward the second whole body. *Q. E. D.*

**COROLLARY 1.** Therefore, if as the distances of the attracted corpuscles increase, the attractive forces of the particles decrease in the ratio of any power of the distances, the accelerative attractions toward the whole bodies will be directly as the bodies and inversely as those powers of the distances. For example, if the forces of the particles decrease in a duplicate ratio of the distances from the attracted corpuscles, and the bodies are as $A^3$ and $B^3$, and therefore both the cubic sides of the bodies and the distance of the attracted corpuscles from the bodies are as $A$ and $B$; the accelerative attractions toward the bodies will be as $A^3/A^2$ and $B^3/B^2$, that is, as $A$ and $B$, the cubic sides of those bodies. If the forces of the particles decrease in a triplicate ratio of the distances from the attracted corpuscles; the accele-

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of how the total attraction of a body scales with its size and the distance of a corpuscle, then introduces a theorem regarding forces that are directly proportional to distance. It establishes that such forces always tend toward the body's center of gravity, behaving like a single globe centered at that point.</summary>

--- Page 384 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition 88, Theorem 45, which demonstrates that if individual particles attract with a force proportional to distance, the resultant force of the whole body is directed toward its center of gravity.</meta>

<page-num>297</page-num>
<header>SECT. XIII. *of Natural Philosophy.* 297</header>

among themselves, are proportional to the distances *AZ* and *BZ*; but if they are assumed to be unequal, they are proportional to those particles and their distances *AZ* and *BZ* jointly, or (so to speak) as those particles multiplied by their distances *AZ* and *BZ* respectively. And let those forces be expressed by the products of *A* × *AZ* and *B* × *BZ*. Join *AB*, and let it be divided at *G* so that *AG* is to *BG* as particle *B* is to particle *A*; thus, *G* will be the common center of gravity of particles *A* and *B*. The force *A* × *AZ* will (by Cor. 2 of the Laws) be resolved into the forces *A* × *GZ* and *A* × *AG*; and the force *B* × *BZ* into the forces *B* × *GZ* and *B* × *BG*. Now the forces *A* × *AG* and *B* × *BG* are equal, because *A* is proportional to *B* and *BG* is proportional to *AG*; therefore, since they have opposite directions, they cancel each other out. There remain then the forces *A* × *GZ* and *B* × *GZ*. These tend from *Z* toward the center *G* and compose the force $(A+B) \times GZ$; that is, the same force as if the attractive particles *A* and *B* were placed in their common center of gravity *G*, forming a small globe there.

By the same reasoning, if a third particle *C* is added, and its force is compounded with the force $(A+B) \times GZ$ tending toward the center *G*, the resulting force will tend toward the common center of gravity of that globe at *G* and particle *C*; that is, toward the common center of gravity of the three particles *A*, *B*, and *C*; and it will be the same as if that globe and particle *C* were placed in that common center, forming a larger globe there. And so we may continue to infinity. Therefore, the total force of all the

<summary>This page proves that if the attractive force of particles is proportional to distance, the collective force of a body acts as if all its mass were concentrated at its center of gravity. **COR.** Hence the motion of the attracted body **Z** will be the same as if the attracting body **RSTV** were spherical; and therefore if that attracting body is either at rest or moves uniformly in a straight line, the attracted body will move in an ellipse having its center at the center of gravity of the attracting body.

# PROPOSITION XCI. PROBLEM XLV.

*To find the attraction of a <term>corpuscle</term> situated on the axis of a round solid, toward whose various points there tend equal centripetal forces decreasing in any ratio of the distances whatsoever.*

Let the <term>corpuscle</term> *P* (*Pl. 24. Fig. 2.*), situated on the axis *AB* of the solid *DECG*, be attracted toward that solid. Let the solid be cut by any circle such as *RFS*, perpendicular to the axis; and on its radius *FS*, in any plane *PALKB* passing through the axis, let there be taken (by Proposition 90) the length *FK* proportional to the force with which the <term>corpuscle</term> *P* is attracted toward that circle. Let the locus of the point *K* be the curved line *LKI*, meeting the planes of the outermost circles *AL* and *BI* at *L* and *I*; and the attraction of the <term>corpuscle</term> *P* toward the solid will be proportional to the area *LABI*. *Q. E. I.* <note>Quod Erat Inveniendum: Which was to be found.</note>

**COR. 1.** Hence if the solid is a cylinder described by the parallelogram *ADEB* (*Pl. 24. Fig. 3.*) revolving about the axis *AB*, and the centripetal forces tending to the various points are inversely as the squares of the distances from the points; the attraction of the <term>corpuscle</term> *P* toward this cylinder will be proportional to *AB* — *PE* + *PD*. And by similar reasoning, if the spaces *DPF* and *EGCB* are divided into particles by the surfaces of innumerable similar spheroids concentric to the former and having one common axis, all these particles will equally attract the body *P* on both sides toward opposite parts. Therefore, the forces of the cone *DPF* and of the conic segment *EGCB* are equal and, by their opposition, cancel each other. And the case is the same for the forces of all the matter that lies outside the interior spheroid *PCBM*. Therefore, the body *P* is attracted by the interior spheroid *PCBM* alone; and therefore (by corollary 3, proposition 72), its attraction is to the force with which the body *A* is attracted by the whole spheroid *AGOD* as the distance *PS* is to the distance *AS. Q.E.D.*

## PROPOSITION XCII. PROBLEM XLVI.

*Given an attracting body, it is required to find the ratio of the decrease of the centripetal forces tending toward its various points.*

The given body must be formed into a sphere, a cylinder, or some regular figure whose law of attraction, corresponding to any ratio of decrease, may be found by propositions 80, 81, and 91. Then, through experiments, the force of the attractions must be found at several distances; the law of attraction toward the whole, made known by that means, will give the ratio of the decrease of the forces of the individual parts; which was to be found.

<summary>Newton proves that a body inside a spheroid is attracted only by the interior portion of the mass, with the outer layers exerting no net force. He then explains how to experimentally determine the law of attraction for the individual parts of a body by observing the attraction of a regularly shaped object.</summary>

### PROPOSITION XCIII. THEOREM XLVII.

*If a solid is plane on one side and infinitely extended on all other sides, and consists of equal particles that are equally attractive, whose forces decrease—as they recede from the solid—in the ratio of any power greater than the square of the distances; and if a corpuscle placed on either side of the plane is attracted by the force of the whole solid; I say that the attractive force of the whole solid, as it recedes from its plane surface, will decrease in the ratio of a power whose base is the distance of the corpuscle from the plane, and whose index is 3 less than the index of the power of the distances.*

CASE 1. Let *LGl* (Pl. 24. Fig. 6.) be the plane by which the solid is bounded. Let the solid lie on that side of the plane that is toward *I*, and let it be divided into countless planes *mHM*, *nIN*, *oKO*, etc., parallel to *GL*. And first, let the attracted body *C* be placed outside the solid. Let *CGHI* be drawn perpendicular to those countless planes, and let the attractive forces of the points of the solid decrease in the ratio of a power of the distances whose index is the number *n*, which is not less than 3. Therefore

<meta>catchword: (by</meta>

<summary>Newton establishes a theorem regarding the gravitational attraction of an infinitely large, flat solid, demonstrating that the total force decreases based on the corpuscle's distance according to a specific power law related to the individual particles' attraction.</summary>
<keywords>attraction, infinite solid, plane surface, corpuscle, power law, index, distance, particles</keywords>

<sig>X 2</sig>

(by Corollary 3, Proposition 90) the force with which any plane *mHM* attracts the point *C* is inversely as *CH*<sup>n-2</sup>. In the plane *mHM*, take the length *HM* inversely proportional to *CH*<sup>n-2</sup>, and that force will be as *HM*. In the same way, in the several planes *lGL*, *nIN*, *oKO*, etc., take the lengths *GL*, *IN*, *KO*, etc., inversely proportional to *CG*<sup>n-2</sup>, *CI*<sup>n-2</sup>, *CK*<sup>n-2</sup>, etc., and the forces of those planes will be as the lengths so taken; therefore the sum of the forces will be as the sum of the lengths, that is, the force of the whole solid is as the area *GLOK* extended infinitely towards *OK*. But that area (by the known methods of quadratures) is inversely as *CG*<sup>n-3</sup>, and therefore the force of the whole solid is inversely as *CG*<sup>n-3</sup>. *Q. E. D.*

**CASE 2.** Now let the corpuscle *C* (*Fig. 7*) be placed on that side of the plane *lGL* that is within the solid, and take the distance *CK* equal to the distance *CG*. Then the part of the solid *L G l o K O* bounded by the parallel planes *lGL* and *oKO* will attract the corpuscle, situated in the middle, in neither direction; the contrary actions of the opposite points cancel each other out because of their equality. Therefore, the corpuscle *C* is attracted only by the force of the solid situated beyond the plane *oKO*. But this force (by Case 1) is inversely as *CK*<sup>n-3</sup>, that is (because *CG* and *CK* are equal) inversely as *CG*<sup>n-3</sup>. *Q. E. D.*

**COR. 1.** Hence, if the solid *LGIN* is bounded on each side by two infinite parallel planes *LG* and *IN*, its attractive force is known by subtracting the attractive force of the more distant part *NIKO* (infinitely extended towards *KO*) from the attractive force of the whole infinite solid *LGKO*.

<summary>This page concludes the proof that the attractive force of an infinite plane solid on a corpuscle is inversely proportional to the distance raised to the power of the original index minus three. This rule applies whether the corpuscle is located outside or inside the solid.</summary>

<page-num>308</page-num>
<header>Mathematical Principles Book I.</header>

**COR. 2.** If the more distant part of this solid is disregarded, because its attraction compared with the attraction of the nearer part is insignificant; the attraction of that nearer part will, as the distance increases, decrease nearly in the ratio of the power $CG^{n-3}$.

**COR. 3.** And hence if any finite body, flat on one side, attracts a <term>corpuscle</term> situated opposite the middle of that plane, and the distance between the corpuscle and the plane compared with the dimensions of the attracting body is extremely small; and the attracting body consists of homogeneous particles, whose attractive forces decrease in the ratio of any power of the distances greater than the <term>quadruplicate</term>; the attractive force of the whole body will decrease very nearly in the ratio of a power whose base is that very small distance, and whose index is 3 less than the index of the former power. This assertion does not hold true, however, of a body consisting of particles whose attractive forces decrease in the ratio of the <term>triplicate</term> power of the distances; because in that case, the attraction of the remoter part of the infinite body in the second corollary is always infinitely greater than the attraction of the nearer part.

But the operations may be shortened by resolving the ordinates into converging series. For example, if a length B is applied as an ordinate to a base A at any given angle, and that length is proportional to any power of the base $A^{\frac{m}{n}}$; and if the force is sought with which a body—either attracted toward the base or driven from it in the direction of that ordinate—may be caused to move in the curved line which that ordinate always describes with its higher extremity; I suppose the base is increased by a very small part O, and I resolve the ordinate $\overline{A+O}|^{\frac{m}{n}}$ into an infinite series $A^{\frac{m}{n}} + \frac{m}{n} OA^{\frac{m-n}{n}} + \frac{mm - mn}{2nn} OOA^{\frac{m-2n}{n}}$ etc. And I suppose the force is proportional to the term of this series in which O has two dimensions, that is, to the term $\frac{mm - mn}{2nn} OOA^{\frac{m-2n}{n}}$. Therefore, the sought force is proportional to $\frac{mm - mn}{nn} A^{\frac{m-2n}{n}}$, or what is the same thing, to $\frac{mm - mn}{nn} B^{\frac{m-2n}{m}}$. ## S E C T I O N XIV.

->*Concerning the motion of very small bodies when moved by centripetal forces tending toward the various parts of any very large body.*<-

***

**PROPOSITION XCIV. THEOREM XLVIII.**

*If two similar media are separated from each other by a space bounded on both sides by parallel planes, and a body in its passage through that space is attracted or impelled perpendicularly toward either of those media, and is not moved or hindered by any other force; and if the attraction is everywhere the same at equal distances from either plane, measured toward the same side of the plane; I say that the sine of incidence at either plane will be to the sine of emergence from the other plane in a constant ratio.*

**CASE 1.** Let *Aa* and *Bb* (*Plate 25. Figure 1.*) be two parallel planes, and let the body fall upon

Let there be erected *IM* perpendicular to *Bb*, the plane of emergence, meeting the line of incidence *GH* prolonged at *M*, and the plane of incidence *Aa* at *R*; and let the line of emergence *KI* be extended and meet *HM* at *L*. About the center *L*, with the interval *LI*, let a circle be described cutting both *HM* at *P* and *Q*, and *MI* extended at *N*; and first, if the attraction or impulse is supposed to be uniform, the curve *HI* (by what *Galileo* has demonstrated) will be a parabola, whose property is that the rectangle formed by its given <term>latus rectum</term> and the line *IM* is equal to the square of *HM*; and moreover, the line *HM* will be bisected at *L*. Therefore, if the perpendicular *LO* is dropped to *MI*, then *MO* and *OR* will be equal; and by adding the equal lines *ON* and *OI*, the totals *MN* and *IR* will also be equal. Therefore, since *IR* is given, *MN* is also given, and the ratio of the rectangle *NMI* to the rectangle formed by the <term>latus rectum</term> and *IM* (that is, to *HM*²) is constant. But the rectangle *NMI* is equal to the rectangle *PMQ*, that is, to the difference of the squares *ML*² and *PL*² (or *LI*²); and *HM*² has a constant ratio to its fourth part *ML*²; therefore, the ratio of *ML*² — *LI*² to *ML*² is constant, and by conversion, the ratio of *LI*² to *ML*², and its subduplicate, the ratio of *LI* to *ML*, are also constant. But in every triangle, such as *LMI*, the sines of the angles are proportional to the opposite sides. Therefore, the ratio of the sine of the angle of incidence *LMR*

to the sine of the angle of emergence *LIR* is constant.

**Case 2.** Now let the body pass successively through several spaces bounded by parallel planes, *AabB*, *BbcC*, etc. (*Pl. 25. Fig. 2.*), and let it be acted upon by a force which is uniform in each of them separately, but different in the different spaces; and by what was just demonstrated, the sine of the angle of incidence on the first plane *Aa* is to the sine of emergence from the second plane *Bb* in a constant ratio; and this sine of incidence upon the second plane *Bb* will be to the sine of emergence from the third plane *Cc* in a constant ratio; and this sine to the sine of emergence from the fourth plane *Dd* in a constant ratio; and so on to infinity; and by equality, the sine of incidence on the first plane is to the sine of emergence from the last plane in a constant ratio. Now let the intervals of the planes be diminished, and their number be infinitely increased, so that the action of attraction or impulse, exerted according to any assigned law, may become continuous; and the ratio of the sine of incidence on the first plane to the sine of emergence from the last plane being all along constant, will be constant then also. *Q. E. D.*

### PROPOSITION XCV. THEOREM XLIX.

*The same things being supposed, I say that the velocity of the body before its incidence is to its velocity after emergence as the sine of emergence to the sine of incidence.*

Make *AH* and *Id* equal (*Pl. 25. Fig. 3.*) and erect the perpendiculars *AG* and *dK* meeting the lines

<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition XCV, demonstrating the relationship between velocity and the sines of the angles of incidence and emergence. It then introduces Proposition XCVI, which addresses the conditions under which a body is reflected rather than refracted.</meta>

of incidence and emergence *GH*, *IK* in *G* and *K*. In *GH*, take *TH* equal to *IK*, and to the plane *Aa* let fall a perpendicular *Tv*. And (by Corollary 2 of the Laws of Motion) let the motion of the body be resolved into two: one perpendicular to the planes *Aa*, *Bb*, *Cc*, etc., and another parallel to them. The force of attraction or impulse, acting in directions perpendicular to those planes, does not at all alter the motion in parallel directions; and therefore the body proceeding with this motion will in equal times go through those equal parallel intervals that lie between the line *AG* and the point *H*, and between the point *I* and the line *dK*; that is, they will describe the lines *GH*, *IK* in equal times. Therefore the velocity before incidence is to the velocity after emergence as *GH* to *IK* or *TH*, that is as *AH* or *Id* to *vH*, that is (supposing *TH* or *IK* to be the radius) as the sine of emergence to the sine of incidence. *Q. E. D.*

### PROPOSITION XCVI. THEOREM L.

*The same things being supposed, and that the motion before incidence is swifter than afterwards; I say that if the line of incidence be inclined continually, the body will be at last reflected, and the angle of reflection will be equal to the angle of incidence.*

For conceive of the body passing between the parallel planes *Aa*, *Bb*, *Cc*, etc. (*Plate 25, Figure 4*) as describing parabolic arcs as above; and let those arcs be *HP*, *PQ*, *QR*, etc. And let the obliquity

<summary>This page concludes the proof that the ratio of velocities in refraction is inversely proportional to the ratio of the sines of the angles. It also introduces the theorem that a body moving into a medium where it slows down will eventually be reflected if its angle of incidence is sufficiently increased.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, velocity, sine of incidence, sine of emergence, reflection, parabolic arcs, laws of motion, perpendicular motion, parallel motion</keywords>

--- Page 406 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Theorem L (Proposition XCVI), demonstrating the conditions under which a moving body is reflected by a series of parallel planes. It explains that when the angle of incidence is sufficiently inclined, the body follows a parabolic path and returns to its original medium.</meta>

<page-num>315</page-num>
<header>SECT. XIV. Of Natural Philosophy.</header>

of the line of incidence *GH* to the first plane *Aa* be such that the sine of incidence may be to the radius of the circle whose sine it is, in the same ratio which the same sine of incidence has to the sine of emergence from the plane *Dd* into the space *DdeE*; and because the sine of emergence has now become equal to the radius, the angle of emergence will be a right angle, and therefore the line of emergence will coincide with the plane *Dd*. Let the body come to this plane at the point *R*; and because the line of emergence coincides with that plane, it is manifest that the body can proceed no further towards the plane *Ee*. But neither can it proceed in the line of emergence *Rd*, because it is perpetually attracted or impelled towards the medium of incidence. It will return, therefore, between the planes *Cc* and *Dd*, describing an arc of a parabola *QRq*, whose principal vertex (according to what *Galileo* has demonstrated) is at *R*, cutting the plane *Cc* at the same angle at *q* as it did before at *Q*; then going on in the parabolic arcs *qp*, *ph*, etc., similar and equal to the former arcs *QP*, *PH*, etc., it will cut the rest of the planes at the same angles at *p*, *h*, etc., as it did before at *P*, *H*, etc., and will emerge at last with the same obliquity at *h* with which it first impinged on that plane at *H*. Now conceive the intervals of the planes *Aa*, *Bb*, *Cc*, *Dd*, *Ee*, etc., to be infinitely diminished and their number infinitely increased, so that the action of attraction or impulse, exerted according to any assigned law, may become continuous; and the angle of emergence, remaining throughout equal to the angle of incidence, will also be equal to the same at the last. *Q. E. D.*

### SCHOLIUM.

These attractions bear a great resemblance to the reflections and refractions of light, which occur in a given ratio of the secants, as was discovered by *Snellius*; and consequently in a given ratio of the sines, as was shown by *Descartes*. For it is now certain from the phenomena of *Jupiter*'s satellites, confirmed by the observations of various astronomers, that light is propagated in succession and requires about seven or eight minutes to travel from the Sun to the Earth. Moreover, the rays of light in our air (as was recently discovered by *Grimaldi*, by admitting light into a dark room through a small hole, which I have also tried) are bent or inflected around bodies in their passage near the edges of those bodies, whether transparent or opaque (such as the circular and rectangular edges of gold, silver, and brass coins, or of knives or broken pieces of stone or glass), as if they were attracted to them. Those rays that pass closest to the bodies are the most inflected, as if they were most strongly attracted; a phenomenon which I myself have also carefully observed. Those which pass at greater distances are less inflected; and those at still greater distances are inflected slightly in the opposite direction and form three fringes of colors. In *Plate 25, Figure 6*, *s* represents the edge of a knife or any kind of wedge *AsB*; and *gowog*, *fnunf*, *ememe*, and *dlsld* are rays inflected toward the knife in the arcs *owo*, *nun*, *mtm*, and *lsl*. This inflection is greater or less according to their distance from the knife. Now, since this inflection of the rays occurs in the air away from the knife, it follows that the rays
