<summary>Newton compares the mechanical attraction of bodies to the reflection and refraction of light, noting that light has a finite speed and exhibits diffraction ("inflection") when passing near the edges of solid objects.</summary>
<keywords>refraction, reflection, Snellius, Descartes, speed of light, Jupiter's satellites, Grimaldi, diffraction, inflection, optics</keywords>

--- Page 408 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the inflection of light rays as they pass near the edges of bodies or through different media, concluding that this motion is analogous to the motion of physical bodies under the influence of attractive forces.</meta>

<page-num>317</page-num>
<header>SECT. XIV. of Natural Philosophy.</header>

that fall upon the knife are first inflected in the air before they touch the knife. And the same is true of the rays falling upon glass. Refraction, therefore, is made not at the point of incidence, but gradually, by a continual inflection of the rays; this is done partly in the air before they touch the glass, and partly (if I am not mistaken) within the glass, after they have entered it; as is represented (*Pl. 25. Fig. 7.*) by the rays *ckzc*, *biyb*, and *ahxa* falling upon *r*, *q*, and *p*, and inflected between *k* and *z*, *i* and *y*, and *h* and *x*. Therefore, because of the analogy between the propagation of light rays and the motion of bodies, I thought it appropriate to add the following propositions for optical uses. I am not at all considering the nature of the rays of light, or inquiring whether they are bodies or not, but am only determining the trajectories of bodies which are extremely similar to the trajectories of the rays.

## PROPOSITION XCVII. PROBLEM XLVII.

> *Supposing the sine of incidence upon any surface to be in a given ratio to the sine of emergence; and that the inflection of the paths of those bodies near that surface is performed in a very short space which may be considered as a point; it is required to determine such a surface as may cause all the corpuscles issuing from any one given place to converge to another given place.*

Let **A** (*Pl. 25. Fig. 8.*) be the place from which the corpuscles diverge; **B** the place to

...to which they should converge; *CDE* is the curved line which, by its revolution around the axis *AB*, describes the surface sought; *D* and *E* are any two points of that curve; and *EF* and *EG* are perpendiculars dropped onto the paths of the bodies *AD* and *DB*. Let the point *D* approach and merge with the point *E*; the ultimate ratio of the line *DF* (by which *AD* is increased) to the line *DG* (by which *DB* is diminished) will be the same as the ratio of the sine of incidence to the sine of emergence. Therefore, the ratio of the increment of the line *AD* to the decrement of the line *DB* is given. Consequently, if point *C* (through which the curve *CDE* must pass) is taken anywhere on the axis *AB*, and *CM* (the increment of *AC*) is taken in that given ratio to *CN* (the decrement of *BC*), and if two circles are described from the centers *A* and *B* with radii *AM* and *BN* respectively, intersecting each other at *D*, then that point *D* will touch the sought curve *CDE*. By touching it anywhere at will, it will determine that curve. *Q. E. I.*

COROLLARY 1. By causing point *A* or *B* to move sometimes to infinity, and sometimes toward other sides of point *C*, all those figures that Cartesius has presented in his *Optics* and *Geometry* regarding refractions will be obtained. The method for their discovery, which Cartesius saw fit to conceal, is revealed here in this proposition.

### Proposition XCVIII. Problem XLVIII.

*The same things being supposed; if around the axis AB (Pl. 25. Fig. 10.) any attractive surface CD is described, whether regular or irregular, through which the bodies issuing from the given place A must pass; it is required to find a second attractive surface EF, which may make those bodies converge to a given place B.*

Let a line joining AB cut the first surface at C and the second at E, the point D being chosen arbitrarily. And supposing the sine of incidence on the first surface to the sine of emergence from the same, and the sine of emergence from the second surface to the sine of incidence on the same, to be as any given quantity M to another given quantity N; then extend AB to G, so that BG is to CE as M — N is to N; and extend AD to H, so that AH is equal to AG; and extend DF to K so that DK is to DH as N is to M. Join KB, and around the center D with the radius DH describe a circle meeting the extension of KB at L, and draw BF parallel to DL; and the point F will touch the line EF, which being rotated around the axis AB will describe the surface sought. Q. E. F.

For imagine the lines CP and CQ to be everywhere perpendicular to AD and DF, and the lines ER and ES

But (because **BG** is to **CE**, as **M**—**N** is to **N**) it also happens that **CE**+**BG** is to **CE** as **M** to **N**; and therefore, by division, **FR** is to **FS** as **M** to **N**; and therefore (by Corollary 2, Proposition 97) the surface **EF** forces a body, falling upon it in the direction **DF**, to continue in the line **FR** to the place **B**. **Q. E. D.**

### S C H O L I U M.

In the same manner, one may proceed to three or more surfaces. But of all shapes, the spherical is the most suitable for optical uses. If the object glasses of telescopes were made of two glasses of a spherical shape, containing water between them, it is not unlikely that the errors of the refractions made in the extreme parts of the surfaces of the glasses may be accurately enough corrected by the refractions of the water. Such object-glasses are to be preferred over elliptic and hyperbolic glasses, not only because they may be formed with more ease and accuracy, but because the pencils of rays situated outside the axis of the glass would be more accurately refracted by them. But the different refrangibility of different rays is the real obstacle that hinders optics from being made perfect by spherical or any other shapes. Unless the errors arising from that can be corrected, all the labor spent in correcting the others is quite thrown away.

->### *The End of the First Volume.*<-

<summary>This page completes the proof for constructing a second refractive surface to converge bodies to a focal point and discusses the practical advantages and limitations of spherical telescope lenses. Newton notes that while spherical lenses are easier to manufacture, the "different refrangibility" of light rays remains the primary challenge in perfecting optical instruments.</summary>

<keywords>refraction, spherical surfaces, telescope lenses, optics, refrangibility, Proposition 98, Scholium, Isaac Newton</keywords>
